The Department in its instruction no. 1849, Novem
ber 25, 1939, expressed interest in receiving a compre
hensive report on various conditions and phenomena ap-
0
parent in the present economic situation in Japan. My
3
despatches no. 4424, December 14, 1939, and 4362, Decem-
ber 22, 1939, were intended to be partly responsive to the
A
Department's instruction. With this despatch I have
the

=== Page 2 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
honor to continue the series which I propose to send
to the Department on subjects within this important and
comprehensive field.
I have from time to time reported the progressive
development of a movement in Japan to rationalize the
nation's economy. This movement did not begin with the
outbreak of the present hostilities in China but rather
has its roots deep in the economic mobilization planning
of the Japanese military -- the navy as well as the army --
which has been in constant evolution from the time when
studies ofthe lessons taught by the World War clearly
indicated the important relationship of the nation's
economy to any military effort whether offensive or de-
fensive. The failure of the Japanese Army to realize its
objective in China by means of a quick smashing blow in
1937, made necessary a rapid acceleration in the implementa-
tion of a controlled economy for Japan. These measures
were explained to the people and to the Diet as necessary
in the first instance to the successful prosecution of
hostilities in China and latterly as necessary to the
establishment of a "new order in East Asia". The very
rapidity with which these measures were instituted pre-
vented careful integration. Strenuous efforts were made
for the expansion of all industrial enterprises with the
emphasis being placed upon heavy industry, machine tools,
iron and steel, munitions, etc. This expansion of industry
was not accompanied or preceded by the necessary expansion
in such complementary fields as coal mining and generation
of electric power.
A

=== Page 3 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
A careful study of the power shortage problem in
Japan indicates a chain of circumstances which resolve
in a vicious circle and seem to be unavoidable. These
ciroumstances will be discussed under three general classi-
fications.
The present shortage of electric power, while finding
its immediate cause in the severe drought which prevailed
throughout must of Japan last summer, fall and which in
some areas, continued through January of this year, is
explainable on the basis of three fundamental factors. In
the first place, there have been for the past several years
definite indications that the electric power industry would
become a state-controlled endeavor. Practical realization
of this objective was brought about last April when the
Japan Electric Generation and Transmission Company, a
semi-official concern, was established. As a result of
apprehension of this development, private electrical com-
panies were loathe to undertake expansion in productive
facilities in anticipation of future demand for electric
power. It is necessary for an expansion in industries supply-
ing producer's goods to forerun expansion in industries pro-
ducing consumer's goods: it is also necessary for expan-
sion of power facilities to precede expansion of industries
utilizing that power for production. Normal demand for
electric power tends to increase each year. With the un-
settled state of affairs of the past few years in the electric
power industry in Japan, and in anticipation of government
intervention and participation in that industry, power facili-
ties

=== Page 4 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
ties were not expanded proportionately to the increase
in demand normally to be expected. It is to be borne in
mind that expansion of power production facilities must
be undertaken far in advance of any increase in effective
demand.
Secondly, the Japanese Government has undertaken an ex-
tensive program of industrial expansion particularly in in-
dustries demanding large quantities of power. This has
artificially increased not only the actual demand for
power, but has also accelerated the annual rate of increase
in demand for power. This program has preempted available
materials for the munitions and other favored industries
under a war-basis economy.
These two basic factors were in themselves sufficient
to present Japan with a power problem of serious dimensions.
There is to be added to these conditions a shortage of coal
and of waterfall. The drought was not inherently an in-
surmountable problem and thermal plant facilities were, if
Japanese sources of information are not entirely unre-
liable, sufficient to meet the immediate situation even
in face of the drastic curtailment of hydro-electric
generation of power. This is said to be true in spite of
the fact that at the end of April, 1939, the Electric Asso-
ciation estimated hydroelectric power as 55 per cent of
the total power produced. There is, however, a serious
shortage of coal, particularly good quality coal.
The basic power problem is inextricably bound up
with Japan's industrial program and will not be per-
manently

=== Page 5 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
manently settled even with an abundance of coal. It will
require the most careful integration of economic planning.
The immediate problem of electric power shortage is, however,
a direct result of the shortage of coal.
I -- COAL SHORTAGE
The Japan Electric Generation and Transmission Company
(the semi-official company controlling the electric genera-
tion industry) had estimated early in the year its needs,
on the basis of the previous ten-year average of waterflow
in the various rivers harnèssed for power, at 5,300,000
tons of coal for 1939. The Company expected to carry over
approximately one million tons into the dry winter period,
1939-40. The summer drought completely disrupted these
plans and consumption of coal from April-August doubled,
trebled and increased even more greatly in many important
plants. The following are examples:
Consumption of Coal
April-August
1938
1939
*Tsurumi Power Plant
2,100 tons
59,500 tons
Saka Power Plant
45,800 tons
84,700 tons
Amagasaki Power Plant
255,500 tons
346,600 tons
As a result of this increased consumption, the amount
of coal held by the Japan Electric Generation and Trans-
mission Company in August was not one million tons but
actually less than a third of that amount. To this was
added the alarming fact that coal deliveries were slow
and far below the contracted amount - further the quality
of the coal was very inferior.
While
* Figures from the ASAHI SHIMBUN, January 31, 1940.

=== Page 6 === (confidence 0.99)
-6
While the vernacular press tended to place the blame
for the failure of electric supply on the Japan Electric
Generation and Transmission Company because of its lack of
foresight in arranging for an adequate supply of coal of
good quality, examination and study of the situation indi-
cates that this company was not solely responsible for the
difficulty. The semi-official concern was late in entering
the field to contract for its coal and as a result was forced
to rely on what are called "outside producers" rather than on
the Showa Coal Company, a combination of the largest pro-
ducers in Japan. Owing to the increased demand for coal and
the government restriction and regulation of the coal in-
dustry, small companies as early as last summer began to con-
duct what were called "secret" transactions selling coal
above the official price and of inferior quality. The semi-
official electric generating company was, because of its
position, forced to accept coal only at the standard price.
There is some indication that the Japan Electric Generation
and Transmission Company, because of its peculiar status,
expected theGovernment authorities to ensure adequate coal
supplies for electric production. The fact that responsi-
bility was divided between the Electric Bureau of the Ministry
of Communications and the Japan Electric Generation and
Transmissin Company has been criticized as being inefficient
and "bureauoratic".
On October 1, 1939, the Ministry of Commerce and In-
dustry issued regulations for the control of coal sales.
It then became necessary for the electrical industry to
submit new estimates for the amount of coal needed for the
remaining months of 1939. The Japan Electric Generation
and

=== Page 7 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
and Transmission Company requested 4,600,000 tons as suf-
ficient for immediate needs and to provide a carry-over
of 500,000 tons for 1940. An application for this amount
was accordingly filed with the Ministry of Commerce and
Industry. Plans for increasing the production of coal were
not materializing successfully and therefore the Ministry
decided to restrict all applications for permits to purchase
coal. The electric company was granted permission to buy
3,200,000 tons. This amount, in view of the continued drought
was insufficient, but if obtained would have prevented the
present severe shortage of electric power. However, coal
companies were prevented from selling coal without a "sale
authorization" from the Ministry of Commerce and Industry.
In other words to obtain coal for electric-power production
it was necessary not only to receive a permit to purchase
but also to receive a sale permit from the Ministry of Com-
merce and Industry. Sale permits for the full amount were not
forthcoming and authorization for the sale of only 2,170,000
tons was granted early in the fall. During the first ten
days of December authorization for the sale of an addi-
tional 500,000 tons and on December 27 authorization for
the sale of 350,000 tons were granted. To further com-
plicate the supply of coal to the electrical industry other
important industries were also suffering a shortage of coal
and with the advent of the stormy winter season it became
impossible to make deliveries of coal already mined. The
situation became particularly acute in the Kwansai area.
The vernacular press seems - and rightly - to place the
blame largely upon the fact that responsible authorities
expeoted

=== Page 8 === (confidence 0.99)
-8-
expected political negotiations between the ministries
concerned to result in timely issuance of permits for
the:supply of coal and naively hoped to be able to obtain
supplies immediately after issuance of the permits. Those
responsible, even if they did anticipate the emergenoy, did
not undertake measures last summer and autumn to avoid the
difficulty.
To prevent a similar crisis next year the Japan
Electric Generation and Transmission Company has already
presented its coal requirements to the Ministry of Com-
merce and Industry for the fiscal year 1940-41 beginning
March first. It estimates a requirement of a little more
than 6,700,000 metric tons. Realizing its mistake in the
past year theCompany proposes to store approximately
1,000,o00 tons of this amount and actual consumption is
estimated at about 5,700,000 metric tons. As indicated in
this despatch, however, the question is not merely one
of estimates and Government permits based upon those es-
timates. It will be necessary to assure adequate pro-
duction of good quality coal as well as adequate trans-
portation during favorable seasons. There is every evidence
of a shortage of barges and other vessels suitable for trans-
porting coal to the consumption areas. In addition, many
such craft are being used for the transportation of coal
of inferior quality which is not subjected to government
price control. The present shortage of coal in consumption
areas is in large measure due to the failure or inadequacy
of present transportation facilities. It is reported that
there

=== Page 9 === (confidence 0.99)
-9~
are at present some 200,000 tons of coal ready for
shipment at the port of Wakamatsu in Kyushu, but owing
to weather conditions it is impossible to ship this coal
at the present time.
The low-price policy has also had considerable bear-
ing on the present coal shortage. The Showa Coal Mining
Company and other large-scale operators were forced by
the Government to adhere to the official prices whereas
medium and small coal mine operators known as "outsiders"
escaped government restrictions. The Showa group were
forced to maintain low wages and low prices for their coal
whereas outsiders were at liberty to arrange their own
wages and coal prices. This brought about a reduction
in the production of the larger and better mines and an
increase in the production of small mines delivering in-
ferior coal. It is reported that in spite of the ordinance
designed to prevent competitive bidding among mine-owners
for employees as many as 1800 miners changed their place
of employment in one month last year. The price of coal
sold by the outsiders was in many instances double the
official price. The press has been particularly bitter in
its criticism of coal dealers but no less bitter in its criti-
cism of the Government's control system which has proved to
be inadequate.
II - EFFECT ON GENERAL INDUSTRY.
It was reported in the ASAHI SHIMBUN on January 31
that industry in general was seriously suffering from the
shortage of power. Spinning mills, for example, in the
Kwansai and Chubu districts were said to be subjected to
an

=== Page 10 === (confidence 0.99)
-10-
an approximately 50 per cent reduction in available
power. It was believed that a 30 per cent drop in production
would be inevitable. After February lst, spinning mills,
it was reported, would be forced to cease work every other
day. In spite of the vital importance of the chemical
industry for the production of fertilizers and other neces-
ssary products, power to that industry as well has been
restricted. This may have serious repercussions on agri-
cultural production next year. Even iron manufacturing
has not escaped and it was reported that a 30 to 40 per cent
restriction resulting in almost the complete suspension of
operations of electric open hearth furnaces, has been put
into force. The ASAHI further reported that the Nisso
Plant in Hokuriku which specializes in the smelting of
aluminum ores was being forced gradually to suspend opera-
tion of its blast furnaces.
These restrictions of course apply in varying degrees
of severity to all industries.
III - GOVERNMENTAL CONTROL MEASURES
The Government was finally driven to the point of
issuing on the basis of the electric power adjustment
ordinance, ministerial notifications restricting the
consumption of electric power. These restrictions, it
is reported,will involve a 30 per cent average decrease
in consumption in the Kwanto Distriot and a 35 per cent
average decrease in the Kwansai district during the period
from February 10 to February 20th. Copies of the Embassy's
translations

=== Page 11 === (confidence 0.99)
-11-
translations of the pertinent Ministerial Notifications
are enclosed.
The Communications Minister replying to
interpellations in the Diet has promised an alleviation
of the restrictions following February 20 which will en-
tail only a 20 per cent reduction for both areas. Coming
at this late date the Government restrictions fail to solve
the critical problem and enforced "holidays" are not only
causing great losses to industrialists but are also working
hardships on the laborers themselves who are forced at
best to accept compensating allowances provided by the
various companies. There has been considerable demand
for Government assistance to the laborers and the in-
dustrialists.
Domestic consumption of electricity has not been
seriously curtailed. Installation of new equipment for
domestic electrical appliances such as refrigerators,
water heaters, electric pumps, etc., has been prohibited.
Various organizations, including the police, have been
conducting campaigns to decrease domestic consumption
through the use of smaller bulbs, careful use of light and
other voluntary measures. Garden lights have been pre-
hibited since January 1, 1940.
It was announced on February 15, 1940, that the Min-
istry of Commeroe and Industry has approved a plan for an
increase of 6,000,000 tons in the production of coal during
the coming fiscal year. A subsidy of 5.00 per ton as well
as funds for new shafts and galleries.are to be granted. A
new semi-official joint sales company is to be established
to ensure maintenance of official prices, to prevent
"seoret"

=== Page 12 === (confidence 0.99)
-12-
"secret" and illegal transactions, to facilitate dis-
tribution and to discourage production of inferior
coal. It is estimated that the cost of this program
will be 1l0,O00,O00. A clipping from the JAPAN
51
ADVERTISER on this subject is enclosed.
IV -- CONCLUSION
The problem of shortages not only of electric
power and coal but of all other commodities will con-
tinue to plague the Japanese Government until such time
as there can be put into effect an integrated and planned
economy which will be workable and not merely apparently
expedient for military purposes. Experience has shown
the Japanese public that restriction of materials to one
industry and allocation to another tends eventually to
disrupt the nation's economy, and to prevent full realiza-
tion of the primary objective of the control measures.
Present failure in the coal and electric industries are
cogent examples to the Japanese public which now show signs
of beginning to realize the impossibility of industrial ex-
pansion not based upon a broad fundamental expansion of the
entire economy of Japan. Industry can not lift itself by
its own bootstraps and the best plans require skillful
execution not yet exhibited by theGovernment control
agencies. Japan can not solve its power shortage nor
other glaring shortages and difficulties until a sound
balance in national economy is reached and a well-integrated
plan developed and made effective.
The

=== Page 13 === (confidence 0.98)
-13-
Theconcluding paragraph of a recent series of articles
in the ASAHI SHIMBUN presents an interesting comment:
"If efforts are made to carry out as
planned an expansion in the produotion of coal,
interference with the plans for the expansion
of the productive capacity of other materials
will result; if we undertake shipment. of coal,
the shipment of other necessary materials falls
into disorder. These facts bring full realization
of the existence of relationships, which can not
easily be reconciled, between the scope of Japan's
wartime economy and the nation's economic capacity
to execute that wartime economy. In order to
rationalize the inconsistency between economic
plans and actual capacity, and to place develop-
ment of the nation's economic strength once more
on a normal and favorable course, it is absolute-
ly necessary to reexamine the program for the
expansion of productive capacity and to reconstitute
that program systematically and in all its elements.
The real solution of the electric power and coal
shortages will then, and only then, be realized."
Present emphasis on a war-basis economy does not,
in our estimation, augur well for the future.
Respectfully yours,
Snth E, fien

Mr. 1s:
I agree in general with theconments
on Lieutenant Towson's memorandum made in
the brief memorandum prepared in
Mr. Hornbeok's office.
In addition, I would like to point out
that Lieutenant Towson's comment in the next
to the last paragraph on page 4 to the
effect that in case we accept the new order
in East Asia, any further action beyond
genuine cooperation in its creation need not
be considered, seems to metobe highly ques-
tionable. I should think that we might
accept the new order in the sense that we
might not resist it by military action, and
at the same time deoline to further its
establishment by "cooperating" in the sense
that we would extend large credits to Japan.
Again, in the first paragraph on page 5
Lieutenant Towson makes the flat assertion
that three- and four-way trade deals are the
rule in countries devoted to policies of
strict authoritarian control of foreign
trade. That assertion, in my opinion, is
simply contrary to fact. I think it is
generally agreed that in the case of such
countries, departures from bilateral trade
balancing practices are the exception rather
than

=== Page 28 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
than the rule. It is, of course, believed
that in some instanoes Germany attempted
to act as a middle-man in distributing
South American doffee. Such instanees,
however, must have constituted a very small
proportion of Germany's trade.
With reference to Japanese gold
reserves, Lieutenant Towson estimates that
as of the end of 1939 gold remaining in
Japan amounted to 59,256 kilograms, or
about $66,000,000. My estimate is con-
siderably higher than this. In my memo-
randum of January 23, 1940 on Japanese
gold and foreign exchange resources, I
estimated that at the end of 1939 there
were some $115,000,000 of gold left 1n
Japan. The chief reason for this dis-
crepanoy is that I estimate that in the
three years, 1937 to 1939, inclusive,
Japan may have acquired as much as
$75,000,000 of old gold, while
Lieutenant Towson estimates the acquisi-
tion of gold from this source at
$29,000,000, or $46,000,000 1ess than my
estimate. His estimate of newly mined
gold acquired exceeds mine by $7,000,000,
so that the net difference in these items
amounts to $39,000,000. In addition,
Lieutenant Towson appears to have over-
looked the acquisit1on of $16,000,000 of
gold from the Banks of Chosen and Taiwan
in 1937 and an export of approximately
$5,000,000'of gold to the United Kingdom
that took place during the same year. It
would therefore appear that even assuming
that

=== Page 29 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
that his figures are accurate in other
respects, his estimate should be
1ncreased by $11,000,000.
With reference to our difference
as to the amount of old gold acquired,
it may be noted that $35,000,000 of
th1s difference of $46,000,000, men-
tioned above, is apparently accounted
for by old gold acquired during the
first eight months of 1937. This item
which was discussed at some length in
my memorandum of May 15, 1939 on
Japanese gold and foreign exchange
resouroes,is based on an estimate pre-
pared by Mr. Collado when he was study-
ing this subjeot intensively for the
Federal Reserve Bank of New York. For
reasons set forth in that memorandum,
I am inclined to accept Mr. Collado's
estimate, although at the same time, I
freely concede that it may be in error.

I have read with interest Lieutenant

entitled "The Eoonomic Position of Japan
Current Estimate".
777
Lieutenant Towson's material on the
importance of imports and exports to
Japan's economy, on the depletion of
Japan's gold reserves, and on the trend
of Japan's fiscal problems seems to me
well chosen and well presented.
I find the material which he has
colleoted under the heading "Politioal"
much less comprehensive, as is not un-
natural in view of the stated subject of
the memorandum. The tentative conolusion
reached by Lieutenant Towson under this
heading seems in general to be supported
by his observations.
Under the heading "Embargo", Lieutenant
Towson advances certain conclusions (last
three sentencesof paragraph at bottom of
page 35 and top of page 36) which seem to
be drawn from data and analysis of which
no presentation appears either in that sec-

APR 25194DEPARTMENT OF STATE
April 24, 1940.
Economic Position of Japan" prepared for G-2
by Lieutenant Towson is on the whole excellent-
ly done and merits close attention. In con-
sidering the conclusions of the memorandum
it should be borne in mind that Lieutenant
Towson has had the advantage of the most au-
thoritative official information available to
the Government. The principal conclusions of
the memorandum are as follows:
(1) Japan's efforts to obtain supplies
of essential raw materials and manufactured
products without the expenditure of substantial
amounts of foreign exchange have been unsuc-
cessful. Japan's debit balance of trade with
foreign exchange countries in 1939 amounted to
437,000,000 yen and it appears probable that
there will be another large trade debit this
year (statistics not available to Lieutenant
Towson at the time he prepared his memorandum
indicate that during the first three months of
1940 Japan's debit trade balance with foreign
exchange countries was larger than during the
first three months of l939). This debit can
be balanced only by continued exportation of
gola.
(2) Reserves of essential materials in
Japan have been progressively depleted.
(3) It is estimated that Japan's gold
reserve at the present time does not exceed
US$67,000,000.

=== Page 33 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
US$67,000,000. (This figure checks very well
with the Treasury's estimate and with studies
done in the Department). Other possible for-
eign exchange assets (i.e., foreign securi-
ties) might increase Japan's foreign exchange
resources to US$200,000,000 and gold produc-
tion in Japan this year is liberally estimated
at US$60,o00,000, making an outside total of
US$260,000,000 of foreign exchange resources
available to Japan, whereas in each of the
past three years Japan has liquidated about
US$225,000,000 of her resources. This means
that the end of this year will witness vir-
tually the complete exhaustion of Japan's for-
eign exchange resources. Serious repercus-
sions on domestic conditions might be felt
before the point of complete exhaustion is
reached.
(4) Price control has virtually broken
down in Japan; prices are soaring; and note
circulation has increased sharply unaccompan-
ied by a corresponding increase in the volume
of goods to be sold.  "It appears that infla-
tion in Japan is getting out of hand".
(Comment: this conclusion may be too bold.
While the evidence of growing inflation in
Japan is clear enough it would seem that the
Government has adequate authority and there
is still time to prevent any immediate runaway
situation from developing.)

OF ECOMCMIC STULIES
In defersnee to Charles d'Avenant who semposed the paragraph
appearing om the first page of this study, we must beware of eaying that
preblems of finance and economics are new aspeets of war. But it is
legitimate to suppose that he would be surprised if he returned to ob-
serve the gigentie proportions to whioh the taek of supplying the Army
has growm, and that he did not anticipate, when he wrote, a time when
the cost of war would be more in a week than the ontire annual ineome
of the England of his day.
The esomomie aspeots of "measures short of war" and of aotual
warfare are not so thrilling as the actual movement of troops, but it
is becoming e commonplace nowadays that wars are won and lost in the
workshop rather than om the battlefield. The present European confliot
is the first in the world's history whioh has been recognised, from its
first day, to be a struggle of mobilised industrial power as woll as of
trained manpower.
For years the nations of the world have been waging pease with
all the esonemie weapons at their command, each, in the effort to in-
ereass its trade at the expemse of its ccmpetitors, has takn measures
whieh sorved to augssnt the stresses whish, one after another, led to
the opeming of astual warfare. Throughout the world, naticns in lese
faverable esmpetitive pesitions, finding themselves deprived of that
degree of eecmemie well-being enjoyed by others, developed the mass
peyehoses whish enabled them to foist upom their peoples the fallaeiess
justifieatiens of aggressive warfare.
In the ecenemie warfare whieh has inereased in intensity.
the astagonists have besn the oppesing systems of eapitaliam and eatareky.

The ramifioations of this warfare transeend the boundaries of areas
wherein armed confliet is normally confined, and the effective range
of its weapone is far greater than that of the world's most potent
artillery.
There are those who, progressing in their studies from front
line combat, to lines of commmication, to matters of supply, to in-
dustrial output of the materials of war, now admit that the study of
economios is not merely a matter of oultural development, but motually
of professional value to the soldier. One step more is needed: a reali-
sation that observed economio phenomena may have their origins anywhere
in the world, and that it is necessary to examine the problem from the
widest possible viewpoint.
The economist may perform three important end valuable services
to the oombat arms: He can assist in evaluating the military potentiali-
ties of possible ememiee in time of peaces he oan mid in proteoting the
home front from the shook of serious sounomio maladjustments mnd the re-
sulting lowered civilien morale; he oen evolve sohemes for dislosating
the soonemio orgemisation of the eneay and so lessen the power of his
combat arms.
Sush econcmie studies should be orgmized to provide the
widest possible range, in order that important reperoussions outside
arbitrarily establiahed geographie areas, and the interplay of sause,
in one arem, and effeot in another, may not bè underestimated.
The carliest indications of potential confliet are to be
found in the statistieal evidenee of esonomio maladjustments. These
may disslese suoh a serieus defieiemey of natural resouroes as to en-

CONCLUSION
There are inoreasing signe of economie strain in Japan. The
war, however, has brought an evanesoent prosperity: the pressure of
population in the rural areas has beem relieved by mobilization and by
the mevemeut of labor into industry, manufacturing is booming despite
a 30 per cent shortage of power40s umemploymont is not a problom; prices
and the oost of living have aavenood. Eut economio troubles are piling
up for the future: a huge and growing internal debt, inflation of the
ourrenoy; the prospeot of runaway prices over-expansion of war industries
at the expense of peace time industry.
Where these dangers are appreciuted, they only strengthen the
41
deternination to puah ahead, everything is being staked on success
Foreign exohange is the waakest point in Japan's economy. She
suffers from oritical deficionoies of supplies essential to the contin-
uance of her war offort and these can only be moquired by expenditures
of foreim ourrenoies. Her war reserves of these stretegic materials
have probably been so depleted by this time that the continuation of
her military pewer depends upon their importation. From date presented
in this study, it appeare that her ultimate resouroes of foreign exohange
will be exhausted this year.
Japen thus faees a hard choiee: Will she abandon the China In-
eident and salvage what she oan from a peace of unoertain prospeots with
a puppet regime? Will she go on to the bitter end with oollapee of her
eeemomy ewalting the eonolusiom? Will she assume that the best defense
1s offense, east the die onse more and try to resusoitate her failing re-
seuress by some further aot of aggression?

67-8.mya.
tyo'B despatch no. 4556, March 4, 1940,
tled "Growing shortages in basic commodities
"daily necessities".
The Embassy here continues its series of
preliminary sketches with regard to economic
nd social conditions in Japan. In the present
despatoh the Embassy points out that there are
widespread and intricate evasions of price
control in Japan consequent upon serious
ahortages in basic commodities and also in
daily consumption necessities. The Embassy also
points out that there is no doubt that the
productive mechanism of Japan has been expanded
in the last two years, and that the expanded
facilities would not be wiped out even should
Japan go through an economic collapse. The
Embassy makes note, in passing, of the great
difficulty of getting factual and statistical
information with regard to Japanese economics
under present conditions.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
MR. 
MAY 1 6 1940
FILED
Washington.
Sir:
With this despatch I have the honor to continue the
February 15, 1940, discussing some of the problems which
Japan faces in its present economic condition. These
F/FG
problems are daily becoming more acute and are the sub-
jeot of considerable discussion not only in newspapers
colloquialism
4

=== Page 98 === (confidence 0.98)
-2~
nai nai zukushi", meaning literally "a lack of everything",
is widely used to describe the situation in which it is
becoming progressively more difficult to obtain basic com-
modities and daily necessities.
As in all economic phenomena, there appear in this in-
stance many causes. Among the more important there is to be
noted first of all a net decrease in the available stocks of
consumer commodities, not only in Japan itself but throughout
what is called the "Yen Bloc Area". The recent expansion of
industry in Japan has been, owing to the emphasis on a wartime
economy, largely effected in munition industries and closely
related lines of endeavor. Obviously, production of munitions
is not, in an economic sense, productive of wealth, but
rather represents consumption of available goods. Therefore,
emphasis on industries of this type tends to decrease the
productive efficiency of all other industries, particularly
through the priority given to munition industries to utilize
available labor and raw material supplies. Even in normal
times emphasis on expansion of industries of this type would
result in a depletion of the stocks of materials available
for ordinary industry. This general trend has been serious-
ly aggravated by the officially-sponsored attempt to achieve
Japan's independence in the so-called heavy industries,
uch as machine tools, heavy chemicals, petroleum refining
and coal liquefaction. To accomplish this end the Government
has, in addition to its abnormally heavy expenditures for
military supplies, made available large sums of money to
encourage development of those industries. This has resulted
1n

=== Page 99 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
in a large increase in the purchasing power of the
people in general. The Chugai, January 7, 1940, is
the authority for the statement that on December 31,
1939, Bank of Japan notes in circulation exceeded
Yen 3,810,000,000, said to be Yen 1,000,000,000 more
than the amount in circulation on the corresponding date
of the preceding year. Concurrently, the Government
has followed a policy of price control in an endeavor
to prevent serious inflation. These factors have re-
sulted in such undesirable practices as hoarding and
"bootleg" transactions involving practically all com-
modities.
In spite of the Government's policy, prices have
not been maintained without marked fluctuation but have
shown a steady rise. On September 18 last the Government
issued a decree stating that from that day on prices in
the great majority of comnodities were not to be increased.
This gave further impetus to the practice of illegal trans-
actions. High prices in daily necessities were, until
last fall, largely limited to items of wearing apparel,
but following the shortage of rice, foodstuffs in general,
including rice, meat, fresh vegetables, fish, etc., began
to rise appreciably. The increased cost of living has out-
stripped the wage increases of the lower salaried groups
and has become a very serious problem.
A short review of some of the methods of illegal trans-
actions which are widely practiced and which have been the
topio of considerable discussion in the vernacular press
recently may not be uninteresting. The Asahi on February 26,
1940,

=== Page 100 === (confidence 0.99)
1940, lists some fourteen methods:
1. Goods having fixed official prices are
sold only in combination with articles
having no fixed price; for example, pur-
chase of kerosene must be accompanied by
purchase of some other expensive article,
the price of which is not officially controlled.
2. Excessive charges are made for delivery serv-
ices.
3. The prospective buyer places a deposit for
the purchase of certain commodities and,
after deliberately forfeiting his deposit,
concludes a new contract with the seller at
the official price.
4. nder a system of barter, commodities having
no official quotation are exchanged for
articles the price of which is controlled
by the Government.
5. Deliberate bribery.
6. Excessive charges for the workmanship in-
volved in minor processing of commodities.
7. The trade names of certain commodities are
changed to avoid official prices.
8. Obligations of the seller are transferred,
in some instances, to the purchaser.
9. Adulteration of the commodity or delivery
of only part of the amount officially pur-
chased.
10. Some purchase contracts include transporta-
tion charges, whereas delivery is only made
to the nearest shipping point.
11. Articles are purchased and slightly used,
after which they are transferred to a third
person at a higher cost.
12. The same article may be the subject of two or
more paper transactions before actually pur-
chased by the buyer, resulting ultimately in
a price above that legally allowed.
13. Similar to the above, contracts are sometimes
made among three people, with one merely act-
ing as a dummy.
14.

=== Page 101 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
l4. The buyer purposely loses in the presence
of the seller a certain sum of money which
is immediately taken to the nearest police
station and later claimed by the buyer who,
conformably with Japanese practice, pays to
the seller ten per cent of the money which he
"lost".
As it has become increasingly evident that the "low
price policy" which has been followed by the recent Cabinets
is incompatible with the Government's grandiose plans for
the expansion of industrial productivity, criticism has
mounted in volume. The Cabinet has recently announced a
plan to make the price control agency directly responsible
to the Cabinet, rather than to the Ministry of Commerce and
Industry, as at present. It has also been announced that
an attempt will be made to institute what are called
"appropriate prices", rather than to maintain low prices
for all commodities. Such articles as are necessary to
the livelihood of the low income groups will, if possible,
continue to be sold at low prices and the deficits of the
producers of those commodities will be made up by subsidies
and allowances provided by the Government. In other com-
modities some rise in price will be allowed, in order that
the producers will not only be able to meet the expenses
of production but will also receive a fair profit. The
vernacular press, however, has expressed considerable
doubt as to the efficacy of such a program which, it is
said, does not cope with the fundamental causes. Refer-
ence is made to the extraordinarily large current Govern-
ment expenditures and to the Yen 10,000,000,000 budget
for

=== Page 102 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
for the coming fiscal year. Rationalization of Govern-
ment expenditures is urged. At the same time the press
suggests that the program for productive expansion be re-
examined, pointing out that continued emphasis on expansion
of productive facilities, new factories, machines, et cetera,
will only serve to accentuate the already apparent dis-
crepancy between the increase in productive capacity and
the lack of a corresponding increase in production. It
has not been possible to develop adequate numbers of skilled
technicians or skilled laborers. The press reports that
many of these new factories are, in fact, wasting valuable
materials in the production of inferior articles. The
Government's policy of encouraging export of foodstuffs
to foreign countries is roundly berated as being unsound,
in view of the growing shortage of such articles within the
country.
Considerable has been said in the present Diet session
concerning the necessity of "promptly stabilizing the live-
lihood of the people". Commodities which are said to be
seriously affected cover practically all the articles of
daily use, as well as most of the basic materials - for
example: matches, rice wine, rice, rice bran, charcoal,
nails, cotton, cotton goods, silk, rayon, staple fiber,
drugs, beer, paper, iron, coal, leather, rubber, gasoline,
soap, medicines, fertilizer, et cetera, et cetera. Measures
which have been suggested include furtlier official encourage-
ment of the purchase of insurance, annuities and of the
deposit

=== Page 103 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
deposit of savings; Government control of such basic
commodities as rice, cereals, coal, gold, et cetera;
a rationing system on the basis of tickets for daily
necessities such as rice, coal, wheat, charcoal, soya
bean sauce, bean paste, et cetera.
Any studies of economic conditions of Japan today
are seriously handicapped and limited by the absence of
.reliable statistics. Important and basic information of
almost every type is considered a military secret. The
information in this despatch is compiled on the basis of
personal observations, current comment in newspapers, and
periodicals. The result therefore is largely impressionistic
rather than factual. It is, however, obvious that Japan's
retail markets are seriously troubled with growing shortages
of daily necessities and basic commodities. The price
policy hitherto followed by the Government has failed and
has resulted in flagrant, clever illegal transactions creating
a price level and a cost of living far above that desired
or admitted to exist by official circles. Government
measures have at best been expedient attempts to meet suo-
cessive crises. These measures have, however, succeeded
to a degree surprising to most observers here in Japan
and abroad. I do not foresee any alleviation of the present
growing difficulties in the near future. There is, however,
no doubt that productive instruments have been in the last
two years markedly expanded. If in the course of the next
ten months Japan is able to utilize that expansion to
produce consumers goods in some measure comparable to the
effective

=== Page 104 === (confidence 0.97)
-8-
effective demand evidenced by utilized purchasing power
of the people, not only internally but throughout the
"yen bloc area", it may be that confidence in the avail-
ability of commodities will be restored and the dangers
of unbridled inflation avoided. The difficulties of mal-
distribution, hoarding, excessive Government expenditures
and the emphasis on a war economy are great. The dangers
of political failure in China, unrest in Japan, and natural
calamities are not to be disregarded in an economy so deli-
cately balanced as that of Japan. I expect for the moment
a continuance of the trend toward inflation with the Govern-
ment attempting to keep prices and supplies under control.
Whether ultimate success or failure is realized, it is per-
haps important to bear in mind that the expanded productive
facilities will not be wiped out even should Japan go through
an inflationary debacle or deflationary collapse.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C.Grew.

ECONOMIC SITUATION IN JAPAN IN 1939
(Prepared in the Division of Regional Information and
based on a report from the Office of the American
Commercial Attache, Tokyo, Japan.)
Preoccupation with the.China Incident dominated Japan's economy during
1939, overshadowing other developments. While most economic statistics
indicate pronounced business and industrial activity and a decided improvement
in the agricultural situation, there were signs that the nation's economic
structure was being seakened by the continued strain of coping with the situation
in China. The most obvious signs included: a sharp increase in the note circula-
894.50/1
tion and general trend toward inflation; a significant decline in the rate of
absorption of national bonds in the last half of the year; an increase of 32 per
cent in the national debt; end a heavier tax burden. Mounting comnodity shortages
-- owing to the depletion of reserves and concentration on production of military
2
and allied equipment -- as well as a decline in labor efficiency and in industrial
2
efficiency, were other evidences of the economic strain.
Economic Difficulties Augmented by International Developments.
Notice by the United States of its intention to abrogate the l9ll commercial
treaty with Japan and the outbreak of war in Europe augmented the numerous per
plexing problems facing the nation, elthough the effect of the war was lessened
somewhat owing to the previous organization of Japan's econony on a wartime basis.
However, the Government took further steps to cushion the impact of developments
arising from the European War, including revisions of the materials mobilization
program, the plans for expanding the nation's productive capacity, and plans for
F
/A
financial mobilization. Measures were introduced to suppress speculation,
5745

=== Page 108 === (confidence 0.92)
Economic Situation in Japan in 1939 - Anril 1940 - 2.
1
regulate commodity prices, assure continuation of low money rates, provide
necessary war risk insurance, and sunc rvise shipning. There developed a specula-
tive wave of buying of securities of export enterprises in anticipation of a war
boom, but it wes subsequently realized thit Japan was in no position to take full
advantage of the trade opoortunities offered by the European war.
0
At the end of the year it w's chvious th t the nation was unswerved in its
determination to carry on its plans for creating a "New Order in Asia," but public
leaders and economic otservers were outspoken with regard to the indefinite
period of further stcrifices that would be renuired to achieve this objective.

=== Page 109 === (confidence 0.98)
onomic Situation in Japan in 1939 - April 1940 - 3. :
2
Small Increase in Total Industrial Production.
According to best available indices, industrial production during 1939
registered a new volume gain of about 5 percent over 1938 and from 10 to 12
percent over 1937, when total production was estimated at 16,486,000,000 yen
($4,761,000,000). The record of industrial output last year, however, is not
impressive when it is considered that thousands of small firms have been es-
tablished over the past two years to produce military equipment; that more than
4,700,000,000 yen ($1,360,000,000) has been invested in expanding productive
capacity since 1937; and that factory employment has reached an all time high.
Factors responsible for the comparatively slight gain in l939 were the shortage
of electric power and coal, the shortage of labor -- particularly skilled labor -
and a decline in labor efficiency and in plant operating efficiency. Moreover,
there was & shortage of basic raw materials and severe restrictions were imposed
on production of goods for domestic consumption. It is believed thot output in
these industries was below the 1938 level.
Attention Concentrated on Expansion of Heavy Industries.
The 1939 gain in industrial production was due almost entirely to larger
output in the heavy industries. While specific figures for these industries are
not revealed, the official composite index covering pig iron, steel materials,
machinery, and tools showed a gain in production of 17.1 percent during the first
nine months of 1939, comp red with the same period in 1938. Special efforts were
centered on expansion of the nation's productive capacity in accordance with the
three year industrial expansion program for Japan, Manchuria, and North China;
but owing to the shortage of commodities and power, end the lack of skilled labor,
it is believed the progress made was not as great as scheduled. Chemical pro-
duction was restricted by power shortage, while the mining industry showed a
volume gain of only 3 percent despite efforts to dncrease mineral output.
5745

=== Page 110 === (confidence 0.98)
monomic Situation in Japan in 1939 - April 1940 4..
Short Supply of Iron and Steel.
Although production in the iron and steel industry reached a record level,
the supply of raw materials was admittedly short throughout the year -- a factor
partly responsible for the slow progress in-expanding other industries. Fear of
a cessation of scrap iron imports from the United States -- following abrogation
of the commercial treaty - and a shortage in ores from British and French colonial
possessions efter the outbreak of the European War, caused restrictions. to be
imposed on deliveries of these commodities to the less efficient plants. It was
decided, furthermore, to exercise unified control over scrap iron purchases and
distribution, while particular attention was given to the possibility of utilizing
low grade ores.
Stocks of Cotton Textiles Increase Despite Heavier Exports.
Conditions were somewhat improved in the cotton textile industry as manu.
facturers and dealers became more accustomed to operating under imposed re
strictions. Raw cotton imports for the year totaled 1,335,000,000 pounds,an
increase of 7 percent over 1938, with India and Brazil receiving the largest.
benefit. Arrivals from the United States end China declined.
Cotton piece goods exports totaled 2,446,0Q0,000 square yards, a 12 percent
increase over l938, but the value of shipments declined by 0.1 percent. Notwith-
standing increased export trade, the heavy accumulation of cotton textile stocks
towards the end of the year presented a problem to the industry.
Labor Shortage and Employment Shifts.
1
The accepted theory th't Japan possesses an unlimited labor supply has been
dispelled by development over the past two years. It became increasingly apparent
in 1939 that the nation was incapable of supplying sufficient manpower for both
military requirements and the increased demand in industrial plants, especially
for skilled labor. The demand for workers in the heavy industries was met to some
5745

=== Page 111 === (confidence 0.99)
conomic Situation in Japan in 1939 - April 1940 - 5.
:extent by shifting employment from the light industries. Consequently, :employment
gains in the machinery, vehicle, tools and instruments, and metal industries were
partially at the expense of the spinning, weaving, dyeing, braiding, knitting and
rubber industries. While the rate of increase in employment covering all in-
dustries since the outbreak of the China Inoident is 26 percent, in the machinery
industry employment has increased by 94 percent; by 68 percent in the tool in-
dustry; 63 percent in the shipbuilding and vehicle industry; and 42 percent in
the metals industry. A definite trend was noted also in 1939 towards the con-
centration of labor in large scale establishments.
Average wage rates were 7.5 percent higher during the first eight months of
the year comp red with the seme period in 1938. However, because of overtime work,
actual earnings showed a gain of 9.9 percent.
Prices and Living Costs Advance Sharply Despite Control Measures.
Commodity price control became an outstanding problen of the year, as under
the stimulus of diminishing supplies, comnodity shortages and rising production
costs, wholesale and retail prices surged upward monthly. Following the outbreak
of the European war the Government instituted measures to peg prices at the levels
prevailing on September 18, but this action had little effect. In fact the
inability of the Government to control prices was a contributory cause for the
resignation of the Abe Cabinet early in 1940.
Wholesale price indices of the Ministry of Commerce and the Bank of Japan
1
showed average advances of from 8 to 1l percent during the first eleven months of
1939, compcred with the same period in 1938; an advance of from 15 to 22 percent
over 1937, and from 33 to 50 percent over 1936. Retail price indices advanced
from 12 to 13 percent over the first 11 months of 1938, 30 percent over 1937, and
42 percent over l936. The most conspicuous gains occurred in retail prices of
food and clothing.
5745

=== Page 112 === (confidence 0.99)
Mconomic Situation in Japan in 1939 - April 1940 6-
 According to the Cabinet's Statistical Bureau, there was a 15.3 percent rise
in living costs of laborers during the year and a l4.1 percent rise for the
salaried class over the 1938 level. Since the outbreak of the China Incident,
the cost of living for laborers has advanced 30.7 percent while that for the
salaried class has increased 28.5 percent.
Greater Prosperity in Rural Areas.
The most favorable feature in Japan's economy last year was the improvement
in agricultural conditions. In the previous year the rise in prices of farm
products lagged far behind the price advance in other commodities required in
farming communities. The improvement in 1939 is reflected in the official weighted
index covering income from rice, silk and wheat, which showed a 50 percent advance
from the end of December 1938 to the end of November 1939, while the price advance
in principal articles for form consumption was only 6 percent during the same
period. The more general prosperity in rural areas was reflected in increased
demand for currency, the liquidation of bank loans, and a sharp rise in rural
bank deposits.
Larger Crops Reported.
Although the rice crop in western Japan was seriously damaged by the drought,
weather conditions were reported favorable for all crops in other sections of the
country, and returns from wheat, barley and rye were the highest on record. The
total rice crop was reported to be somewhat higher than in l938 while the cocoon
crop reached the highest level since 1933.
Reorganization of Japan's agriculture on a wartime basis was largely re-
sponsible for the more satisfactory crop returns. The improvement was achieved
in face of such handicaps as the heavy drain on farm labor by military require-
ments and industry, and the shortage in fertilizers and farm equipment.
5745

=== Page 113 === (confidence 0.99)
monomic Situation in Japan in 1939 - April 1940 - 7.
Rice Prices Advance as Supplies in Cities Diminish.
The Government took measures during the year to check rising rice prices and
endeavored to control distribution of rice. Notwithstanding these measures,
prices rose sharply as a result of the small volume of available stocks --. due to
the light carry-over from 1938, the drought, and a subnormal crop in Chosen.
Reluctance on the part of farmers to sell their holdings contributed to the price
advance and caused a serious shortage of rice in urban centers towards the end of
the year. The Government is reported to be considering steps to reduce 1940 rice
consumption, but it is expected that heavy imports from foreign countries may be
necessary.
Silk Prices Forced Upward.
Conditions in the silk industry at the close of 1938 and early in 1939
clearly foreshadowed the rise in silk prices. Domestic consumption of silk wes
heavy as a result of the ban on domestic use of cotton, and foreign demand for
silk was strong with stocks low owing to the poor cocoon crop of the previous
year. These factors, plus speculative activities on the part of raw silk dealers,
soon forced quotations to a point where the Government began liquidating its silk
holdings. This quantity was soon absorbed, however, and the market continued in
an upward trend. Despite official warnings against the undesirability of high
silk quotations, no cction was taken to check the upward movement -- as was done
with other commodities -- owing to the fact th-t larger amounts of foreign ex-
change accrued from the higher value of silk exports. The average quotation for
Standard 13/15 denier white D-grade silk at the end of the year was 2, 230 yen
($535) per bale comp red with 821 yen ($223) per bale at the end of 1938. How-
ever, largely as a result of regulations enforced in January of this year govern-
ing control over the distribution of silk, quotations declined to less than
1,700 yen ($408) per bale by the middle of February.
5745

=== Page 114 === (confidence 0.99)
conomic Situation in Japan in 1939- April 1940 8 .
Financial Strain More Evident.
During 1939 the national debt increased by 5,297,000,000 yen ($1,377,000,000)
reaching a peak of 21, 520, 000,000 yen ($5,595,000,000) at the end. of the year,
double the amount outstanding at the beginning of the China Incident.. In the last
half of 1939 there was a pronounced decline in the national bond absorption rate,
and a tendency towards inflation manifested principally by a sharp increase in the
note issue end rising commodity prices. The average daily note issue of the Bank
of Japan totaled 2,374,000,000 ($617,000,000), compared with the 1938 average of
1,919,000,000 yen ($547,000,000) and 1,525,000,000 yen ($442,000,000) during 1937.
When the issue reached the record high of 3,317,000,000 yen ($992,000,000) on
December 30, 1939, the 1egal 1imit was exceeded by 1,116,000,000 yen ($290,000,000)
More Deficit Bonds Floated - Taxation Higher.
Bond flotations during 1939 totaled a value of 5,281,000,000 yen ($1,
373,000,000), an increase of 22 percent compared with 1938. While the absorption
rate increased from 87.4 percent in 1938 to 89.2 percent last year, the rate
during the last six months of the year dropped to 77 percent. Considerable con-
cern prevails over the future trend of the bond absorption rate, especially as it
is probable that bonds to the value of 5,800,000,000 yen ($1,500,000,000). may have
to be floated during 1940 in view of estimated expenditures of 10,360,000,000 yen
($2,694,000,000) in the 1940-41 budget.
A new tax system, providing for raising taxes of all classes, was approved
toward the end of 1939. That the tax burden has become heavier since the China
Incident is indicated by the rise in tax revenues from 1,050,000, 000 yen
.($287,000,000) in the 1936–37 f1scal year to 2,245,000,000 yen ($584,000,000)
in the 1939-40 fiscal year.
Exchange Control Continued - Yen Linked to Dollar.
All foreign exchange transactions continued to be rigidly controlled
5745

=== Page 115 === (confidence 0.99)
nomic Situation in Japan-ir 1939 - April 1940 - 9.
throughout 1939 and importers experienced increasing delay and difficulty in
securing permits to make remittances abroad in foreign currencies. In view of
this situation, a majority of American firms adopted the policy during the last
half of the year of doing business with Japan only on the basis of irrevocable
confirmed letters of credit. By the end of the year there was a further accumula-
tion of frozen funds of American firms operating in the country.
The exchange market was featured by the decision reached on October 24 to
shift the linling of the yen from sterling to the United States dollar, and to fix
the selling rate at $0.23-7/16. This action was taken to avoid undue exchange
fluctuations and to facilitate exports, particularly to the United States and
South Americen morkets.
Foreign Trade Increased - Import Excess in Trade with Foreign Currency Countries.
Total exports fron Japan Proper amounted to a value of 3,576,000,000 yen
($930,000,000) for 1939 -- an al1 time high and an increase of 32 percent over
1938. Imports were valued at 2,918,000,000 yen ($759,000,000), representing a
gain of 9.5 percent compared with 1938. The export excess of 658,000,000 yen
($171,000,000) was also a record high and a substantial increase over the
27,000,000 yen ($7,650,000) recorded in 1938. However, Japan's export trade ex-
pansion.resulted primarily from heavier shipments to the yen-bloc area, that is to
Manchuria and China, for which little foreign exchange was received. Total ship
ments to these areas amounted to 1,747, 000,000 yen ($454, 000,000), an increase of
50 percent compared with 1938, while imports from the yen-bloc totaled 682,000,000
yen ($177,300,000), a in of 22.percent over 1938.
Exports to foreign currency countries, on the other hand, increased by only
.12 percent, to a value of 1,829,000, 000 yen ($476,000,000) in 1939 and imports
advanced by 6.4 percent, totaling 2,235,000,000 yen ($581,000,000), leaving a net
import balance in trade with foreign currency countries of 406,000,000 yen
5745

=== Page 116 === (confidence 0.98)
lc Situation in Japan in 1939 - Anril 1940 - 10.
($105,000,000), compared with 575,000,000 yen ($164,000,000) 1n 1938
Exports of all economic categories increased during l939, food and-beverage
exports increasing by 42 percent, raw materials by 74 percent, semimanufactures
by 41 percent, and manufactured goods by 23 percent, compared with the previous
year. Thee were also significant increases over 1938 in imports of raw materials
and simimanufactures, amounting to 9 and 22 percent, respectively. Arrivals of
manufactured goods, however, declined by 13 percent, reflecting the efforts to
make processed materials locally.
Substantial Increase in Trade with the United States.
Imports from the United Sttes into Japan totaled 1,002,000,000 yen
($261,000,000) during 1939, representing a gain of 9:5 percent compared with 1938,
while 1939 exports to the United States amounted to 642;000,000 yen ($167,000,000),
an increrse of 50.8 percent over the previous year. Raw cotton imports from the
United States declined by ll.8 percent, this loss being more than offset by
increased purchases of scrap iron, rcfined copper, and machine tools. It is
believed that purchases of petroleum products, aircraft and parts and automotive
products declined.
Raw silk shipments to the United States increased 47 percent in value, but
declined 15.5 percent in volume, compared with 1938. Considerable value gains
were registered also in exports to the United St tes of canned foods, vegetable
oils, fish oils, unbleached cotton piece goods and electric light bulbs.
War in Europe Stimulates Export Trade.
The European war afforded some stimulus to e xport trade, shipments of
Japanese goods to al1 countries averaging a value of 367,000,000 yen ($95,000,000)
per month during the last four months of 1939 compared with 263,000,000 yen
($68, 000,000) in the first eight months of the year. Most of the gains were
with North Americen and South American markets. Imports were only slightly
affected by the war, falling to a monthly average of 240,000,000 yen ($62,000,000)
in the 1ast four months compared with 243,000,000 yen ($63,000,000) in the
previous eight months. Imports from Germany averaged 6,700,000 yën ($1,700,000)
compared with the previous, eight months' average of 14,500,000 yen (3.800,000).

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
SION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
August 6, 1940
Reference Tokyo's despatch no. 4801,
£1, 1940, entitled "Effeot of European War
Japan's Foreign Trade".
The most immediate effeot of the outbreak
the war upon Japan was the loss of her trade
with Germany. Between Germany, Japan and Man-
churla there had been carried on a three-
sornered trade of major importance in soya beans
and bean oil to Germany in return for machinery
and tools essential to Japanese military and
industrial plants. The stoppage of this trade
was felt keenly both in Manchuria and Japan
and refleoted prinoipally upon Japan's
ambitious plans for industrial and military ex-
pansion. Moreover, souroes of several important
industrial materials were out off, notably pulp
from Finland, steel and steel bearings from
Soandinavia, and machinery from Great Britain.
Thus far Japan's exports have neither suf-
fered greatly nor have they been benefited as a
result of the European war. There has been an
inorease in Japan's exports 1n 1940, but most
of the increase took place with respect to
yen bloo markets. Japan has been put in a
position of greater dependence upon the Ameri-
can market and present Japanese trade policies
place more emphasis upon securing access to
sources of supply.

Sir:
In continuation of the serios of despatches on
various phases of Japan's econogy palled for by the
§94 50/114a
Department in instruction no. 1849 of November 25, 1939,
I have the honor to comment briefly on the effect of the
F/A
European war on Japan's foreign trade. As far as possible
this disoussion avoids the details and figures which would
be more appropriate in a commeroial report, and endeavors
to

=== Page 119 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
to prosent an outline picture of the situation.
During the Great War of 1914-18 Japan enjoyed a
trade boom of such proportion that the country was lifted
from an insignificant position in world trade to a place
among the commercial nations of the first rank. The
principal factors bringing about that boom were briefly
a follows: (1) domination by Japanese merchants of
the markets of the Far East, (2) the expansion of trade
with neutral countries at the expense of the warring
nations, (3) cultivation of new markets in the colonies
of the belligerents higherto dominated by exports from
the mother country, and (4) enormous demand for Japanese
manufaotured products,principally oonsumer goods, on the
part of the belligerents.
As soon as news of the outbreak of war in Europe
reached Japan in September l939, a wave of optimism swept
over the country and the stock-markets boomed for a few
days. On second and more critical thought it was realized
that none of the above-mentioned factors whioh were in foroe
during the previous war need necessarily operate in the
same way to Japan's advantage during the current European
hostilities.
While Japan's trade with the Far East may have gained
to some extent at the expense of the European combatants,
that trade is now largely on a paper yen basis and does not
return any specie or foreign exchange to Japan. Nor does
it supply many of the commodities that are so sorely needed
in Japan. No war booms have appeared in any country, even
1n

=== Page 120 === (confidence 0.99)
-3
in the United States, which would create a demand for
Japanese products, nor have recent inoreases in Japaneso
trade with Latin America, of which so much has been heard
recently, been of much importance relative to Japan's
total foreign trade. With Japan engaged in large-soale
military operations in China, it has been impossible for
her to export military supplies to the belligerents in
Europe es she did in the Great War - much to her profit.
Even Japanese shipping which profited greatly during the
last war is largely occupied with the transport of military
supplies for the Japanese armies on the continent and is
unable to take advantage of its neutral situation.
The most immediate effect on Japan of the war was,
of course, the loss of the trade with Germany and the
countries invaded by Germany. Between Germany, Japan and
Manchuria there had been carried on a three-cornered trade
of major importance in soya beans and bean oil to Germany
in return for machinery and tools essential to Japanese
military and industrial plants. Financially the conse-
quence of the stoppage of this trade was felt more im-
mediately in Manchuria than by Japan, but the effeot on
Japan's ambitious plans for industrial and military ex-
pansion was strongly felt. At the same time the trade,
especially the import trate, in several important indus-
trial materials from Europe has been out off, notably pulp
from Finland used in the Japanese rayon industry, steel and
steel bearings from Soandinavia, machinery from Great Britain.
Thus

=== Page 121 === (confidence 0.99)
Thus far important Japanese exports have suffered
comparatively little from the European war. Nor on
the other hand has the war brought any great benofit
to that trade except in isolated and minor items. Japan's
principal exports are, in approximate order of value:
cotton textiles and yarns, raw silk, rayon textiles and
yarns, machinery and parts, preserved comestibles. Exports
of textiles of all kinds have suffered little injury,
largely because the war has aggravated the problems of
Japan's biggest competitor, Lancashire, in the competition
for the world's cotton tertile trade. However, the recent
decline in the sterling rate has tended to offset one of
Japan's advantages, namely a depreciated currency in terms
of foreign exchange, in the large market in British terri-
tories which takes approximately 80 percent of Japanese
cotton textile exports. Rayon goods were shipped abroad
in increasing quantities during the first stages of the
war, but a recessional trend is now in evidence. The
problem of pulp supplies for the making of rayon, imports
of which to the extent of about 40 per cent came from
Scandinavia and Finland, is bothering the Japanese who
anticipate increasing difficulty in obtaining sufficient
supplies from North America. Exports of raw silk, machinery
and comestibles show little effect from the war. None of
these items is exported to Europe in large quantity.
The total export trade of Japan during the first four
montks of 1940 showed a substantial inorease over that
of l939. It is significant, however, that most of the
inorease

=== Page 122 === (confidence 0.99)
-5
increases took place with respect to exports to the
yen-bloo markets which return no foreign exchange to
Japan, and can not be attributed to the effects of the
European war. Moreover, where increases are indicated
in certain items exported to gold-remitting countries,
such as silk to the United States, such increases are
accounted for more by advanced prices than by larger
volumes of exports. While thus far wartime measures
invoked by several nations to embargo or to control
imports have not seriously affected Japan's trade with
territories of the combatants, it seems inevitable that
as the scope and duration of the hostilities extend, an
increasing trend in that direction may be expected. At
the present time the atmosphere in Japanese exporting
circles is filled with foreboding of the closing of further
markets abroad and demands are heard for remedial action
by the Government.
Thus far the most serious effect of the war on Japan's
foreign trade has been on the side of imports rather than
exports. With the warring nations and neutrals as well
adopting various measures to guard their resources of
essential materials, Japan is encountering increasing
diffioulty in obtaining its requirements for the program
of military activity and industrial expansion now in progress.
The effeot has been particularly oppressive because of the
faot that much of Japan's export trade depends on raw mater-
ials imported from abroad for processing in this country.
The stoppage of machinery imports from Europe, especially
Germany

=== Page 123 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
Germany which furnished about 27 per cent of Japan's
machinery imports, has been a severe blow to Japanese
industry. In.the case of machinery, and likewise with
cotton, petroleum, scrap iron, copper, chemicals, wheat
and pulp, which are among the principle commodities essential
to Japan, the effect of the war has been to place this country
more completely in a position of uneasy dependence on an un-
friendly Americanmarket.
Accordingly it is not surprising that present Japanese
trade policies tend to place more emphasis upon securing
access to sources of supply than to the promotion of ex-
ports. It is reliably reported that since the German con-
quest of the Netherlands, Japan has demanded and obtained
from the Netherlands Indies assurances against interruption
in the supply to Japanese industry of essential materials.
In recent months Japan has concluded trade agreements and
treaties with various countries, Uruguay, Thai, Nicaragua,
and Columbia for the same purpose. Other treaties with
India and Russia and a new multilateral treaty with Italy
and Manchukuo are in prospect. The purpose of all of these
agreements is not only to secure a position of advantage in
disposing of Japanese products, but more important, to assure
the smooth flow of essential materials into Japan.
It may in short be said that thus far the effects of
the European war on Japan have been nogative when they have
not been definitely unfavorable. If the present war should
tako

=== Page 124 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
take on the aspect of the previous Great War and become
a protracted struggle of attrition in which the combatants
were obliged to purchase increasing supplies of both
munitions and consumers' commodities, Japan would undoubted-
ly find the war increasingly profitable to her trade. But
the turn of events in Europe seems to preclude that favorable
development. From the standpoint of foreign trade the Euro-
pean war has brought to Japan little but new problems.
Respectfully yours,
631
Joseph C. Grew.

CONFIDENTTAL
A telegram (no. 489) of November 27, 1940, from the
American Embassy at Peiping reads substantially as follows:
Peiping
It is believed that the only publicity given so far in/
to the five year plan for north China is the article in
the September 20 issue of the Ch'enpao (see telegram
no. 486, November 26, 1940, from Peiping) and a similar
article which was published in July 1940. In neither of
these articles has the Embassy found any evidence on the
part of the Japanese of a realization that previous plans
were too optimistic or any suggestion of a possible change
in economic policy. Japanese spokesmen have admitted from
time to time that their experimental process has not
worked out satisfactorily in some respects and should be
altered, and it is clear that many of the earlier plans
have not been successful in application. However, the
fact remains that at least for the past two and one half
years there has taken place no noticeable abandonment
of basic Japanese policy with regard to the economic
exploitation of China, especially north China and "Man-
chukuo". Although there are from time to time isolated
cases of improved treatment promised or given Chinese and
commercial interests and unimportant changes in local
regulations, the Embassy is not aware that the burden
imposed by the chief restrictions on almost all forms of
normal

=== Page 149 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
normal economic development in the Japanese-occupied areas
has been generally lightened or permanently removed.
Instead the trend in general still is in the direction of
complete control by the state with Japan as the principal
beneficiary, if not the only one. Therefore, it is the
opinion of the Embassy in Peiping that in the absence
of developments to the contrary the announcement in
Peiping of a five year plan for north China and the an-
nouncement in Tokyo of a ten year program for the yen
bloc should be considered not so much an increasing sense
of realism in connection with China's economic potentiality
as the expression of an intention to strengthen policy now
existing.

Sir:
I have the honor to refer to the Department's tele-
14
gram 476, November 15, 6 p.m., ond this Embassy's 1175,
November 20, 6 p.m. relative to the Outline of a Ten Year Plan
for the Economic Construction of the "Japan-Manchukuo-China
Bloc" announced on November 5, 1940.
PSG C
There is attached hereto a copy of an article which
appeared in the December, 1940, issue of the Tokyo Gasette,
entitled
131

=== Page 155 === (confidence 0.99)
entitled, "Summary of the Program for Economic Construction
Embracing Japan, Manchukuo and China", as announced by the
Cabinet Information Bureau on November 5, 1940. The Tokyo
Gasette is published monthly by the Tokyo Gasette Publishing
House under the supervision of the Cabinet Information Bureau.
Its contents therefore may well be considered official.
Under my Despatch No. 5161, November 22, 1940,
there was transmitted the Commercial Attache's Weekly Economic
Report No. 20 dated November 18, 1940, which dealt entirely
with this new Ten Year Economic Plan.
A careful study of the attached official statement
does not reveal any evidence of a realization on the part of
the Japanese that earlier plans for economic construction of
the so-called yen bloc were too optimistic. On the contrary
this new plan appears to be an intensified reiteration of the
anticipated permanency of Japan's dominating position in the
Far East. It envisages a closely kmit well organized economic
unit so rationalized industrially, financially and commercially
as to insure the peak of productive capacity within this bloo
and the building up and maintenance of an adequate defense force.
This may appear to foreigners as only another grandiose scheme,
but to the group now formulating Japan's future policies it re-
presents something real and vital, something leng anticipated
and cherished, and something which they consider worth risking
the destiny of Japan.
The Cabinet Planning Board is now engaged in preparing
a

=== Page 156 === (confidence 0.98)
a plan for the new National Eoonomic Structure of Japan.
When such a plan is completed and officially adopted it will
undoubtedly form a basis for this Ten Year Scheme, for
Japan mest first perfect her own economy so that it can
ast as a foundation for the broader and more extensive pro-
gram embracing Japan, "Manchukuo" and China, and eventually
Greater East Asia.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.
