SUMMARY OF THE PROGRAM FOR ECONOMIC CONSTRUCTION EMBRACING
JAPAN, "MANCHUKUO" AND CHINA
- Announced by the Cabinet Information
Bureau on November 5, 1940 -
BASIC PURPOSES
Now that the fundamental national policy of establishing
the Greater East Asia sphere of common prosperity, which aims
at the construction of a new world order, has entered upon a
new phase with the conclusion of the Three-Power Pact between
Japan, Germany and Italy, the Government, at a recent session
of the Cabinet Council, have decided on the Summary of the
Program for Economic Construction Embracing Japan, "Manchukuo"
and China, with a view to coping with new developments in the
situation, and intend hereafter to carry out national policies
consistently in conformity with that fundamental national pur-
pose. The objective of the new policy is to realize unprece-
dented progress in the Greater East Asiatic sphere of common
prosperity based upon a comprehensive economic development of
the three countries, in accordance with a new outlook on the
economic order.
The world economic system of free trade resting upon the
basic assumption that people and nations everywhere can freely
exchange goods is in the process of disintegration. Accordingly,
the Japanese system is also called upon to reorganize on a
new basis discarding the old structure which is dependent on
the old order. In this reorganization, however, Japanese
economy must be made a higher, broader and stronger structure,
capable

=== Page 158 === (confidence 1.00)
capable of leading the people of East Asia to improve their liv-
ing conditions and to find their respective places in the new
world order. What is meant here by "higher" is that the power
of life in possession of our nation must be made to create a
higher productivity; by "broader" is meant that the sphere of
mutual economic dependence must be extended from that of Japan,
"Manchukuo" and China to that of Greater East Asia and must be
firmly established, and by "stronger" is meant that impregnable
economic strength able to meet any situation must be maintained
by keeping the extent to which Japanese economy is dependent
upon other countries to a minimum. To this end sufferings
attendant on efforts of renovation must be overcome internally
and any pressures and threats from outside be repulsed, by
uniting all the forces and resources of the nation and by a
determined will. Thus a new East Asiatic economic order under
Japanese leadership must be established within about ten years.
In this East Asiatic order alone, the economic system not only
of "Manchukuo" and China but also of other countries of East
Asia can be expected to improve and develop to the highest
extent.
ESSENTIALS OF BASIC PRINCIPLES
For the purpose of realizing the task of constructing a
new East Asiatic order and thereby ensuring the lasting peace
of the world, the processes of the renovation of her internal
structure and of the enlargement and organization of her living
sphere must be brought into perfect unity. Her basic economic
policies must accordingly be established upon the organized
planning of the three following processes:
1. Perfection

=== Page 159 === (confidence 1.00)
1. Perfection of the reorganization of national
economy.
2. Organization and strengthening of Japanese-
"Manchukuo"-Chinese economy.
3. Enlarged organization of the East Asiatic sphere
of common prosperity.
BASIC LINES OF POLICY
1. The objective of the program for economic construction
embracing Japan, "Manchukuo" and China is to establish a
self-supporting, self-sufficient economic structure within
about ten years, in order to strengthen the position of East
Asia in the world economy by accelerating the construction of
the East Asiatic sphere of common prosperity.
2. Guiding principles on which Jupan is to undertake
the economic construction program embracing the three countries
lie in the promotion of the general welfare in an ideal state
of co-existence and common rosperity through the unitary
cooperation of the three countries, in accordance with the
high and broad spirit of "Hakko Itiu".
3. In order to expedite the economic construction Japan
will endeavor to uplift the morale of the nation, renovate the
internal structure, augment national strength while rendering
assistance to the economic reconstruction of "Manchukuo" and
China. For this purpose she will strive for the promotion of
science and technique and undertake the development of pioneer
industries.
4. "Manchukuo", being in an inseparable relationship with
Japan

=== Page 160 === (confidence 1.00)
Japan, is expected to swiftly perfect and develop important
basic industries.
5. China is expected to develop her resources and re-
construct her economic system in collaboration with Japan and
"Manchukuo"; in particular, progress in communications and
transport services, smooth exchange of goods, and development
of essential industries and resources are called for. Thus
she is expected to contribute to the establishment of the East
Asiatic sphere of common prosperity.
6. With a view to adjusting and accelerating the or-
ganized planning of economic construction embracing Japan,
"Manchukuo" and China the administrative machinery relating
thereto shall be speedily perfected.
PRACTICAL POLICIES
In view of the fact that Japan, "Manchukuo" and China
constitute the backbone of the East Asiatic sphere of common
prosperity, having thereby the duty to regulate economic rela-
tions on the basis of their closest union, the government
has decided on essential policies in respect to spheres of
industrial activity, labor, finance and banking, exchange,
and communications and transport covering the three countries.
Spheres of Industrial Activity
In determining spheres of industrial activity, the condi-
tions peculiar to the three countries and their respective
phases of economic development should be considered, thereby
effecting

=== Page 161 === (confidence 1.00)
effecting decisions from the comprehensive point of view and
treating them as an organic whole.
Viewed in that light, Japan should hereafter strive for
the promotion of the highest type of precision industry as
well as mechanical industry while endeavoring to develop basic
industries, such as the heavy, chemical and mining industries.
"Manchukuo" is expected to make an epoch-making advance
in the mining industry and in electrical undertakings. For
the development of her heavy and chemical industries Japan is
prepared to offer necessary assistance.
China is expected to develop her mining and salt indus-
tries and to undertake large-scale production of raw materials.
Since her natural conditions afford much room for the develop-
ment of heavy and chemical industries, progress along that
line is also expected hereafter.
The development of light industries on the Continent
must be greatly promoted. In this connection, it is necessary
that Japan should readjust her light industries, particularly
the fiber and miscellaneous industries. Their removal to
the Continent must be seriously considered.
With regard to agriculture in Japan, measures will be
taken for the improvement of various systems relating to land,
renovation of farm management, betterment and security of the
living of farmers and ensuring of the staple food of the
people as well as the maintenance of definite numbers in the
farm population. Measures will also be taken, in this connec-
-tion

=== Page 162 === (confidence 0.99)
tion, for the steady development of fishery enterprises and
for the rationalization, utilization and conservation of
forest resources.
With regard to agriculture in Manchuria, in view of the
position of that area as a base for the supply of foodstuffs
and fodder for Japan, "Manchukuo" and China, and as the
source of supply to the world of special agricultural pro-
ducts, the increase to the fullest extent of agricultural
production is expected. For the purpose of this development,
the migration of Japanese agricultural settlers will be
facilitated. In respect to agriculture in Cnina, efforts
for the assurance of the staple food of the nation must be
made. The increase of the production of cotton and other
staples is also urgent.
Labor
In order to hold a predominant position in world economy,
the place of labor and technique will become increasingly
important. For this purpose, their structure in Japan must be
radically revised. Moreover, labor power available in each
of the East Asiatic countries and regions must be enabled to
make its contribution to the improvement of the whole labor
force, thereby maintaining the superiority of the East Asia
sphere in worla economy. With this in view Japan intends to
perfect a new structure in labor and technique, endeavoring to
train and build up the bodies and minds of workers, to provide
a thoroughgoing scientific education, to heighten the producti-
-vity

=== Page 163 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
vity of labor, and to train technicians and skilled workers.
Thus the economic construction of "Manchukuo" and China will
be accelerated and assisted as required by the two countries;
"Manchukuo" and China will be supplied with technicians and
skilled workers capable and necessary for their industrial
development and economic construction. Needless to say, the
two countries themselves have to train those types of men in
view of the importance of technique. It is further required
of "Manchukuo" that she should plan not merely for the migration
to and settlement in the country of labors from North China
but for the establishment of measures for the self-sufficiency
of labor as well as the improvement of its management, in
particular, in the mining industry.
Finance and Banking
With a view to expediting the construction of national
defence economy, the function of finance and banking also should
be made to conform to the national purpose. In other words,
it should so function that the materials required by the
State may be ensured in quality as well as in quantity. In
order to make possible the execution of an industrial pro-
gram embracing Japan, "Manchukuo" and China, the distribution
of capital must be determined according to an organized plan
and a system equal to that task be set up. In that connection,
there must be perfected a financial mechanism facilitating the
storing of staple goods, in keeping with the progress of
teohnique, along with the creation of spheres of industrial
activity

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.98)
一8-
activity, and with changes in industrial equipment. Capital
required for the three countries must of course be supplied by
their own accumulation. To this end measures must be taken
by the three countries for the increase and efficient utuliza-
tion of such accumulation. With regard to capital necessary
for the development of essential industries, Japan is prepared
to give assistance in its supply. And the relationship of
mutual assistance in international settlements should be
established between the three countries as their economic re-
lations become increasingly closer.
Exchange
In respect to exchange in the coming new world order, the
principle of commercial trade of the past must be revised to
a considerable extent; there must emerge the principle of
productive trade, which enables each country, region and
economic sphere to supply to others goods required by them in
exchange for the goods necessary for its own planned production;
and which regulates trade between those countries and regions,
not to speak of that between Japan, "Manchukuo" and China,
in such a manner as will create a mutual, unitary relationship.
In so doing, a special agreement for payments between those
countries and regions will be required, with a view to
facilitating the closer flow of goods between them.
Communications and Transport
With the closer flow of goods between Japan, "Manchukuo"
and China as well as between the regions in the sphere of common
prospority

=== Page 165 === (confidence 0.99)
prosperity, systems of communications and transport between
the three countries must be perfected and operated by a com-
prehensive and organized plan, thereby ensuring the security
of the whole of the sphere. Accordingly, the connection
between land and sea transport services of the three countries
must be expedited; a large-scale increase in shipping, control
of and connection between air lines, as well as the perfection
and extension of electric communication facilities, must be
planned.

=== Page 166 === (confidence 0.98)

=== Page 168 === (confidence 0.98)

December 19 issue of the JAPAN TIMES AND ADVERTISER.
The article is based on a Domei news agency report from

program for North China in the agricultural sphere of

North China, according to the report, as has already

the production of raw cotton, wheat and miscellaneous
cereals such as millet, kaoliang and corn. The focal
point and primary objective of the assignment is the
freeing of the entire "Japan-Manchukuo-China bloc", from
dependence on the United States and other foreign countries
for raw cotton and whe at. It is explained that Japan in-
stead of as hitherto relying on American and European
countries for raw cotton for its textile industries will
look to the "East Asia Prosperity Sphere" for an adequate
supply of these materials. Although the article does not
mention the fact, it will also be recalled that under the
ten-year economic plan it is envisaged that the textile
industry is eventually to be removed from Japan to China,
particularly to North China, and accordingly in such an
eventuality under this program North China will only be
Aly defeurh
dependent upon itself for the raw materials for its textile
industry.
The report states that prior to the outbreak of
hostilities in China raw cotton production in North
China ranged from 4,500,000 to 5,000,000 piculs annually
(a picul

=== Page 169 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
(a picul equals 133-1/3 pounds). By 1950 it is planned
to expand the production to 10,000,000 piculs. The North
China Cotton Improvement Association, operating under the
instructions of the Government authorities is to organize
and direct this program.
With regard to the production of wheat in North China
the report states that the program calls for an increase
of 15,000,000 piculs within the next ten years. This amount
it is deemed will amply cover the deficit of wheat in that
area which in the past has had to be made up by imports of
from 7,500,000 to 10,000,000 piculs annually, bought from
Japan,Central China, North America and Australia. It is
further s tated in this respect that by this increase the
production in wheat in North China will be raised from
the present estimated figure of l32 million piculs for a
normal year to 147,000,000 piculs.
In the rest of the report there is submitted informa-
tion and statistics regarding the projected increase in the
production of the other cereals mentioned and plans for
agricultural land development in North China.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
February 25, 1941
CO:
Tokyo's despatch 5284, January 15, 1941
"East Asiatic Co-Prosperity Sphere".
In Contemporary Japan, January 1941, Kame-
kichi Takahashi attempts to explain and defend
the East Asiatic co-prosperity sphere on
historic and economic grounds.
He states that the Western powers, partio-
ularly Britain and the United States, have
retarded the economic development of the Far
East through their interests directed to the
development of the sources of raw materials in
which the powers are deficient, and by obstruct-
ing the development of industries which would
induce economic independence. The East Asiatio
co-prosperity sphere will emancipate this area
from its present dependent state.
The Embassy points out that although Japan
is expecting to follow the course of exploita-
tion which she condemns in the Western powers,
still the article will have a strong appeal to
the Japanese public who are eager to find a
foreign scapegoat to bear the onus for the
present ills of Japanese economy.

The Honorable

Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to submit the following digest of
an article appearing in the January, 194l, issue of
CONTEMPORARY JAPAN entitled "Economic Significance of
the East Asiatic Co-Prosperity Sphere" by Kamekichi
PS/DB
Takahashi, a Japanese writer on economic subjects. The
attempt to explain the Japanese concept of that often
repeated but little understood slogan, "East Asiatio
Co-Prosperity Sphere", and to defend it on historical
and
4.0

=== Page 174 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
and economic grounds appears to be of some interest.
As his primary thesis, the author maintains that
the Western Powers, particularly Great Britain and the
United States, have exerted economic and political pressure
to retard the economic development of the Far East and to
render its economy subservient to the needs of their own.
Western interests, it is stated, have been guided by this
principle in such major activities in this region as develop-
ment of natural resources, launching of industrial enter-
prises, negotiation of commercial treaties and customs agree-
ments, extension of communication facilities, application
of international agreements for the control of labor con-
ditions and the organization of the banking system. It
is alleged, without citing any concrete examples, that
those Powers have pursued a policy of encouraging the
production of such raw materials as are essential to their
needs, principally agricultural but including certain mater-
ials in which they are deficient, such as tungsten and tin,
and of intentionally obstructing the development of those
resources which would be conducive to independent industrial
progress in this region such as coal, iron and other mineral
resources. With, possibly, a measure of justification, it
is pointed out that Occidentals generally have viewed with
complacence the low-wage scales in effect in East Asiatic
countries but have nevertheless become extremely critical
of those same wage-scales when the affected industries
compete with similar Western industries.
To emerge
1

=== Page 175 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
To emerge from the present subordinate position in
world economy to which Western machinations have confined
it, East Asia must concentrate on the expansion of heavy
industries (notably engineering and chemical) and the per-
fection of industrial technique in order to be able to ex-
ploit its own natural resources in defiance of external
pressure and obstruction. The author points to the advance
in the dye industry and to the perfection of superior spin-
ning and weaving machines in Japan (relieving the country
of dependence upon second-rate spinning and weaving machines
formerly imported from Britain) as examples of the progress
already made in the desired direction of industrial inde-
pendence. Outside Japan proper, the development of water
power resources in Chosen, Taiwan, and Manchuria, and the
exploitation of deposits in Chosen and Manchuria of iron,
non-ferrous metals, light metals, gola, silver and coal are
enumerated as similarly encouraging beginnings.
Proceeding from the forthright accusation that "the
traditional policy of Great Britain and the United States
is aimed at vigorously thwarting the autonomous growth of
East Asiatic economy", the author concludes that "it is
essential at this stage of development of international
affeirs for it (East Asia) to establish itself as a clear-
cut unit in the economic structure of the world".
Deploring the past efforts of the Western interests
to "impede the organic union of the industry, natural
resources and markets of this region", the author maintains
that,

=== Page 176 === (confidence 0.99)
-4
that, while the attainment of the objeotives of the
"East Asiatic Co-Prosperity Sphere" will emancipate the
economy of this area from its present, dependent, semi-
colonial state and will necessarily involve great changes
in the international division of labor and in international
trade relations, the consequent rise in living standards of
East Asiatic peoples will, after a period of transition and
adjustment, open an enlarged market to the merchandise of
Western countries and thereby benefit Western economy in
the long run.
However fallacious may appear the premises upon which
this article is based and the reasoning by which its argu-
ment is developed, it is nevertheless quite certain that
this thesis has a strong appeal to numerous Japanese who
are, quite naturally, eager to seize upon a foreign scape-
goat to bear the onus for the present ills of Japanese
economy, warped as it is by the necessity of sacrificing
the everyday needs and comforts of the people to the demands
of an insatiable war machine.
As applied to Japan, the argument of Western oppression
is obviously untenable. Any lag in the progress of its heavy
industries, it should be frankly admitted, reflects not ex-
ternal restraints but the simple fact that Japan is a late-
comer in the community of industrial nations and, moreover,
is seriously handicapped by a deficiency within the country
of essential natural resources. While not granting the
validity, even as applied to the non-Japanese portion of
the

=== Page 177 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
the projected "sphere", of the charge of obstructive
exploitation levelled against Western interests, it
appears fairly obvious that Japan must follow precisely
the policy which it deplores in others in order to attain
the objectives of its projecteā bloc economy. How can
Japan, itself poor in natural resources, expect to fulfill
its role as industrial center and organizing head of the
bloc unless the manner of exploitation of natural resources
in the entire area is made to conform to the requirements
of Japanese inaustry? In view of its inperialistic recora,
moreover, it is difficult to imagine that Japan would be
averse to succeeding to the rights and interests of the
Western Powers, whose activities are so harshly condemned,
and even vastly extending the field of foreign (though
Japanese) exploitation to suit its purposes.
In the grandiose schemes for the creation of an
economic bloc to embrace the entire Far East, the author
appears to overlook the fact that, after coping with
the opposition to be expected from the Western interests
now being unceremoniously evicted, Japan must still win
the effective cooperation of the other peoples in the
Far East if its program is to succeed. It is evident
that, in spite of three and a half years of inconclusive
warfare on an unprecedentedly large front in China, the
Japanese are still unwilling to entertain any misgivings
concerning the whole-hearted cooperation, political and
economic

=== Page 178 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
economic, to be expected from its partners in the
projected "East Asiatic Co-Prosperity Sphere".
Respectfully yours,
710
Joseph C. Grew.

JAPAN: MATERIAL RESOURCES.
STAPLE FIBRE.
With the severe restrictions imposed on the
use of cotton and the manufacture of cotton cloth
for domestic consumption, the Japanese were forced
to use more and more 'staple fibre' in their
textiles. Staple fibre is publicized as the
"National Fibre", and it was intended to take the
place of cotton entirely in the Japanese home and
clothing.
However; even the production of stable fibre
has been badly affected by the scarcity of labour,
power and coal in Japan. As the following table
showing production from January 1939 to June, 1940
inclusive, plainly shows:

Production in March 1938 was 39,000,000 1bs;
output in January 1939 was a 40% decrease
At the beginning of 1939 there was a great
scarcity of pulp, with the result that even the
'national fibre' was striotly controlled in its
distribution. But from October because of the lack
of coal, etc., output, and distribution had to be
controlled again.
Staple fibre is still more or less in its
infant stage, and its quality can be greatly im-
proved. But there has been no time to do this
in Japan, so that all fabrics in which it is in-
cluded are very faulty and inferior. It has been
used principally as a substitute for cotton, but so
far it has not been so adaptable.
In the production of 1 ton of staple fibre,
the Japanese use:
1.2 tons of rayon pulp
.95 tons of caustic soda
1.5 tons of sulphuric acid
35 tons of carbon bisulfide
3
tons of other chemicals, and
8
tons of coal,which includes the
amount used for generating
electric power for the entire
process.

=== Page 182 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
Shoes with tops made of staple fibre have been
selling in Japan at Y.l0 a pair, but they have worn
very badly, the fibre weakening when it is wet.
The principal manufacturers of rayon are also
the principal manufacturers of staple fibre.

Report No. 180
100 - Political
Japan
105 - Spheres of Influence
30 October 1940
JAPANESE ECONOMIC ACTIVITIES IN THE SOUTH SEAS
AREA
Japan's Southward Advance Policy is making rapid
strides, and in typical Japanese fashion is being accomplished
by e combination of military economic steps designed to obtain
holdings and then to consolidate them. At the moment, Japan
is carrying on conversations with the French in Indo China
and the Dutch in the East Indies to obtain essential materials
in order to minimize the effects of further American-British
economic pressure. For all practical purposes the Japanese
can be said to control French Indo China economically and
strategically. The Dutch are proving harder to win over,
but since they cannot be sure of United States support and
Great Britain is now unable to give more than nominal assist-
ance, Japan will no doubt in the very near future obtain
important economic concessions from Batavia.
That Japan is preparing to consolidate and defend her
newly won gains, is shown by the fact that air bases have
been or are being established in Tongking,Cambodia, and the
Spratley Islands and permission to use the naval bases in Hanoi
and Kamranh Bay are being obtained from the French. It is
only & que stion of a few months before the Japanese will be
within easy striking distance of Malaya, Borneo, and Java.
Japanese investments in the South Sea area already
amount to the consideraple sum of ¥250,000,000. These consist
of interest in various mines, plantations, and lumber and
fishing enterprises. The foilowing is an outline of the
most important Japanese interests as published in an article
in the "Asahi Weekly" written by Soji Okada, one of Japan's
well known economists.
Petroleum: The production of petroleum in the Dutch
East Indies is so completely monopolized by European and
American capital that Japanese interests have almost no share
in its development. However, the Borneo Petroleum Company,
an affiliate of the Mitsui Bussan Kaisha, and the Nippon
Petroleum Company have been prospecting for oil on the
eastern coast of Dutch Borneo for the past ten years without
success thus far.
Tin: Two tin mines in British Malaya, owned by the Ishi-
hara Industry and Navigation Company, are not being operated et
the present time. The Mitsubishi Mining Company and the Nanyo
Kogyo Kaisha, however, are each operating a tin mine in Thailand.
Iron: Japenese capital enjoys & monopolistic position in
the production of iron in the South Sees inasmuch as the Western
Powers are not much interested in the exploitation of iron
deposits so remote from them. The most important source of
iron ore supply in this region to Japan's iron and steel
industry is British Malaya where the Ishihara Industry and
Navigation Co., the Nippon Mining Co., the South Seas Iron
Mining Co., and the Iizuka Iron Mining Co., have several
mines in Johor, Tringanu, and other provinces.
The Ishihera Industry and Navigation Co. also
operates an iron mine in Surigao, Mindanao, while the Nippon
Mining Co. has been exploiting a wolfranite mine on Palawan
since 1937.
Report No. 180-40

=== Page 187 === (confidence 0.97)
Report No. 180-40
/2/
Other sources of supply include mines in French
Indo-China and New Caledonia which are being operated with
Japanese capital.
The Nippon Mining Co. has an iron mine concession
in Dampier Land on the northwestern coast of Australia but
due to a strict ban by that government on ore exports in
July l938, no ore shipments have as yet been received in
Japan from the mine.
Nickel: The Taiyo Mining Company is engaged in nickel
mining in French New Caledonia and is reportedly making efforts
to buy a nickel mine in Celebes.
Bauxite: The Ishihara Industry and Navigation Company and
the Nissha Shokai each have a bauxite mine in Jehor.
Gold and Silver: The Trade Mining Company is exploiting
a gold and silver mine on Cebu Island, Philippines.
Mica: Japanese interests are operating one mica mine in
Celebes.
Copper: The Ishihara Industry and Navigation Company has
a copper mine concession in Sourakarta Province, Java, but it is
not being operated at the present time.
Rubber: The first Japanese rubber plantation was started
in l902 in British Malaya and since that time there has been a
steady growth. In l334, as a result of an investigation made
by the Overseas Ministry, it was determined that there were
36 Japane se rubber plantation firms and 178 Japanese-owned
private farms with a total leased area of 514,000 acres (of
which l22,o00 was actually planted) and an aggregete crude
rubber output of about 20,o00 tons. Most of the Japane se
leased plantations are in Malaya, Sumatra, Java, and Borneo and
those owned by corporations account for about 9ó per cent of
both leased acreage and output.
The leading corporations with their locations of

Hemp: The production of hemp by Japanese at Davao, Philip-
pines was started in 1907. At the present time, Japanese hemp
plantations in that region are on a minor scale with the excep-
tion of the Ohta Kogyo and the Furukawa Colonization Company
which have large interests. In addition to Philippine hemp,
the Nissan Agriculture and Forestry Company has transplanted
hemp to British Borneo and is now meeting with success in its
production.
Other crops cultivated by large Japanese firms in
territories owned by the United States, Great Britain, Nether-

GREATER EAST ASIA ECONOMIC POLICY
894.50/137
4 TEN YEAR PLAN
The Cabinet has made public an ambitious ten year
"Greater Rast Asia Economio" plan. Aocording to this announoed
plan, Japan's fundamental economio polioy is to
1. reorganize the national economy;
2. organize Japan-Manohukuo-China economy on a firm basis;
3. organize an East Asia sphere of common prosperity.
PS/JHS
Japan proposes to regulate industry, labor, finance
trade and communications In the three countries concerned.

7 November 1940
GREATER EAST ASIA ECONOMIC POLICY
A TEN YEAR PLAN
The Japanese Cabinet has made public the main lines of
the plans for the economic-political construction of Japan,
Manchukuo and China into an economic bloc. The following is
the official statement in regard to these plans:
"In order to attain Japan's mission of promoting
the permanent peace of the world by establishing a new
order in East Asia, the reform of the national structure
and the organization of the sphere of living on an
extended scale must be pushed forward collectively
and conjointly. Accordingly, Japan's fundamental
economic policy must be framed with the following
three objectives for attainment by stages:
"1. - Reorganization of the national economy.
"2. - Organization of the Japan-Manchoukuo-Chinese
economy on a firm basis.
"3. - Organization of the East Asian sphere of common
prosperity."
The fundamental policies to be follov:ed to attain the
above objectives are:
"1. - The ob jective of the establishment of the
Japan-Manchoukuo-Chinese economy is to establish within
the next ten years a state of self-sufficiency economy
among the se three countries, at the same time expediting
the creation of the East Asian sphere of common prosperity,
so that the position of East Asia in the world economy
may be strengthened.
"2. - Japan's guiding spirit in establishing the
Japan-Manchoukuo-Chinese economy is to promote the
common existence and prosperity and the general welfare
of the three countries through their close co-operation,
in consonance with the lofty principle of 'hakko ichiu'.
"3. - In order to expedite the establishment of the
Japan-Manchoukuo-Chinese economy, Japan will invigorate
the national spirit, effect reforms in all branches of
administration and increase the national resources, while
aiding the economic construction of Manchoukuo and China.
To this end, she will try to develop science and technical
skill and help forward various industries.
"4. - Japan expects Manchoukuo, which is inseparably
related to Japan, to adjust and develop its important
key industries speedily.
"5. - Japan expects China to develop its natural
resources and rehabilitate its economy with the co-opera-
tion of Japan and Manchoukuo. It is particularly desired
that China will direct special attention to the develop-
ment of the means of transport and communication, to the
provision of better facilities for the exchange of goods,
and to the exploitation of natural resources so as to
contribute to the establishment of the East Asian sphere
of common prosperity.
"6. - In order to accelerate the adjustment of
collective economic construction plans covering Japan,
Report No. 189-40

=== Page 191 === (confidence 0.99)
/2/
eport No. 189-40
Manchoukuo and China, the necessary measures will be
taken speedily.
"Japan's guiding policy in establishing the
Greater East Asian sphere of common prosperity with
the crection of a new world order in view has entered
on & new stcge through the conclusion of the tripartite
pact among Germany, Italy and herself. In order to meet
the new situation, the Covernment has fixed the outlines
of Japan-Manchoukuo-Chinese economic construction, forming
part of the fundamental economic policy, at a recent
Cabinet meeting, and future policy is to be shaped and
carried out along these lines.
"The object of this policy is to compass the
vigorous development of the Greater East Asian sphere
of common prosperity, which is to be founded on the
collective advance of Japane se, Manchoukuoan and Chinese
economy in conformity with the conception of a new
economic order. The world economy based on free trade,
which aims at the free exchenge of goods between nations
is crumbling before our eyes together with the old order.
Japen's economy must be free from the old structure de-
pendent on the old order so that it may be reorganized on
a new basis. By this reorganization, Japanese economy
must be mede higher, wider and stronger, thereby elevat-
ing the standard of living for all East Asien peoples and
enabling them to have the ir proper place. To make it higher
means to give higher productivity to the national vitality;
to make it wider means to extend the sphere of mutuel
economic dependence among Japan, Manchoukuo and China to
Greater Ecst Asia so as to establish a strong sphere of
mutual prosperity covering Greater East Asia; cnd to
make it stronger, mccns to establish the national economy
so firmly by reducing the degree of dependence on outside
Powers to the minimum th:t it can stond the strain of any
situation that may crise. In order to mcke the Jcpenese
economy higher, wider nd stronger in this wεy, the en-
tire resources of the nation must be put together and
while overcoming at home all hardships cattendant on
reforms by dint of & strong national will, all kinds of
pressure and menace from other Powers must be resisted
stoutly, so thct the new East Asicn economic order in
which Jupcn exercises the directive influence may be
completed within the next ten years. Under this new
order only can all East Asian countries including Menchou-
kuo and China achieve a brillicnt economic development.
"In view of the fact that Japen, Manchoukuo and
China are the ma in factors in the East Asian sphere of
common prosperity, they have the duty of regulting their
economic rel tions in close collaboration.From this
point of view, the Government hcs fixed the fundamental
measures to be taken in the field of industry, lcbour,
finance, trade and communicctions of the three countries.
"In the realm of industry the physical conditions of
the se countries and the stages of their economic develop-
ment must be taken into careful consideration, if they
ure to work as a true organic body. In Japan, it is
necessary to secure the rapid development of precision
industry of a high degree &s well as the me chinery industry,
besides helping forward vigorously key industries suoh
as the heavy, the chemical εnd mining industries. In
Manchoukuo, the mining and the electric industries
Report No. 189-40

=== Page 192 === (confidence 0.99)
/3/
eport No. 189-40
must be promoted. Japan will also give Manchoukuo
the necessery help in developing heavy and the chemical
industries. In China, mining and the salt manufacturing
must be pushed forward and the mass production of
industrial materials must be encouraged. There is also
room for the development of heavy and the chemical indus-
tries in that country, and these must be helped forward.
Further, it is considered necessary to encourage light
industry in the continent. Light industry in Japan -
the fabric industry and miscellaneous industry especially -
must be reduced gradually and be transferred to the
continent.
"With regard to Japanese agriculture, various
system governing land must be reformed and the methods
of agricultural management be improved so as to stabilize
and improve agricultural life and ensure an adequate
supply of the staple foodstuffs. It is also proposed
that the country should maintain the farming population
on an adequate level.
"Regarding Manchoukuoan agriculture, in view of the
fact that Manchoukuo is an important source of supply of
foodstuffs and fodder for the three countries and is also
the supplier of agricultural products of special kinds
to the world, Japan expects that country to increase
agricultural production considerably. For the agricul-
tural develcpment of Manchoukuo, Japan will encourage the
settlement of Japane se farmers there.
"As regards Chinese agriculture, while endeavouring
to ensure the production of staple foodstuffs for the
Chinese people, it is considered necessary to increase
the production of raw cotton and other special products.
"In order to secure a high position in the world
economy, the labour and technical skill of the nation are
of special importance, and from this point of view the
structure of labour and technical skill in this country
must be fundamentally revised. Similarly, if the East
Asian sphere of common prosperity is to maintain a high
position in world economy, it is important that steps
should be taken to see that the labour resources of each
country or in each region are so utilized as to contribute
to the progress of the whole.
"In Japan, the new structure will be applied to labour
and technical skill, labourers will be trained physically
and mentally, scientific education will be promoted, labour
productivity will be increased and experts and mechanics
will be trained. Japan will also give the necessary aid
to the economic construction of Manchoukuo and China.
"Manchoukuo and China will be supplied with eble
experts and technicicns necessary for their industrial
development and economic revival, but at the same time
these countries must also endeavour to train good tech-
nicians themselves.
"While admitting labourers from North China for settle-
ment in the country, Manchoukuo must also endeavour to in-
crease the supply of labour in its own country. It is
Report No. 189-40

particularly necessary for Manchcukuo to reform
measures for the control of labourers in the mining
industry.
"On finance, trade end communications also, the
outlines of the plans laid down have been made public."

Tokyo's despatch no. 5431, March 11,

Embassy
The aspa reports: There is scant
evidence of the successful application of the
ambitious plans for renovating the Japanese
economic structure through planned national
economy; wide divergence of opinion in Govern-
ment and private circles developed when the
general principles for the new economic struc-
ture were being formulated; industrialists took
exception to drastic restraints upon private
management originally contemplated and a con-
cession was made regarding the mandatory pro-
visions for the creation of a supreme central
organization to control all industries.
The Government plan provides "economic
bodies" composed of industralists engaged in
similar activities, operating through directors
appointed by the industrialists and approved
by the Government. The diree s will ex-
ercise guiding and supervisory functions over
individual enterprises. rrivate management
is the general rule with State management re-
stricted to cases of special nece sity. Of-
ficial fixing of prices and payment of divi-
dands are provided and "the Government shall
guide and supervise economic bodies"
and "shall cooperate with the Imperial Rule
Assistance Association to promote progress
in organizing economic
bodies".

=== Page 205 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
bodies".
A recent article attempting to set
forth the methods by which the plan is ex-
pected to operate states that if, for example,
i0 million tons of iron is required by the
State during the year, the Government will
place an order with the Directors of the
hiron manufacturing group" who if they
accept the order will be responsible to the
State for its completion. The Directors will
allot the order to members of the Group who
are responsible to the Directors. The
allocation of orders is to be predicated upon
the coordinated, joint management of member
producers.
The Embassy finds puzzling the lack of
achievement during the three months since
the Government announced the"Principles of
the New Economic Structuré' but sees no prospect
for a rapid and thorough tranformation of the
economic system. The Embassy also observes
that it is unlikely that the'New Economic
Structure can be discarded because of the
great publicity which it has received and
therefore a Government gesture towards im-
plementing the program is expected.

Sir:
APR 18 1941
With reference to the Embassy's despatch no. 4928,
FILED
PS/JHS
August 30, 1940, and later despatches discussing the
TME
formulation and ramifications of a new political concept
10/29/45
in Japan, known as the "New Structure", which has served
Fumimoro
OOP
the present Konoye Government as the central rallying
slogan for extensive internal reorganization, I have
the honor to outline the development and results of
efforts

=== Page 207 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
efforts to apply "new structure" principles to the re-
organization of Japanese economy.
The entire "new structure" movement, including its
economic aspects, derives its impetus from conditions of
increasing stringency induced by prolonged hostilities in
China. In attempting to meet this situation with his
loudly proclaimed "new structure" it can at least be sus-
pected that Prince Konoye has once more resorted to the
favorite Japanese device of formulating vague, grandiose
and frequently fanciful panaceas intended to gloss over
unpleasant realities. As might have been expected, the
ambitious plans for renovating the economic structure,
through the introduction of a system of planned national
economy, have encountered numerous obstacles and to date
there is scant evidence of their successful application.
Following a period of prolonged consideration, the
Government agreed to and announced on December 7, 1940,
a set of "Principles of the New Economic Structure", a
1/
translation of which is enclosed. While a detailed dis-
cussion of the conversations and negotiations leading up
to the issuance of these principles appears unnecessary,
it is interesting to observe that wide divergences of
opinion, both in Government and private circles, became
apparent, during October and November, 1940, while these
principles were being formulated, in regard to the form
which the new economic structure should take. A preliminary
draft plan drawn up by the Cabinet Planning Board formed
the

=== Page 208 === (confidence 0.99)
-3~
the working basis for negotiation. Exception was
taken by industrial interests, principally to the
drastic restraints upon private management of in-
dustry contemplated in the original draft plan.
According to the Japanese press, s trong pressure was
brought to bear on the Govemment by KANSAI indus-
trialists (in conferences with Mr. Kobayashi, Minister
of Commerce and Industry), the Japan Economic Federa-
tion (meetings between the president of that organiza-
tion, Baron Goh, and the Prime Minister were reported)
and the Japan Chamber of Commerce.
Various and conflicting rumors concerning the
nature of the new economic structure were circulated.
One of these, alleging that the Government planned in-
corporating into the new economic structure communistic
ideas, involving the denial of the right of private
ownership of capital, evoked strong denials from the
Prime Minister. On November 30 announcements were made
by the Army and Navy Ministers of their support of the
Government's plans for internal reform and condemning false
rumors to the contrary as schemes designed to overthrow
the Cabinet.
The chief concession made to the industrialists is
understood to have been the elimination from the original
plan of a mandatory provision calling for the creation
of a supreme central organ to control and co-ordinate all
industries. The plan as announced by the Government leaves
the question of the creation of such a supreme organ to
future decision upon the basis of future necessity.
The

=== Page 209 === (confidence 0.97)
-4-
The Government plan envisages the organization of
"economic bodies" within certain (not designated) major
industries, such "bodies" to be composed of industrialists
engaged in similar activities or dealing in s imilar com-
modities. These "economic bodies", operating through direct-
ors, appointed by the interested industrialists and approved
by the Government, will exercise certain guiding and super-
visory functions over individual enterprises. The Govern-
ment's plan emphasizes that private management is to be
allowed to function as freely as possible but it provides
for the possibility of replacing private management by
State control in special cases. The plan provides for
official price fixing and for restrictive limits on dividend
payments. The "economic bodies" are designed to function
as advisers to the Government in their special fields and
to assist in drafting and executing important economic
policies.
An excerpt is quoted below from an article by Yohiji
Minobe, who holds the combined post of section chief of the
N8F
Price Bureau of the Commerce and Industry Ministry and of
the Cabinet Planning Board. This article appeared in the
February 1941 issue of CONTEMPORARY JAPAN, and the exeerpt
quoted professes to give an example of the manner in which
the Government's plan is expected to operate:
"To

=== Page 210 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
"To give an example of the future system, suppose
the State requires l0 million tons of iron in the
course of the ourrent year. The Government will
place an order with the directors of the iron manu-
facturing group for the production of such an
amount on condition of supplying them with the
necessary raw materials and labour, after seeking
their views. In case they accept the order, the
directors will be responsible to the State for
the production of the amount of iron specified
and will allot production to the member producers
of their group. The production of any specific
amount of goods, however, will be predicated upon
the coordinated efficiency of the limited production
facilities, raw materials, labour, motive power and
fuel available. Consequently, the quotas of produc-
tion to be allotted to member producers are also to
be predicated upon the coordinated, joint management
of enterprises on the part of the member producers,
who are responsible to their directors for the
production of their allotted quotas. The directors
in turn are responsible to the State for the produc-
tion of their accepted amount of iron on the basis of
designated volumes of raw materials and labour. They
are quite free as to the manner of activity to which
they will put their member producers, who on their
part are responsible only for the production of their
accepted quotas and take their own initiative in
deciding upon the method or manner of production.
Inasmuch, however, as there exist inseparable con-
nections between production and the quality and price
of products, acceptance of allotted quotas will in-
variably be accompanied by consideration of quality
and the price of products."
The professed object of this economic program is the
fullest and most efficient utilization of all resouroes of
the State. The co-ordinating activities of the "economic
bodies", are expected to insure that total national re-
sources will be effectively exploited, and retention of the
principle of private management (though subject to the prin-
ciples of "priority of public interest and service to the
State") is expected to insure maximum industrial efficiency.
The

=== Page 211 === (confidence 0.99)
-6
The degree to which the plan will, in operation, result in
a separation of management and capital, is receiving the
anxious attention of industrial circles.
It should be noted that in its present form, the
"Principles of the New Economic Structure" are not law
and have no binding legal force. They could be given legal
validity by an Imperial Ordinance issued under the authoriza-
tion contained in the "National General Mobilization law".*
Such an ordinance has not yet been issued, however, and any
action taken by industry so far to comply with the Govern-
ment's program can only be described as voluntary.
Reports have appeared that the iron and steel industry,
also the coal and cement industries, are taking steps to
form "economic bodies" as contemplated in the organization
of the new economic structure. Definite information,
however, concerning concrete developments in this direction
are lacking.
The lack of achievement, or even indications that
serious efforts were being made to realize the objectives
of the "New Economic Struoture" during the three months
that have elapsed since the Government announced its
"Principles" is somewhat puzzling. Such apparent inactivity
cannot in any case be interpreted as an optimistic sign
and it remains to be seen whether the Government, now
freed from its preoccupations with Diet matters, will
proceed with its economic plans. All indications point
to
* Embassy's despatch no. 2871, April 11, 1938.

=== Page 212 === (confidence 0.96)
-7-
to the conclusion that no rapid and thorough trans-
formation of the economic system of Japan, along the
lines of the Government's "Principles", is in prospect.
On the other hand, in view of the great publicity which
accompanied the drafting of economic policies, it is un-
likely that the "Economic New Structure" can be discarded
outright, and therefore at least some gesture by the Govern-
ment toward implementing its program may be anticipated.
In this connection, it should be observed that the
"Law for the Revision of the National General Mobilization
Law", conferring materially increased wartime economic
powers on the Government, has been enacted by the present
session of the Diet and officially promulgated. A separate
treatment of this important measure will be reserved for a
later despatch.
Respectfully yours,
Nord 6. Frun

Joseph C. Grew.

=== Page 213 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure No. 1 to despatoh
No. 5431 dated March 11, 1941,
from the Embassy at Tokyo.
Translation of the "Complete Text of Principles of the
New Economic Struoture" as published in the Asahi Shimbun
of December 8, 1940.
I. Basic Policy
In order to establish a self-sufficient prosperity
sphere with Japan, Manchukuo and China as one unit and
embracing Greater East Asia, to secure an autonomous de-
fense economy based upon the resources of that sphere,
and to realize through the cooperation of the Government
and the people a co-ordinated planned economy centered
around the key industries as the means of coping with the
stringency of the current situation, of contributing to
the consunmation of the national defense structure by per-
fecting armaments and of securing the stabilization of
the national livelihood and the permanent prosperity of
the national economy, it is necessary
(1) to establish an industrial structure whereby
enterprises representing an organic entity of capital,
management and labor shall constitute an element in the
organization of the national economy under a co-ordinated
state plan, and shall be entrusted to autonomous control
under the initiative and responsibility of industrialists
who, through the exercise of maximum efficiency, will in-
orease industrial productive power; and
(2) in accordance with the principles of priority of
public interest and of service to the State, and in order
to evoke the full national strength for the attainment of
the national objective of powerful national defense, to
direot the economy of the people and to make it into an
organio unit through the organization of economio bodies.
In

=== Page 214 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
In regard to the execution of these principles, it is
necessary, in view of the present situation, to follow
the proper order of execution by according priority to
urgent matters and to avoid lowering industrial productivity,
disrupting distribution and causing uneasiness among the
people. Further, in order to conform to the arrangements
for this structure, reorganization of the affected admin-
istrative organs and of their functions shall be effected.
II. Industrial Structure
With a view to maintaining and strengthening productiv-
ity, the industrial structure shall be established with
the individual enterprises retaining initiative and re-
sponsibility in management, subject to the objectives of
the State.
1. Private management of enterprises shall be the
general rule, and management by the State or by national
policy companies shall be limited to cases of special
necessity.
2. There shall be applied to enterprises, according
to their nature and in conformity with fixed standards,
necessary restrictions regarding establishment, etc.
3. According to their nature and in conformity with
fixed standards, enterprises may be divided or merged from
the standpoints of produotion plans or technique.
4. Medium and small-scale enterprises shall be main-
tained and fostered. When such maintenance proves diffi-
cult, however, adjustments and mergers may be carried out
independently and the smooth transformation will be as-
sisted.
5. In

=== Page 215 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
5.. In order to contribute to the strengthening of
national production and to realize its permanent develop-
ment, enterprises shall be subject to appropriate guidance
and control.
(a). In fixing official prices for principal
commodities allowance shall be made for a fair
profit, using average production costs as a basis.
(b). The realization of speculative and monopolistic
profits, detrimental to the maintenance of the
national economic order shall be prevented but
fair industrial profits will be allowed. An
increase in profits will be allowed to persons who
have specially contributed to increasing national
production.
(c). Appropriate restrictions shall be applied to
the distribution of industrial profits but amounts
in excess of the restriction limit shall be held in
reserve in Government bonds or in some other form.
A means of disposing thereof shall be determined
under fixed conditions after the lapse of a fixed
period of time.
(a). Special means will be devised to reward persons
who have contributed to the strengthening of
national production through inventions or dis-
coveries.
(e). The technical arts shall be opened to the public
and, in order to accelerate progress in those
arts, rewards will be given to persons who excel
therein.
(r).

=== Page 216 === (confidence 1.00)
-4-
(f). In order to facilitate the renovation of
industrial facilities and other measures to reinforce
the foundations of industry, amortization shall be
strengthened.
(g). Assistance shall be rendered to the expansion
and development of enterprises in the order of and with
emphasis upon their contribution to national production
strength.
6. Separate consideration will be given to the indus-
trial structure for management of agricultural and fishery
enterprises.
III. Economic Bodies.
(1) Organization of economic bodies.
(a) The units for major divisions of industry
shall be enterprises and associations. Economio
bodies shall be organized on a functional or a
commodity basis to comprise industrialists engaged
in similar industries or dealing in similar com-
modities. The fundamental conditions therefor are
as follows:
(1) Economic bodies shall be special
juridical persons.
(2) Economic bodies shall operate
under the guidance of directors recommended
by the industrialists and approved by the
Government.
(b) Other industries shall be organized
into economic bodies when necessary in accordance
with the preceding clause and on a funotional or
regional basis.
(o) Enterprises outside of Japan Proper shall
be organized into economic bodies in the various
regions in accordance with the provisions of
the

=== Page 217 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
the preceding clause. Appropriate measures
shall be devised, however, for national regu-
lation if there is special need for control
being unified with that in Japan Proper.
(a) In connection with the organization
of economic bodies, the following points must
be specially heeded:
(1) The establishment of economic
bodies shall be in the order of the most
important to the le ss necessary.
(2) Separate consideration shall be
given to enterprises having special im-
portance from the military standpoint.
(3) A supreme economic organization
charged with supervision of all industry
shall be established when the necessity
for it is recognized.
2. Functions of economic bodies.
(a) Functions of major industrial economic
bodies are as follows:
(1) As co-operative organs of the
Government, they shall co-operate wi th the
Government in framing important policies
and shall assume responsibility for draft-
ing and executing plans for their enforce-
ment. Moreover, in case of necessity, they
shall place their views before the Govern-
ment.
(2) In the enforcement of plms as set
forth in the preceding clause, they shall
undertake to guide subsidiary economic
bodies and enterprises under their jurisdic-
tion.
(3) As necessary, they shall investi-
gate the actual results of management in
production, distribution, etc. Also they
shall supervise subsidiary economic bodies
in the task of inspecting the quality of
goods produced.
(4) To contribute to the development
of industry, mutual aid from the joint ac-
count or by some other means shall be ex-
tended to saorificed industries.
(b)

=== Page 218 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
(b) Functions of other bodies correspond
in general to the foregoing.
3. Government supervision and relation to the Imperial
Rule Assistance Association.
(a) The Government shall guide and super-
vise economic bodies. Arrangements for economic
bodies as well as their management shall be
made as autonomous as possible with guid ance
and supervision limited to general principles.
(b) The Government shall co-operate with
the Imperial Rule Assistance Association to
promote progress in organizing economic bodies.
4. Separate consideration shall be given to
the organization of economic bodies for agricultu-
ral, forestry and fishery industries.

=== Page 222 === (confidence 0.99)
Enclosure no. 1 to despatch
no. 5437 dated March 12, 1941,
from the Embassy at Tokyo..
Conversation
March 6, 1941.
The American Ambassador, Mr. Grew,
with the Australian Minister, Sir John G.
Latham.
Sir John Latham called today, at his request, and
we talked for an hour on various subjects. He said that
since coming to Japan he had been in touch with many
Japanese business men, especially the wool merchants
who had recently given a dinner for him of forty or
fifty merchants from various parts of the country. In
talking with these men individually and analyzing the
results Sir John said that he was greatly impressed by
the present deplorable condition of the economic life
of the country. In this connection he geve me a few
concrete illustrations:
1. A Japanese who had recently returned
home from abroad had been unable to find a house
anywhere and Sir John asked him why he did not
build a modest residence, especially as he was
convinced that plenty of lumber existed in Japan.
The Japanese replied that this was true but that
it would nevertheless be utterly impossible for
him to build a house because, first, he could not
get transportation for the lumber, second, he
could not get nails for the construction and
third, he could not get labor for the building..
2. A Japanese had recently imported a large
machine for turning out smaller tool-making machines
but the big machine was lying idle. When Sir John
inquired why the owner did not put it to work the
latter replied that to be set up the machine required
a base of some twenty tons of cement but that after
months of effort the owner had been able to assemble
only eight tons of cement up to the present.
3. A Japanese company had received a large
order for many gross of p encils and had tried
to assemble the various supplies required for
their manufacture, namely the wood, the graphite,
the rubber, and the brass bands for holding the
rubber in place. Each category of these supplies
had to be obtained by filing separate applications
and owing to the delays encountered it had been
utterly impossible to assemble the material for
manufacturing the pencils.
4.

=== Page 223 === (confidence 0.99)
-2-
4. The present demand for glass is im-
possible to fill for the reason that some of
the prime necessities for manufacture are
lacking, especially a product which comes from
Eritrea, so that manufacture has been reduced
to a minimum. Sir John said that the same lack
of essential commodities was restricting produc-
tion all along the line.
Sir John said that Mr. Ohashi, the Vice Foreign
Minister, had recently summoned him and in a threatening
manner had said that Japan required a large quantity of
certain grades of Australian wool and that unless Australia
should meet these requirements the Japanese Government
for its part would restrict Japanese exports to Australia.
Sir John said that he had laughed and had pointed out to
Mr. Ohashi that any such restriction by Japan would be
impossible in view of the primary importance of Japan's
export trade and the urgent need of acquiring foreign ex-
change. He said that Australia is now at war and that a
great amount of wool is needed not only to supply the
steadily increasing Australian army but to supply British
needs as well, but that after these needs had been met
Australia would do its best in its own interest to maintain
its wool market in Japan, although Japan could not necessarily
count on receiving the particular grades of wool which it
desired. Sir John said that at the end of the conversation
Mr. Ohashi's tone had completely changed and that he was
appealing to him for cooperation, instead of demanding.
Sir John said that whenever a Japanese embarked on
the immigration theme and criticized Australia for shutting
the door to Japanese immigration he merely pointed out that
Japan

=== Page 224 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
Japan had likewise closed the door to Chinese and Korean
laborers simply because the latter would work for less
pay and could exist on a lower standard of living than
the Japanese workman. Sir John said that precisely the
same considerations had brought about the immigration
restrictions in Australia and in the United States and
he said that if his hearers would apply a little logic they
would realize the reasonableness of the Australian and
American position. He said that his contacts seldom had
any reply to this argument.
The conversation then turned to the Gallup and other
polls which Sir John considered to be exceedingly dangerous
because they allegedly recorded what was in fact the un-
informed and uneducated views or snap judgments of the
masses and that they exerted an influence out of all pro-
portion to their real value. He said that the same considera-
tions applied to the petitions signed by thousands of names
which were often circulated and that when he had been in
the Australian Parliament he had succeeded in discrediting
these petitions by pointing out the manner in whioh they
were drawn up, especially by certain interests which cir-
culated blank telegraph forms for telegrams to be sent free
of charge to the legislators. He said that at one time the
school children used to stage competitions to see who could
sign the greatest number of such petitions and that in one
school a small girl had won with a record of thirty such
signatures, it being her habit to sign a different name to
each petition. I took issue with Sir John on the value of
the Gallup and other polls and expressed the opinion from
oareful

=== Page 225 === (confidence 0.98)
-4-
careful personal observation that the American public
is today extraordinarily well informed, especially by
radio and the press, with regard to what is going on in
the world and that their votes in these polls are believed
to rest on carefully formed judgments based on wide informa-
tion and incisive thought and that this applied to all
classes of society.
J.C.G.

being greatest in skilled workers. In April, 1939, the

M.I.D. report no. 10,348. March 5, 1941,
Tokyo.
A revision of the National Mobilization
aw hes been effected which, according to the
report, "will give the Government complete
control over all national economic and finan-
oial matters in all their many ramifications
as well as over many of the activities of the
individual". The report lists each article
revised and in commenting on the various
individual revisions the report states, e.g.,
"gives the Government power over investments
and equipment of businesses concerned with
mobilization enterprises", "provides for com-
plete Government control over almost any form
of activity", "has power over the proprietors
of all forms of business", "extends the Gov-
ernment authority more widely in the matter
of price regulation", "owners of like enter-
prises may be required to combine their
operations", "the new clause is a punitive
one which sets forth the classes of violators
According to the report the new law
should be "very nearly the maximum effort
toward directing the activities of the country
along lines designed to cope with the present
emergency".
Translation of the law enclosed.

oonsidered its many very drastio features. As time went
EQ. 11652. Sec. 3E md SD)
on however, it became olear that there were loopholes in

its complete and thorough application, and there arose a

sented itselr in the current session of the Diet which

control, to say nothing of the shadow of the Imperial
Rule ssistance Association whioh was hanging over its

drastic governmental regulation, were hustled through
the Diet with soaroely a oritioal comment other than a
few discussions as to the severity of certain of the
punitive olauses.

 In the interests of facility in making comparisons
betweem the old law and the law in iss revised form, a
translatiem of whioh is attached here, the various ehanges
are indisated below.
Artiele S. The term "looal public bodies" is ohanged to
read "local publie bodies or those appointed by the Govern-
mont".
Comment. The change is reported as having been designed to
place more power in the hands of designated officials with
a view to obtaining more prompt cooperation than has been
found possible when the regulation or the enterprises con-
cerned was carried out by local organizations.
Artigle 6. The words "assumption of office or retirement"
are added after the word "dismissal", and the words "wages
and other labor terms" are replaced by the words "wages,
saleries, and other working conditions".
Comment. The changes are designed to give greater control
over labor and the conditions under whieh it works.
Artiole 8. The words "general mobilization materials" are
changed to read "goods".
Comment. Under the former law there was considerable fric-
tion over what constituted "mobilization materials". The
substitution of the word "goods" removes any opportunity
for further disoussion by placing all materials of any
nature under the provisions of the law.
Artiele l0. The words "use or expropriate" are replaced by
the words "use or expropriate, or cause a person who admin-
isters general mobilization business to use or expropriate".
Comment. The extension of the using or expropriating powers
to persons engaged in mobilization business is in keeping
with the similar ohange in Artiole 5 and tends greatly to
speed up decisions as to use of goods and materials of all
kinds.
Artiele ll. The words "use of capital" are changed to read
"operation of their funds, or undertaking or guaranteeing
monetary obligations".
Cemment. This change merely extends a step further the con-
trol whieh the Government already exeroises over all financial
operations.
Artiele 13. Clsuse 3. The words "use or expropriate land
houses, and other working facilities necessary for general
mobilization enterprises" are changed to read "manage, use

SIR:
RESEA
COMMERCIAL AFFAIRS
I have the honor to report that the Consulate
JUN 12 1941
DIVISION OF
has been informed by the Mitsubishi Economic Research
Bureau that it has discontinued the publication of its
Monthly Circular with the April 194l issue. A refund
on the Consulate's subscription to this journal has
been made by the publishers.
The Monthly Circular, which is an "Analytical and
Statistical Survey of Economic Conditions in Japan" is
of course well known to the Department. In the April
PS/HK
VIS  CIAL
SEITS
issue, the number of which is 21o, indicating that the
JUN 27 1941
FILED
JUN 2 6 1941
jourtal has been issued for more than 17 years, there
appeared a statement as follows:
"Tokyo, 1st April, 1941
DEPART
"Dear Sirs,
Owing to the recent enactment of the
National Defence Seourity Law, our "Monthly
Ciroular", whioh specializes in the statistical
and analytical survey of Japanese economic
conditions, will be placed under grave restric-
tions. We have therefore decided to suspend
9x1
publication

=== Page 241 === (confidence 0.97)
-2-
publication temporarily, and you will not
receive our Ciroular subsequent to April,
1941 (No. 210).
On this occasion we wish to express our
gratitude for your valuable support in the
past, and beg to assure you that the un-
expired subscription will be refunded as
soon as possible.
In conolusion, we wish to express our
willingness to supply you with any economic
data, the publication of which does not
transgress against the laws of this country.
We remain, dear Sirs,
Yours very truly,
MITSUBISHI ECONOMIC RESEARCH BUREAU"
Many students of economic conditions in Japan made
full use of the Monthly Circular, and although at times
doubt is cast on the reliability of Japanese statistics
("The authors' picture of Japan's prosperity and
economic invulnerability is probably too rosy, due to
their excessive respect for not always reliable Japanese
statistics"--from a review in FOREIGN AFFAIRS, April
1941, of "The Industrialization of Japan and Manchukuo,
1930-1940", Schumpeter et al.), the Monthly Circular
will nevertheless will be missed by those who are
interested in economic studies of Japan, especially
at a time when there is so much debate as to the economio
strength of Japan as a factor in international relations.
Respectfully yours,
AmeelSobohin
Samuel Sokobin

. Mr. Hareld THompsos, an American eitisen, returned te San Framcisco
TamarA Manu" July 4, 194l. For the past five years he has been in
axgeets to leave San Francisco for Washington on July 5, 1941.
2. Mr. THompsoN states that the Japanese, generally speaking, are very
"fed up" en the Germans; they have come to Japan and are fercing their way into
emsry eorner. The German business man is giving the Japanese real competition
in Chima; as the Japanese take new territory the Germans are right there to start
in delmg business. This used to be an exclusive and lucrative field for the
imts high Japanese gevernment offices. They are active in authoritative positions
in the following:
Division of Censorship
Home Departmont (Police)
Educational Bureau
Through these key pesitions they are spreading their propaganda. Por awhile
the Germans had rough going to get into top positions with the armed forces,
but have finally succeeded and are now active with their plans both in the Army
and Navy.
3. Mr. THOMPSON feels that until Matsuoka is changed as Foreign Minister,
Japan's attitude towards us will not change very much, as Natsueka is very pro-
German and is responsible for the German people coming into Japan in such large
numbers.
4. Japan has numerous secrets that are always being diseussed among the
pepulase, two examples are as follows: Huw much oil have we? Are we building
many ships eto.? Mr. THOMPSON'S answer to the question of oil is that he has
reasen te believe Japan has a large reserve supply of oil, enough for at least
ene years full fighting strength. In shipbuilding it is very difficult to
ebtain much information but it is his belief that & large battleship has just
recemtly been commissioned although he could not give confirmation of this.
The gevernment in many ways is doing & rather blundering job; as an example
this year there will be a plentiful amount of rice, where at one time during the
year rice was being rationed and now the condition exists where there probably
will be some rice wasted due to the poor estimates of the government department
handling this activity. Cenditions are not as bad as most people are lead to
believe with regards to foed, clothing, etc., which are obtainable in reason-
able quamtities. In the interior however there are some parts where this is
not entirely true; reeently a group of about l00,000 young boys 15 to 16 years
old were brought from the interior to Tokyo to see the Emperor and parade and
beseme enthused to fight for Japan. These boys were poorly dressed, appeared
underfed, and many had no shoes. The morale of the Japanese people is not good,
they are very bitter about the endless China war and are very concerned about

=== Page 248 === (confidence 0.99)
DECLASSIFTED
NTIAL
11652, S0. ) nd 
EOT: ECONOMIC CONDITIONS IN JAPAN
Mr. Julio Jesus Passas Coelho, a British subject residing at 77
Sagiyama Naka-ku, Yokohama, Japan, who arrived on the KAMAKURA MARU June 28,
1941, for the purpose of conducting business with R. J. Roesling & Company,
243 California Street, San Francisco, gave the following information regarding
economic conditions in Japan:
FOOD:
A quota system was inaugurated in Japan for distributing food
in November, 1940. Food cards were issued for rice, flour, sugar,
and other foodstuffs, but by poor management the distributing of the
food and the cards became confused and a great deal of dissension
arose among the poor class of people. This confusion has been
alleviated by the import of German technicians who now have complete
control of food, clothing and gasoline tickets. At the present time
eighteen kilos of rice per month per person are permitted,- approxi-
mately two pounds per day for a family of four persons, and four
pounds of sugar per month. A serious shortage of tin has necessi-
tated the packing of most food in glass containers, and at the present
time empty beer bottles are being employed as containers for food.
A shortage, at present, of potatces is very acute, and in Yokohama
and Nagoya every available space is being used for the planting of
potatoes and other vegetables.
CLOTHING: Woolens, cottons and felts are very difficult to purchase, and
a process of using a pulp mixed with cotton is being employed as a
substitute for pure cotton goods. The price of leather shoes has
made them nearly prohibited. Shoes that would normally retail for
eighteen yen are now retailing for 50 yen, and tanned sharkskins are
being used as a substitute material in the construction of shoes.
GASOLINE: Cards permitting the bearer one gallon of gasoline per day are
being issued at present, but approximately 75 per cent of the private
cars and motor busses that are operating are using charcoal fuel and
a mixture of a vegetable and fish oil is being used for lubrication.
IRON AND METALS: Throughout all of Japan a concerted effort is being made
to reclaim iron and metals. The iron manhole covers are being
replaced by wooden covers, and a house to house campaign by ex-service
men is in effect. This campaign is to obtain copper, brass, aluminum
and lead. There are some aluminum utensils to be purchased in large
department stores, but their prices are prohibited.
POLICE: The Secret Police and Gendarmes have become very strict and corrupt
at the same time. The many restrictions placed by the government upon
the merchants have made it nearly impossible to conduct business
without infringing onsome law, and as a result it is a common practice
among the merchants to close the probing eyes of the police with
financial gifts.

-2-

=== Page 249 === (confidence 0.97)
CONFIDENTIAL
JECT ECONOMIC CONDITIONS IN JAPAN
BuSINESS: Since June 1, 1941, all business accounts must be kept in Japanese.
The shortage of staple commodities has forced many merchants to close
their stores except for two or three hours a day because they lack
sufficient merchandise to stock on their shelves. There is also a
bitter complaint and considerable amount of hardship resulting from
evacuation of American and English citizens from Japan. These people
were the means of bringing American dollars and English sterling into
Japan in considerable amounts and their leaving has closed many clubs
and caused considerable hardship to those Japanese citizens who were
employed as servants. As a result of these depressing conditions
small merchants and farmers have become very much disillusioned and
disheartened by the government's inability to bring the Sino-Japanese
war to a conclusion and there is an increasing distrust in the German
Japanese pact which is believed to be blocking Japan from making
millions out of the present conflict in Europe, and there is an
increasing reference to the fact that during the last world war huge
fortunes were made, but during this present conflict misunderstandings
and pacts have caused the economy of Japan to grow constantly worse.
The gossip among Japanese business men is to the effect that Japan is
playing her last card with the United States and it is believed that
Prince Konoye's new convenient sicknesses are a sign that he and
Mr. Matsuoka are about to resign, and that a new government will be
formed of men who have a more friendly attitude for the government of
the United States.
