JAPAN
Economic Estimate
1. General Economic Charecteristics.
a. Natural Resources. The Japanese islends constitute an area
ebout equal to that of Celifornis, but only about one-seventh of this
area is arable. iost of the arable land is under cultivation; future
increeses in totel production of agricultural commodities, therefore,
will be negligible under present agricultural methods.
The scercity of raw materials in Japan is the greatest
hendicap to economic independence. Until recent months, food would
not have been included, but at present, the basic commodity of food
is rationed along with other raw materials. Although man-power has
been cheeper than machinery, man-power must have good food in order to
function at maximum efficiency. Since the beginning of the Chinese
"Incident", the intelligent disposition of man-power, as one of the
natural resources, hes become a factor of importence in the industrial
life of the nation. Such industries as silk and tee growing, cotton
spinning and handiwork heve required individual work rather than that
of the machine. Industry requires raw materials; men-power requires
food - in this statement is conteined the major problems confronting
Japan at the present time to a greater extent than these problems
confront any other country.
Even though raw materiels were available, Japenese industry
has not yet reeched the stage where the country would be self-sustaining
in the manufecture of ll necessary supplies for a serious war. For
example, the lack of fecilities, for refining in unlimited quantities
of aviation gesoline, or the manufecture of high quality mechine tools,
are illustrative of menufecturing needs with which Japan would be con-
fronted. Ye cen see vividly how difficult Japan's problem is when we
realize that nearly one-half of her people live on farms. Of these,
one in every three families is dependent upon silk culture for their
livelihood. In meny instences, the income from silk means the differ-
ence between bare sufficiency end dire privstion. Since the United
States consumes over 85% of Japan's silk, the weekness in Japan's
economy is obvious. If Japan were deprived of this export trade in
silk, 18,000,000 persons would be reduced to privation.
Politically, the State of Manchoukuo is not & part of the
Jepanese Empire. Actually, Japen exercises control over this country
and in time of war the resources of Manchoukuo will be evaileble to
Japen like any other part of the Jepanese Empire. This fact hes been
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 269 === (confidence 0.99)
2
CONFIDENTIAL
Japan
Economic
taken into consideretion, and Japan's economic position in war has
been estimated on the basis of the inclusion of Menchoukuo as an in-
tegrel part of a wertime Japanese Empire.
In the same way, but not to the seme extent, we must consider
the occupied provinces of China in the seme category es Manchoukuo;
they are not at present & part of the Japanese Empire in the political
sense, but we can depend on the fact that the resources of a portion of
the territory now occupied by Jepan in China would be aveilable to the
former country in case hostilities should be expanded in the Orient.
Economically, the Japanese Empire may be regarded as consist-
ing of three distinct parts: (1) Jepen proper, including the main islends
of Honshu, Shikoku, Kyushu, and Hokkaido; (2) the remeinder of the
Empire, including Manchoukuo and thet part of occupied China over which
she could continue to exercise control in an extended war; and (3) terri-
tories over which she has nominal but not actuel control such as Indo-
China and Thailend from which she expects considereble essistance in the
forms of increased importetion of raw materials. Assuming uninterrupted
trade between these three portions of her economic empire, the Jepenese
still could not consider themselves independent economicelly of the rest
of the world.
b. Dependence on Foreign Trede. Japan's present economic stabilit:
depends to a considerable extent on cheep labor, low stenderds of living,
the export of rew silk, silk and cotton goods, and the importetion of
rew cotton and other essentiel raw meterisls which are needed both for
home consumption, the manufacture of wer meterials, and on the menufacture
of erticles for exnort. The "vicious circle" runs ebout es follows:
Imports of rew materials ere cut down to minimum essentiels in order to
conserve a rapidly shrinking balance in internetionel exchenge; when
imports of rew materiels ere cut down, exportsble goods are decreesed;
when exportable goods are decreased, belance in foreign credits is de-
creased thet much more. The acceptance by the Japanese people of in-
ternel menipulstion and regulation by the government prevents totel
financiel collepse. Deficiency of raw materials snd the continuing
need of foreign markets for the disposal of her manufactured goods make
Japan dependent on foreign trede in war as well as in peace. Any power
or combinetion of powers which can obstruct this trede completely can
seriously cripple Japan.
2. National Production Capacity.
An astimate of the productive capacity of the Japenese
Empire includes a consideration of the resources of Hanchoukuo,
portions of China which heve been occupied and other portions of the
Orient from which Japan can receive raw materials. In time of war
with countries other than China, Japen will utilize to the maximum
the resources of the portions of the Asiatic continent over which she
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 270 === (confidence 0.99)
3
CONFIDENTIAL
Japen
Economic
has direct or indirect control. The following discussion will indi-
cete roughly the extent to which Japan cen become independent of sources
of supply outside of the Asiatic continent.
e. Agriculture
(1) Distribution of Lands. The area of lands under tillage
in Japen in 1937 was 6,098,000 Cho, approximetely 15 million acres, or
15.8% of the total area of the country. This percentege is less then
helf of that for Germany or Italy. The rest of the country is too
mountainous for farming purposes end is thus utilized for forestry
(62.7% of the total aren), and for miscelleneous other purposes, 21.5%.
The proportion of areble land in other parts of the Empire
is higher than in Japan proper becsuse of the more fevorable conditions
of geogrephy and topography.
The totel number of farming households in 1937 wes 5,574,879
or 42% of the entire number of households. There has been a gradual
decline in this ratio, the percentage in 1903 being 64.7% and that for
1924 being 50.38%. The aree tilled per farming family in 1937 was 1.09
Cho or epproximetely 2.7 acres.
The ratio between free-holders end tenents in 1937 wes 31%
to 27% while 42% is represented by perties which are free-holders as
well es tenants. The trend over the pest twenty years would indicate
that the perceit of free-holders is diminishing while combined free-
holders end tenants ere increasing.
fore then helf of the entire areble lend is used for the
production of rice end more then helf of the value of ferm produce is
represented by this cereel. While formerly Japen proper was self-supply-
ing in the cereal, the demand hes so expended es to necessitate imports
from ebroad - from other perts of the empire es well es other countries.
At present Jepen imports between 20 end 25 percent of her needs from
Chosen end Taiwan alone.
b. Fishing. On account of her unique position es & maritime
country, Japan's fishing industry hes developed until todey Japan occupi.
the first position in the world in the output of aquatic products. In
1938 these amounted to ¥754,758,284, and engaged as many as 1,442,713
persons in the various branches of the fishing industry. Of this number
1,143,618, were males end the rest females. From two to three percent
of all the people in Japen proper, Korea, Formosa, end Karefuto ere en-
gaged in fishing.
The total number of boats and vessels engaged in fishing at
the end of 1938 was over 356,482 of which 288,327 were without engines,
while 68,155 were with engines. It is obvious that if Japan should be-
come involved in war with a strong navel power, her fishing fleet would
be extremely vulnerable.
A classification of the fishing industry of Japan would in-
clude coastal fishing, pelegic (deep sea), whaling, trewling, fisheries
in Russian waters, as well as floating canneries in Kamchatke and in
the North Atlantic.

=== Page 271 === (confidence 0.99)
CONFIDENTIAL
4
Japen
Economic
Japan is on a strong export besis in fishing products, the
exports of which represent an important source of foreign exchange.
These exports have been increasing in recent years because of super-
vision end stimulation by the Japanese Government.
Exports of fishing products fron Japan mey be divided into
two groups: (1) Those which are generelly taken by countries within
the Jepanese sphere of influence; and (2) those which find markets in
Western countries, perticulerly the United Stetes and the United King-
dom, as well as in British end Dutch Asiatic possessions. The first
group consists lergely of boiled, dried, and salted fish, whereas the
second consists predominantly of "fresh" (frozen) and canned fish.
The United Stetes is by fer the most important merket for
Japanese exports of "fresh" (frozen) fish and shellfish. These ex-
ports consist almost exclusively of frozen swordfish, frozen tune, and
seed oysters, for a 5-year average ennuel tota1 of $786,000.
The United States renks second es a merket for Jepanese
exports of εll canned fish and shellfish combined, having taken in
recent years, in terms of velue, from 15 to 30 percent of the totel.
Since t!e outbreek of the European wer in 1939, Jepen is finding it
difficult to dispose of the canned fish normally taken by the United
Eingdom - formerly the principel merket of these products.
The United States is the principal market for Japanese
canned crab meat, imports of which have averaged 44 perceat of our im-
ports of all fishing products from Jepan in the last 5 years, and amounte
to $2,945,000. Restriction of imports by the United States would in-
flict serious economic injury to Jepen, end could destroy the entire
Jepanese crab cenning industry. Jepenese operete canneries on Soviet
soil.
The United Stetes is also the most importeat merket for canned
tuna, importing epproximetely $1,230,000 worth. Jepen is not likely
to find a ready market for her canned tuna should imports into the
United States be prohibited.
Thus it cen be seen that imports by the United Stetes of
various products of the Japanese fishing industry smount to more then
$5,000,C00 annua11y.
Large quentities of fish end fish-liver oils ere rendered in
Jepanese shore end floeting fectories, end sbout equally large quantities
of whale oil are produced. Wheles taken in Japanese snd Korean waters

c. Supply of Other Foodstuffs. In the 1930's Jepan proper, with
the help of its colonies, Chosen end Taiwan, prectically echieved self-
sufficiency in food requirements. Jepan depends upon imports for 35
percent of its millet and 65 percent of its corn, but the consumption
of these two products is so smell thet they play little part in the
Japanese diet. Japan is self-sufficient in barley and buckwheat.
In the pest few years wheat production in Jepan increesed
to a point where it is not only sufficient for domestic requirements,
but also leaves a small net exportable surplus. Japan imports e con-
sidereble volume of wheat (6,624 tons in 1938) which is principally
of types different from those produced domestically. Part of this
wheat blended with domestic wheat is exported in the form of flour.
In recent years (since 1938) the volume of such exports in terms of
wheat has exceeded that of imported wheat.
Food self-sufficiency has not been echieved in Japan at
the expense of under-consumption. The rise in the per cepite consump-
tion of grains, leguminous crops, vegetebles end fish over a long period
of years bears this out.
Wherein, then, is the food shortege concerning which there
have been so meny reports recently? The answer cen be found in severel
conditions three of which would be (1) the high quality end quentity of
food sent to the Japanese armed forces in China, nd (2) poor crops
of rice in Chosen in 1939-40 and in Jepan proper in 1940-41, and (3)
deterioretion and subsequent fsilure of their trensportation system,
both lend and sea. /See page 44 under "Trensportation"/. Hence,
Jepanese rice imports from Burma, Thailand, French Indo-China end China
ere estimated at over 4 billion pounds es egeinst en average of over
100 million pounds normelly supplied from these sources. In other words
Japan cen provide an adequate amount of food if there is no failure in
eny one basic source, but if there are poor crops in any one grain,
then the shortage is felt in all.
Under normel circumstances, a serious food problem could
arise in Japan only if she were denied access to Menchurian soybeans,
Chosen's rice, and Teiwan's rice end sugar. In case of crop failure,
a serious food problem could arise if Japen were deprived of rice from
Burme, Thailand, and French Indo-China.
Reduced to caloric value, the food consumed by e Japanese
comperes well with that of other countries. Based upon European
standards the food the Japanese consume reveals glering deficiencies
largely because vegetable, rether than animel, food predominates.
From the point of view of Oriental stendards, however, Jepanese diet
is perheps the best bεlanced.

Cattle and buffalo hides are grouped together in Japanese

(6) Raw Silk and Silk Vaste. Japanese rew silk exports to
the United States produce about 12 perceit of the total
foreign exchange obteined from exports of all products from Japen.
Approximately 2,000,000 out of 5,500,000 farms engege in sericultural

or part of their income from the silk industry.
In the five years 1935 to 1938, 84 percent of all Jepanese
raw silk exports were to the United States. Conditions during 1940,
however presented a sharp contrast to the high prices and strong
foreign demand which prevailed during the previous years. In January
1940, prices for one grade dropped from ¥ 2,410 per bale to ¥ 1,650
per bale. After this abrupt decline, prices moved slowly downward
during the first six months until they reached a low level of : 1,350
per bale, the price at which Government purchases were made in order
to stebilize quotations. The Japanese Government has found it necessary
to give extensive financial support to silk farmers. The existing
depression in the raw silk industry is because of (1) diminished demands
of Americen importers and, (2) government distribution end price control
which curteiled domestic purcheses.

(e) Cinchone Berk end Quinine. The supply of anti-

supplying about helf of the remaining ten percent. Part of the

factory et Bendoeng, Java, the largest in the world, and the rest
is exported or held in stock.
Japan imported from the Netherlands' Indies 3,450 pounds
(gross weight) of quinine or crude quinine in 1937; in 1938, the
amouat was 63,034 pounds; in 1939, 75,525 pounds were imported.
If the United States end Great Britain could control

British India, and the Netherlands' Indies by negotiation, purchase
of stocks, or by force, Japan would be seriously affected by lack
of these strategic materials.

pounds in 1939. 0f these amounts, Japan imported 53,557,238 pounds

used since 1937.
Thus it can be seen that unless all other sources of the
meterial cen be cut off, it would not be profitable for the United

(c) Camphor. Japan, including Formose, has a world
monopoly of the production and distribution of natural cemphor.
Export of raw cemphor to the United St&tes in 1936 emounted to
2,226,000 pounds; in 1937, the emount was 851,000 pounds; in 1938
it was 627,O00 pounds. The development of synthetic camphor by the
Du Pont company hes reduced the requirements of the United States
for natural camphor. Although the synthetic product cannot be
produced as cheaply es natural camphor cen be imported, the Du Pont
company has a production capacity sufficient to supply 80 percent of
United Ststes'consumption and could in a few months expend its
capacity to supply the full requirements.
The prohibition of imports of natural cemphor from Jepan
thus to shut off foreign exchange amounting to 450 to $650,000
annually might be recommended.

(i) Fertilizers. Chemical fertilizers and their raw
materials are compounds of nitrogen, phosphorus, or potassium. Such
materials may contein one or more of the three essentiel elements in
such e form thet, applied to the soil, they become available to plant
life. The elements are not interchangeable, and their chemical com-
pounds find important application not only in agriculture but in the
arts end industries. Nitrogen compounds, for example, are the besis
of modern militery explosives.
Notwithstending a large production of fertilizers, Japan
is on an import besis with respect to the principal fertilizers,
such as ammonium sulfate, phosphate rock, potash selts end sodium
nitrate.
All of the supply of ammonium sulphate, a nitrogen com-
pound, in the possession of a nation may be used as a fertilizer in
time of peace. Under wer conditions, militery considerations determine
the quantity of ammonium sulnhate available, although the commodity
es such is not used extensively in explosives or other war materials.
Ammonium sulphete is a combinetion of emmonia and sulfuric acid, two
fundamentel materials in modern militery explosives manufecture. It
is the demend for these basic materials that restricts the supply of
enmonium sulphate for the needs of agriculture in wer-time.
For a variety of reasons, Japanese producers have not been
able to attain self-sufficiency in ammonium sulphate. Jepan can
hardly displace foreign sources of supply with increesed domestic
production; if she restricts consumption, a decline in the rice crop
will be the result.
Jepan rormelly consumes anproximately 1,000,000 metric tons
of phosphete rock. Despite intensive efforts to exploit the phosphate
deposits, Jepen hes been able to provide a maximum of only one-third
of its annual requirements. She has obtained from the United States
end countries under British control approximetely 65 percent of her
annual consumption of phosphete rock. The emount roduced in Jepan
will, however, take care of ell her needs except fertilizers. Any
curtailment of the consumption of phosphate rock would be reflected
almost entirely in the reduction of the production of superphosphate,
which in turn would result in a decreese in agricultural production.
A new source of supply of phosphete rock hes been opened up
by Japan's entrance into Tongking in Indo-China.

(3) Coel and Coke. (a) Coal. Coal is one of the very
essential commodities of great military end commerciel value and hes
a number of derivitives such as coal-tar, dyes, benzene, picric acid
and T.N.T.
The Jepanese Empire, exclusive of Manchoukuo and North
China, possesses but limited coal resources, elthough 42,495,000
metric tons was produced in 1938. This coal was mined on the three
islands of Kyushu, Honshu and Hokkaido. The coel fields of Kyushu
and Hokkaido are the most extensive and valuable in Japan. The center
of coal production is in Northern Kyushu, which yields about 60 percent
of the total output of Japan. Of the coal fields in Northern Kyushu,
the Chikuko coal field is the largest end yields about 72 percent of the
coal from Kyushu, while the famous Miike coal field ranks next both in
respect to its coel reserves end its output.
There are unlimited coal supplies at the present time (april,
1941) for Japan in the Fushun mine of Manchoukuo. The lack of trans-
portation facilities, however, has curtailed to a considerable extent
the adventages of access to the Fushun mine.
In the long run Japan will probebly have to rely on Manchuria
for its future coal supplies, despite the Government subsidies for
increesed output and for opening new veins in Japan.
North Manchuria fields are to a great extent virgin fields,
and therefore await definite openings. But production of coal, in
Manchuria in 1939, as the result of one mine's record of 10,000 tons
a day, switched attention from South Maichuria to North Menchuria. And
lest year Japanese capital established the Mitsuyama Tanko K.K.
(itsuyama Coal lline Co. Ltd.) with a capital of F 100,O00,O00, to
exploit these North Manchurian fields. The total tonnege of coal
reserves in Manchuria, including these North Manchuria fields, hes been
estimated to run as high es 20,000,000,000 tons.
(b) Coke. Perhaps the most important factor in the
Japanese iron industry is the matter of obtaining adequate supplies
of metallurgical coke for the making of which Japanese reserves of a
suitable type of coal are small. Just what part of all imported coal
is coking quelity is unknown, but it is certainly much the smaller pert.
The imported coking coal comes largely from the Kaiping mines in North
Chine operated by the Kailan Mining Administration, an Anglo-Belgian
interest at present under the control of the British. This Chinese
coal, when mixed with Jepanese coal in a ratio of one to four, produces
a satisfactory metallurgical coke.
The requirements of coal at the Jepsnese iron foundries after
years of steady growth in 1936 reached 3,423,000 metric tons for coking
purposes end 2,560,000 tons for other purposes, the total being
5,983,000 metric tons. While the requirements for the latter purposes
are fully met from domestic sources the supply of coking material must
be sought from external sources to a considerable extent. Native coal
is generally unfit for this purpose beceuse of abundance of ashes and
volatile matter, a serious handicep which the domestic industry hes
always had to fece in the course of its development. However, by an
improved process of dressing and treetment of netive coal by thet of
mixing as described above end by special adeptation of the coking oven,

and other technical points, the domestic foundries are now self-
supplying in coke.
The island of Karafuto is rich in coal deposits, although

of this island contains large quantities of coel and many of the

(c) Production. The total production of coal in Japan is
now in excess of 40,000,000 tons per year, with imports in the neighbor-

The mining of coal in Karafuto has steadily increased from
415 tons in 1912 to 251,000 tons in 1925 to 2,536,000 tons in 1937.
(4) Iron and Steel. World Production. Of the 162,000,000
tons of steel produced in 1938, the United States supplied only 18 per-
cent (about 29,000,000 tons) whereas the United States had supplied
about 35 percent (about 73,250,000 tons) of the 211,000,000 tons pro-
duced in 1937. Of the total amount produced in 1936, the United States
produced 49,572,000 tons; France, 33,300,000 tons; Germany, 7,340,000
tons; Great Britain, 12,900,000 tons; Sweden, 11,250,000 tons; India,
2,570,000 tons while Japan (including Chosen) produced less than
1,000,000 tons.
Iron Ores. The iron ore deposits in Japan proper and Korea
are estimeted at approximately 80 million and 10 million metric tons
respectively, the aggregate total being about 90 million metric tons.
Of these deposits, however, those at present in operation on a commer-
cial basis are less than a dozen in Japan and some half a dozen in
Korea. The output from these sources was reported in 1936 to have
grown to approximately 1,250,000 metric tons, although this was only
one-third of the requirements which reached aproximately 5,078,000
tons for the year in question.
It is also important to note that in addition to the above
deposits there are in Japan resources of iron sand, computed to be in
thousands of millions of tons, and in Korea extensive deposits of low
grade ore. The internal sources of iron sand on which the swordsmiths
and iron manufacture of the country depended in old times are at
present availed of only in a few special lines and more recently by a
special enterprise operated by a patented process which has not as yet
been fully tested. The low grade ores in Korea are magnesite, the
estimated deposits being between 4 and 5 million metric tons of about
50% grade and some 100 million tons above 40% grade, and 300 million
tons above 30% grade. The desulphurized pyrite at the chemical fer-
tilizer plants, amounting to upward of 1, 751,000 metric tons in 1936
alone is also receiving an increasing amount of attention as a poten-
tial source of raw material for iron mills.

Scrap Iron. A heavy consumption of scrap iron is charac-
teristic of iron and steel manufacture in Jepan. This is explained
by the preponderance of open hearth furneces for which scrap is used
in high percentages beceuse of economic reasons. The use of scrap
which declined to a considerable extent in the years 1929-31 agein
began to increase in 1932 until en impressive total of 3,337,000
metric tons was recorded for the year 1936, a two-fold increase in a
period of 7 years. This trend hss been oonsiderably accelerated
under the stimulus of the present expensionist policy followed by the
heavy industries.
O1 an average the use of screp in all foundries is to the
extent of 58s as ageinst 42, of pig.
With regard to the supply of scrap, however, no accurate
figures are aveilable. The total amount taken for 1934 was estimated
at 2,500,000 tons, of which something like 500,000 tons were supplied
from internal sources end about es many tons of steel scrap were
produced at the domestic steel mills, while 140,000 tons were imported.
Of the above quantity approximately 150,000 tons were directly used
as raw material at rolling mills for the manufecture of small size
steel products.
The external sources of scrap supply are distributed almost
all over the world, the most important being the United States of
America, British India, the etherlands ast Indies, Australia, China,
lMenchoukuo, the first named country in 1936 accounting for nearly 68%
of the total imports. It is estimated that imported scrap in 1936
exceeded 60% of the total consumption in the country, although there
are in evidence trends to increase the domestic production of pig iron
for the steel mills.
Of late there has been a tendency to replace scrap with ore
in the manufacture of pig iron. Vhereas scren cost about ¥ 80 per
metric ton in 1939, ore can be purchased at about ¥ 20. As less than
two tons of ore are necessery to produce e ton of steel, price primarily
caused the shift from scrap to ore. This change is being impeded some-
what by the lack of an adequate supply of good ore and by the greater
supplies of coel which are required. In addition, the Jepan Iron and
Steel ienufacturing Company is about the only organization able to make
this radicel change to an ore policy, as the majority of other companies
rely on open-hearth furnaces, which must use scrap iron. But the
tendency has been started, and it is one the industry is likely to
continue in the future as the supplies of richer ores are tapped in
Chine and ignchuria.

is the oldest, the contrect being concluded in 1899. The scope of
external supply hes since been extended accompanying the growth of

The dependence of the steel industry on foreign supplies
to the extent indicated, is admittedly a weakness in the whole
structure of the iron and steel industry in Jspan. The difficulties
of transportation, the scarcity of labor in exploiting the supply
sources abroad and shutting off some of those sources by the war are
making a difficult problem much more serious. If Jepan is able to
improve her position in regard to iron and steel, it will be due to
the fact that she has been able to develop to their full capacity
the mining end metallurgicel resources of Manchoukuo. Even by this
measure, Japan could hardly expect to reduce her imports of iron ore
much below 50 percent.
Iron and Steel Industry. Operating conditions were anything
but fevorable in the Japanese iron and steel industry during 1940, and
the volume of production was undoubtedly below the level of the previous
yeer. Fectors responsible for the reduced output included acute power
end fuel shortages during the first quarter of l940, and to a lesser
degree, during the closing months of the year; a sharp decline in the
volume of screp iron imports during the greater part of the year as a
result of the scarcity of foreign exchange, end the virtual cessation
of imports after the introduction of the licensing system by the United
States on October 15; the poorer quality of scrap eveilable; and re-
duced arrivels of pig iron and iron ore from ritish Indie and British
Melaya as a result of a dearth of shipping facilities snd export res-
trictions.
Produders of iron and steel endeavored to obtain Government
permission to reise prices to more remunerative levels in order to
compensate for the higher production costs end declining profit rates,
but such action was strenuously onposed by the military authorities.
After becoming e netionel politicel issue, a compromise wes finelly
reeched when the Government agreed to compensate the members of the
industry for the higher costs of imported raw materials by the granting
of official subsidies. These were distributed through the Japan Iron
and Steel Raw Materials Controlling ompeny which was established in
July, 1940, under the authority of the Hational Peneral Mobilization
Act.
By far the greatest problem facing the industry at the end
of the year was that of finding a solution to the problem creeted by
the licensing of scrap iron shipments from the United States. Despite
the optimistic comments of leaders of the industry and various
officials, the shortage of scrap iron is expected to curteil dras-
tically steel mill operations during the coming year. The Government
ordered a reduction in the quantity of scran iron for consumption in
flet furnaces from 47% to 4z), εnd all produation quotas for steel
ingots and semi-furnished goods were reduced by 5, during the last
quarter in order to conserve existing stocks. Since the supply of

In 1938, the last year for which fairly accurate figures
can be compiled, Japan obtained about 6,000,000 metric tons of
ferrous materials. About 22 percent of this represented ore produced
in the Japanese Empiro, 30 percent vas ore imported almost exclusively
from the Philippine Islands and British Ialaya, 33 percent was scrap
steel imported chiefly from the United States, and 13 percent was pig
iron imported from India, chiefly, and the United States. The other
two percent represents semi-finished products imported mainly from the
United States. How long Japan can continue full scale production
without scrap iron from the United States remains to be seen.
Exports of iron ore from British IIalaya and the Philippine
Islands to Japan have continued; those from the Philippine Islands
to Japan have increased (April, 1941).
The reserve or hold-over supply of ferrous materials in
Japan is estimated to be about 6,500,000 tons; in other vords,
sufficient to enable her to continue producing steel for eleven
months, exolusive of ferrous materials she is continuing to import.
Therefore, elimination of the imports from the Philippine
Islands and British Malaya would be the most effective additional
measure which could be taken for reducing Japan's production of
steel.

Although the increase in production has been noteworthy,
there has been an equally noteworthy increase in domestic consumption.
In 1935 consumption was estimated to be around 11,000 tons. In 1937
it was 20,000 tons end the consumption for 1939 is estimated to have
been over 38,000 tons. In other words, in spite of its rapid growth,
the domestic industry has been eble to supply only about helf of the
effective demend, he country being dependent upon foreign sources
of supply for the other half.
In order to reduce the amount necessary to be imported
from foreign countries, an ambitious program of expansion was sanc-
tioned by the government in 1939. This program, however, along with
other recent projects, has been held up beceuse of Japan's major
military and economic effort on the continent.
As hes been said, Japan has no bauxite and experiments
using aluminum bearing cleys from Chosen and Manchoukuo have not
proved to be as veluable as they were originally expected to be just
after the industrial invasion of Manchuria in 1932. Accordingly the
whole Jananese aluminum industry depends on imported raw material for
its very existence.
Since Japan has no reserves of bauxite, it imports most
of its aluminum grade ores from the Dutch East Indies, British India,
Johore, Greece, and Hainan Islands. Although it is reported to be
exploiting deposits in Manchoukuo, Koree and North Chine, the 1939
report of the Bureau of lines lists no imports of bauxite from any
of these sources.
In 1938, the consumption of aluminum in Jepen was 38,000
metric tons or nearly four times that which was consumed in 1933.
The reason for the extraordinary increase in consumption is because
of a variety of fccts. Only a short time ago the country embarked
upon an ambitious industrial expansion program designed to expand
markedly the productive capacity of key industries both in Japan
proper and in Manchoukuo. At the same time it is pushing vigorously
aheed with its great armament program. Since there is a shortage of
metals, especially steel and copper, aluminum is in great demand as
a substitute metal. The biggest single demand for aluminum today
comes from the war industries, especially the airplene factories and
those concerns turning out water-bottles and mess kits for the troops.
But the "peace" industries, too, are steadily expanding their con-
sumption. Numerous hydro-electric power sites heve been developed
during the last few years end others are planned for the near future.
It is estimated that perheps 90s of the high voltage transmission
lines erected in connection with these power development schemes
use aluminum cables. Those most recently instelled use aluminum
cables with steel cores for greater strength. Aluminum pots, pens,
kettles, and similer domestic utensils are steedily gaining in popular
favor and are replacing the iron articles formerly in vogue. Other
important consumers of aluminum are Japan's newly developed industries

(6) Fluorspar end Bauxite. Large scale production of

Fluorspar is used in lead smelting and in the making of ferro-sili-

alumna in making cryolite synthetically, Cryolite, in turn is used
in making aluminum. Fluorspar is also used in the steel, opalescent
glass, enamel and chemical industries.
The importance of fluorspar to Japen hes been indicated by
her anxiety to obtein the material over the period of the last two
years. The supply from Chosen spparently has not been sufficient to
meet the demend. In comparison with the proportion of fluorspar
used in the manufecture of steel in the United States, Japan's pro-

tons of fluorspar as a flux which is considerably more than the pro-

discovered in Manchuria, but the production will probably not exceed
3,000 tons per year. The first exports of fluorspar, sbout 540 tons,
from the United States to Japan were sent out in 1939. Another fact
of interest is that during 1939 end 1940 over 90 percent of the
fluorspar production of the Union of South Africa wes sent to Japan.
It epnears thet the Union of South Africa is at present the chief
source of Japan's foreign supply.
Next in importance is the supply from Mexico, which is
being negotiated through the Compania Internacional de Comercio,S.A.,
a Jepanese company. This organization effected a contract for the
purchase of 12,O00 tons of fluorspar from Sr. Federico Deschamps,
the owner of the fluorspar deposit. This deposit is probebly the
largest, not only in Mexico, but in the Western Hemisphere. It is
situated near the highway close to Taxco in the stete of Guerrero.
After the contract with Deschamps hsd been completed, the
Japenese company requested him to enter into a new contract. This
Deschamps refused to do because of previously made errangements to
sell his product to American companies. Then the Compania Inter-
nacional de Comercio placed an embargo on the mine forcing the owner
to put up an exorbitant emount of money as a bond. While the suit
is in the process of litigation, the Japanese cen exploit the mine
on the basis of the previous contract. As a result, even if the
Japanese lose the lawsuit, they will have achieved their objective
of obtaining the mineral now.
This Mexican fluorspar is considered to be of excellent
quality and cen be produced in great quantities. Beceuse of the pro-
duction of the low grade and the low price of fluorspar in the
United States, Mexican producers have been able to find only a limited
market for their product in the United Stetes.

(8) Nickel. Mickel is largely used in the manufecture
of steel alloys, as a cetelyzer in the plating industry, and in
coinage. In the manufacture of certein grades of steel it is indis-
pensable. Nickel-steel, which contains from 3 to 3.5 percent of
nickel, and ebout .25 percent of carbon, is much tougher and stronger
then ordinary steel, and extremely ductile. The combination of these
properties causes nickel-steel to be regarded as the best meterial
for armor plates. Nickel is strongly anticorrosive and has qualities
of increasing the toughness, durability end tensile strength of alloys.
It is malleable and can be easily controlled metallurgically.
It cen be seen, therefore, that nickel has not only attained
front rank as en essential metal of modern industry, giving it an
enormous secondery munitions velue, but that its importence in the
manufacture of heevy guns, armor plate, casings for rifle and mechine
gun bullets, and for rotating end driving band in shells, makes it
indispensable from the point of view of primary munitions needs as
well.
Japanese supplies. In spite of the experiments tried
in recent years by the Showe Mining Company (Showa Kogyo Krisha) in
producing metellic uickel, Japen is almost l00 percent dependent
upon imports for her supply. The Showa company is producing ferro-

proposed to edopt the Krupp process for meking ferro-nickel; the
originel intention of producing metellic nickel hes been abandoned
temporarily owing to lack of technicsl knowledge.
Jepan is sadly lacking in nickel; the resultant effect
on the menufacture of certsin types of steel has already been pointed
out. At one time Jepan negotiated with a French concern for the
establishment of a nickel refinery in Jepan for handling New Caledonia
ore, but this plan fell through; consequently, Jepen has hed to
import all of her supnlies of this metal.

The manufacturing industry is the lergest enterprise in

industry accounted for production of * 16,412,000,000, representing
78% of the velue of output from all industries. The rate of expension

rapidity, the velue of production having increased by over 2.5 times

as meny hends as the manufacturing industry.

in the value of production. The returns of the Depertment of Commerce
and Industry for 1937 show that the textile industry accounted for
23.83 of total production of all menufacturing pursuits, followed by
the metal industry with 20.5%, the chemical industry with 18.6% and
the machine and tool industry with 14.5%
The relative position of the enterprises within the manu-
facturing industry discloses some significant chenges. The textile
enterprise, for instance, slipped from 41.4% to 23.8% in its share
of total output of the manufacturing industry between 1926 and 1937,
while the metel industry jumped from 6.4% to 20.5% in the same period.
The absolute production figures for the manufacturing industry
increased considerably. In 1926 it stood at 6,936 million yen. In
1937 it was up to 16,412 mi1lion yen.

When the Sino-Japanese conflict began in July, 1937,
Japanese industry had not reached a stage at which it could easily
meet the enormous demand for war materials required by the emergency.
The development of heavy industries was still insufficient, end a
rapid change in the structure of Jepanese industry was required in
order to secure an adequate supply of war materiels and to provide
a basis for general productive expansion. As foreign assistance
could not be relied on, the restriction of imports became inperative
in order to belence international peyment. Japanese industry, there-
fore, had to undertake the hard task by itself. The enforced change
required strong Government control in every field of the Japanese
economy.
Government control became stricter after June, 1938,
perallel with expansion of the war front. The Temporary Fund Adjust-
ment Act was promulgated, whilst control of commodities, foreign trade,
foreign exchenge was tightened by the promulgation of the Temporary
Export end Import Control Act. As & consequence of the General Mobil-
ization Law, labor and engineers and experts were subjected to Govern-
ment control. These measures checked the flow of capitel, materials,
end labor into non-essentiel industries, whilst positive policies
encoureging the repid expension of the productive cepecity of vital
industries were also adopted, such es the Automobile Manufacturing
Industry Act, the Iron and Steel Industry Act, the Petroleum Resources
Exploitation Act, the iineral Production Lew, the Japan Gold Production
Compeny Lew, the Machine Tool Industries Law, and the Aeroplane Manu-
facturing Industries Law.
These measures, both negative and positive, brought about
a structural chenge in Japenese industry, with resultant great ectivity
in wartime industries, and a retrogression in peacetime industries.
Throughout 1939 efforts were exerted to resuscitete the peecetime
industries end in bringing ebout a better balance with the munitions
enterprises with a view to stimulating Jepanese exports.
Among the industries most adversely affected by the Sino-
Japanese hostilities were the textile enterprises. Teking the yeers
1931, 1932 and 1933 as 100, the production index for cotton yarn
wes down to 68.3 in March, 1939. The high point in cotton yarn pro-
duction was recorded in 1937 when the index was up to 140.4.

(3) Important Lines of Manufacture. The discussion of
manufacture in the various industries such as steel, textile, chemicals,
etc., have been discussed previously in connection with the raw materials
concerned. The following discussion concerns phases of the subject
of manufacturing which were not discussed earlier.
(a) Machinery. In spite of the expenditure of great
effort to become self-sufficient in the manufacture of industrial
equipment, Japan has not been able to become an important machinery-
producing nation. Japanese nachinery production is not ten percent
of thet produced by the United States.
In 1938, Japan imported slightly more than $66,000,000
worth of industrial machinery. Of this amount, more than half
($35,333,000) was imported from the United Stetes. Japan's imports
in 1939 amounted to $64,250,000.
The best that Japan could claim in the field of machinery
is that she is 80% self-sufficient. She is dependent to a great de-
gree on imports of certain high grade types which fact makes her
extremely vulnerable to embargo. Japan particularly needs ball bear-
ings, steam turbines, mining and smelting machinery, oil well and
reiining machinery, machine tools end electrical apparatus. The
cooperation of the United Kingdom and Canada would be necessary to
deprive Japan of these necessary imports.

ships under repair was unusually high, undoubtedly caused by the
strain on individual ships due to constant operation over several
years without the usual time out between trips for minor repairs.
3. Commerce.
a. Domestic Trade.
(1) The rail and water communications of Jepan have been
so overworked during the last few years, that the movement of cormod-
ities end menufectures within the Empire heve been greatly curtailed,
so much so that shortages of all sorts have appeared in the large
cities. Replacement of worn out cars and vessels heve not kept up
with the increased demand and in consequence the distribution system
of goods end services in the mpire has been unable to carry the
required load.

b. Foreign Trade (Exvorts) 1914-1937. For many years raw silk
has been the chief article of Jepanese trade. The first World War
increased the variety of items exported end trebled the volume of export
trade. Foreign trade did not suffer from the post-war depression until
1930 and 1931, when a severe recession cut the value of the export
trede in helf. In 1932 the Japanese depreciated the value of the yen
and, in the fece of world-wide depression, they increased their export
trade by invading markets previously held by Greet Britain and other
Western powers.
1937-1939. Following the Japanese invasion of China, Japanese
goods were boycotted in many parts of the world. Tariff berriers,
already high, were generally raised. The strain of war caused short-
ages of labor and materials and resulted in higher production costs.
Japanese export trede is deuendent on en emple supply of cheep imported
rew materials which are no longer forthcoming. Government regulations
to conserve the foreign exchenge have robbed business of its flexibility.
The deprecieted yen is no longer an advantage.
1939-1940. The present European War hes proved a mixed blessing.
It hes reised the cost of imported raw materials or has entirely pre-
vented their importation. An advantageous barter agreement with
Germany (Manchurian soy beens for German machinery) promised great
profits, but since the ship-borne trade with Germany was suspended,
only a fraction of the profit was realized. New orders have come to
Jepan from South and Central America, and from other former German
merkets, but the reduced stocks of goods in Japan have handicapped
exporters.
In spite of these difficulties, the velue of Japanese export
trade struck an all time high during 1939 end though only Empire figures

c. Foreign Trade (Imports) 1914-1937. The volume of Japanese
imports has been largely dependent on the volume of exports. The
prosperity brought about by Japanese exports during the first World
War was followed by a wave of spending, chiefly for consumers' goods
and raw materials to supply her factories. High tariffs were imposed
on all foreign goods that might be regerded as luxuries.

Economic
No expense was spared to secure the latest foreign machinery,
inventions, end commodities that could be copied end manufactured in
Japan et a fraction of the cost abroad. The military forces have
always endeavored to accumulate large supplies of strategic materials
and great stocks of munitions. Private industry, under Government
pressure, copied the ermy and navy in this policy and laid in great
stocks of cotton, wool, metels, oil, etc.
1937-1940. Since the outbreak of the Chinese war stringent
regulations have been set up to prevent the importation of any but the
most essential commodities. Cotton, for instence, cannot be imported
unless en equal amount is exported as cotton goods. This "link system"
has made the trensaction of business difficult for Japanese business-
men.
The present import trade consists principally of (1) raw materials
which can be manufactured into erticles for export; (2) strategic
materials such as oil, steel scrap, copper, nitrates, and aeroplene
perts; (3) machinery which will enable Japan to be self-sufficient.
A nation as poor as Japan can never hope for complete "autarchy"
or self-sufficiency. The abrogation of the Trade Pact of 19ll by the
United States hes nevertheless redoubled Japan's determination to be
dependent on no one country for her supplies. Even at a financial
loss, she has begun to divert part of her purchases of petroleun to
Iren, iexico, end the Netherlends Eest Indies. Similarly, she is
changing the source of her cotton supplies. She bought the bulk of her
cotton from India, until the imports of Japanese cotton fabrics were
restricted by India; then she gave the trade to America. Now she is
seeking cotton from Brazil, Africa, end Chine.

General Remarks on Japan's Foreign Trade.
Japan's foreign trade is an integral part of the nation's
life. Xinety years ago, Japan was self-sufficient and self-contained.
The pendulum swung far in the other direction, and five years ago
Japan seemed to be irrevocably committed to living by international
trade. She remains self-sufficient in most of her foodstuffs, but
raw materials to feed her growing industries are required and in a
measure paid for by the labor of her millions turning out finished
goods for foreign currency.
The rising control of Japan's military leaders have changed
Shidehara's plan for the peaceful development of international trade
to one of extending by military force Japan's political and economic
control over a vast region. They have today changed the vwhole pattern
of Japanese life. The Army in China and Hanchuria, together vith a
horde of small business men and carpet-baggers have moved in, easing
the unemployment problem, in fact creating a shortage of skilled labor
but disrupting the smali enterprises dealing in consumer goods to
almost a chaotic state. The luxury import trade has been completely
shut off, causing little concern among the masses, but considerable
murmur amon the upper moneyed classes. The most elementary consumer
articles have been curtailed for the Japanese people in favor of the
colossal demands of the Army and Navy for steel, machinery, oil,
trucks, wool, cotton and nitrates. Industries are being nationalized,
integrated, directed and controlled.
The American abrogation of the Treaty of Commerce and Navi-
¿ation gave great impetus to Japan's determination to reshape her
economy and avoid derendence on American supplies and markets. She
has sought to substitute Chinese, Indian, and Egyptian cotton for
American cotton and N.E.I. oil for United States oil. Japan has
been exchanging missions vith a number of Latin-Anerican countries in
recent months as part of the Japanese Government's policy of gaining
ner: trade markets to replace the rapidly shrinking markets in other
parts of the world--in Lurope because of the war, and in the United
States because of embargoes. "hile some of the commodities purchased
from Latin American countries may be reshipped from Japan to Germany
by way of the Trans-Siberian Railway, Japan has looked principally
to filling her own needs. Particular importance is attached in Japan
to the visit of a Mexican mission which cave the Japanese an opportun-
ity to exchange oil for textile oods. In spite of their wish to cut
loose from trade with the United States, the present circumstances
have intensified their dependence on America, not only as a consumer
of Japanese goods, but also as a supplier of essential meterials. In
1939 the United States purchased 35 of all Japanese exports outside
the yen bloc; United Stetes purchases of one Jepanese item, silk,
accounted for 24 (in value) of all Japanese sales outside the yen
bloc area. The hardest single blov vhich Japan's vital export trede
could suffer would be loss of the United States silk market. Conversely,
now commodities which in 1939 constituted 50, of our exports to Japan
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 310 === (confidence 0.99)
43
CONFIDENTIAL
Japan
Economic
are under embargo or subject to licenses vhich could be used to halt
shipments at any time.
The preceding tables show: that exports to Asia have in-
creased since i93l in exact proportion to a decrease in exports to
North America vith respect to the total yearly figure. Exports to
the United States in 1939 were approximately half the proportion of
imports. Imports from Asia, however, have only been maintained
vhile considerable gain from "orth merica has been shown. Imports
from South America have made reat strides.
The adverse trade balance climbed steadily to almost un-
precedented heights by 1937 but by 1939 shifted completely to a high
favorable balance. The war boom peak seems to have been reached the
summer of 1940 vhich brought the favorable balance down by the end
of the year.
The trade balance must be examined and compared vith respect
to the yen-bloc and free exchange areas. The adverse balance of trade
with free areas reached a hih in 1937 but dropped gradually by 1939.
Trade figures for the first nine months of 1940 indicate the unfavor-
able balance is again rising.
A careful examination of the trade position of Japan shows
the desirability of larger imports than exports from the yen bloc
areas, and that the opposite position be maintained with regard to the
free trade areas. The table shoing this position gives exactly the
opposite picture. Imports from the free areas are being extended in
order that exports vill go to the yen-bloc areas. This in itself
seems a contradiction to the policy of the military. Fi ures for the
nine month period of 1940 show even an increase in this tendency.
Japan's aim to become self-sufficient in trade within a
Great Dast Asia co-prosperity sphere has net vith a certain degree
of success. Actual consumnation of this grandiose scheme depends
entirely upon the nature and scope of interference offered by the
United States and Great Britain. On this one i sue rests the destiny
of Japan.
Even without interference, Japan's position as a nanufactur-
in nation is vitally dependent upon a much wider sphere, the whole
international sphere of trade. The international exchance of goods
on a basis of comparative costs remains essential to Japan's prosperity.
Japan, whether victorious or defeated in Asia will need foreign loans
and commercial arrangelents favorable for her imediate recovery in
export trade and eventual repayment of her indebtedness.

4.
Transportation.
a.Railroads.
(1) Railway net. The Empire has a railway net which
adequately serves the normal demand both for freight and passenger
traffic. At the present time, however, the increased demand caused
by the var situation and the reat increase in population of the
large cities, has throvn a reat strain on the railrays.
In 1938, Japan proper had 24,878.20 kilometers of railway
lines, most of this line has a gauge of 3 feet 6 inches. About 7,000
kilometers of the total are local railways, privately owned.
The lines in Chosen consisted of 3,831.1 kilometers in 1939,
owned by the Government and 1,375.7 kilometers of privately owned
lines.
In Taivan there were, in 1939, 881.9 kilometers of Govern-
nent owmed railvay. Privately owned lines, nearly all narrow gauge
amounted in 1939 to 2,619.4 kilometers.
Karafuto has 342.9 kilometers of Government owmed line.
(2) State of Maintenance. The exact present state of the
railways has been considered a military secret since September 28, 1939,
but it can be said that the reat demands made upon the lines have
caused considerable deterioration in the rolling stock.
(3) Rolling Stock. The numbers and conditions of the
rolling stock of the Japanese railvays have been considered a military
secret from January, 1933.
All reports recsived indicate that there is a reat short-
age of rolling stock in Japan proper.
The same mystery has been thrown over any new construction
in the Empire. It may be assumed that there is not very much new
line being laid due to the shortage of steel.
(4) Railvay Equipment. Although most of Japan's equipment
is manufactured in Japan, there is a shortage of engines and cars
caused by the tremendously increased load placed on the railways by
the war. There are reports of great deterioration of rolling stock.
Because of these reasons, there have been shortages of materials in
Tokyo end other industrial centers due to the inability of the railvays
to carry them.
b.
Highways.
(1) Japan's roads cover approximately a million kilometers.
Practically all of this mileage is in dirt roads, very narrow, and
capable of takin only the typical Japanese tvwo-wheeled carts. There
are probably not over 2,000 kilometers of first class highway in the
Empire.
(2) Road Transportation. Since 1937 the conditions of
motor transport have not been made public. From available sources,
hovever, a fair picture can be made. The number of cars and trucks in
private hands have dininished considerably since 1938, due to the lack
of gasoline - and in the case of charcoal run vehicles, a shortage of
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 312 === (confidence 0.99)
45
CONFIDENTIAL
Japan
Economic
charcoal. Other reasons for the smaller number are (1) the con-
tinuing demand on the Japanese automotive producers by the Army,
and, (2) the smaller importation of foreign cars and parts, due to
the necessity of conserving Japanese foreign credits for military
purposes.
(3) Motor and Other Road Transportation. Due to the
severe rationing of asoline and other fuel, total fi ures on the
number of cars, trucks and busses vould be misleading, as probably
75% of the motor vehicles in private hands are off the road. Accord-
ing to the latest available reports, some 50% of the busses and 30%
of the trucks are using charcoal or some other substitute fuel.
These have proven to be reletively inefficient in operation, and in
addition there have been sporadic shortages of charcoal. Practically
no new production of motor vehicles has been designated for private
ownership, it being earmarked for the military. The chief trouble
of the automotive industry is the rationing of raw materials.
C.
Water Transportation.
(1) Merchant Marine. At the end of September, 1940,
Japan had registered 2,165 ships of 100 tons or over; 1,151 were of
1,000 tons or more.
The Japanese merchant fleet, having been closely tied
to the Navy, especially as to designs of ships, has a high proportion
of vessels convertible to naval use, as seaplane tenders, auxiliary
cruisers, scouts, transports, etc.
About 400,000 tons of ner shipnin is built annually.
The Herchant Harine includes 39 tankers and 6 whalers
convertible to tankers. Total oil capacity - 4,123,710 barrels.
The Japanese Terchant service is the third largest in the
rorld, and has been considered the most efficient. In the last
fev years the great denands made on the merchant ships by the war in
China have caused constant operation of vessels vithout opportunity
for periodic overhauls and small repairs. In consequence, there is
a reported reat deterioration in the merchant fleet, especially
freighters and smell passenger ships.
(2) Shinyards and Docks. The Japanese Empire has about
600 dockyards. The exact fi ure, since 1937, has been considered secret.
ost of this number are small and build only fishing and coastal craft.
The large yards - excluding naval building yards - are at Yokohama,
Kobe, Osaka, Fagasaki, and Hikoshima (Shimonoseki).
(3) Ports and Harbors. The principal ports in Japan are
Kobe, Osaka, Yokohama, oji, Magoya, and Nagasaki.
(4) Trade Routes. In recent years the percentage of
Japanese ships which bring goods to and from the Eastern coast of
Asia has risen sharply in relation to the volume of shipping to other
parts of the world. This is due mainly to two factors, (1) the war
in China which is absorbing an increasingly large part of the Merchant
Harine, and, (2) the great increase in trade with the Yen Bloc.

(5) Inland Later Routes. Formerly (before the China

the China run, thus dislocating a very important part of Japan's

(2) Airplanes and Airplane Manufacture. On March 27,
1941 the Army and Navy air forces had, on hand, 2,921 first line
planes of all types with 50%; estimated in reserve, at schools, etc.
At the same time the production per month was 144 air frames and

5. Comrnunications.
a.
Cables. Cables connect Japan vith China, iberia, Hanila,
Guam and Jepan's outlying possessions. One American cable landed at
the Bonin Islands and two Danish lines landed at  a asali are the
only foreign cables into Japan, the latter have recently been ab-
sorbed by Japan. Mo nev. foreign concessions are being granted. The
United States is usually reached via Guam; and Lurope, Couth Asia
and Australia via hanghai. The island of Yap continues to be an
important center of cable communication.
b. Radio. Radio stations are distributed at various points
throu hout Japan and her possessions, inclucin the northernmost
Kurile Island, the southern tip of Formosa, all the leading Mandate
Islands, the Korean border and in  anchuria and China. The net is
very complete, so that Japanese ships operatin any here in the
Western Pacific are in easy contact with Tolyo. General broadcasting
bean in 1925 and at the end of 11ay 1940, there were about 5,000,000
licensed receiving sets. Fadio broadcasting in Japan is controlled
by one company, the Broadcastin Corporation of Japan, which is a
public service company operating vithout profit. Operating expenses
are covered by a monthly charge from each holder of a receiving
license.
Japan is divided into at least fourteen broadcasting
districts in cach of vhich a 10-ky. siation has been built. These
key staticns are at Tokyo, Osaka, Nagoya, Hiroshima, Kumamoto, Sendai,
Sapporo, Onomichi, Oita, Fukushima, Bofu, Koriyima, Latsuyama and
Aomori. There are also at least 28 sub-stations or relay stations
ranging from 300 watts to 3 k. so located that all parts of Japan
proper are vithin l00 miles from one of the stations.
Short vave radio broadcasts rere. inaugurated by the
Broadcasting Corporation of Japan in 1935. By March, 1939, Japanese
programs vere being heard in China and the South Sees, Forth and Couth
America and Europe. Analysis of the 300 hours of pro ram material
broadcast per month showed two-thirds of this time devoted to politi-
cal propaganda and news.

Africa, South America and Indo-China. The sending station is near
Nazaki, and the receiving station is near Komuro.
c. Telegraph. The telegraph system through 13,648 stations
extends to practically every village of the Empire. The lines exceed
27,000 miles and the service is fair.
d. Telephone. The telephone net extends to all the most
important tovns, with a fairly complete local net in the larger cities.
There were in larch, 1937, 1,019,329 telephone instruments and
4,369,474 miles of telephone lines. The service is comparatively poor,

f. Financial Stability. Since 193l Japan has been increasing
her tempo of active expansion and military might. To keep this up a
new economic and financial program has been forced uron the nation.
Tith linister Takahashi as financial helmsman fron May, 1932, to
February, 1936, there vas financial stability, as he alone best knev
how to counter the increases in military expenditures and heavy
investments to consolidate the ains in lanchuria. By 1936 he rea-
lized that the limit of reflation had been reached and held firn to
a change of financial policy. He firmly maintained that the national
debt must not increase, taxes should not rise further, and net: bond
issues be reduced. Extension of sound peacetime industry and trade
rather than increase in nilitary expenditure and conversion of Japa-
nese productive economy to var industries vas his fornula for a strong
and healthy internal financial condition.
In February 193ó during the "Incident" r. Takahashi vas
killed as he and his policy interfered with the eneral program of
the military. From now on Japanese finance became subordinate to
militaristic demands. The military have ambled everything on their
expansion program. Hanchoukuo then and China nor must bend to their
will or Japan collapses. As more and more the vill of the military
is forced on the business man and all the people, the larger becomes
the stake and sacrifice of all concerned to this end. Japan must have
capital and to produce this, the national income and savings must be
increased. As national expenditures, including military expenses
mount, more and nore bonds must be floated.
It has long been contended that a ¿old reserve is
essential to the financial stability of a nation. This is true vith
reard to the external trade relations, but an internal financial
structure can be maintained with a small gold reserve by heavy direct
taxes or large scale savings, as evidenced by Germany, rho collects
taxes up to 30% of the national income. iany hsve predicted the
collapse of Japan due to her diminishing ¿old reserves, but she still
keeps on. The financial structure of Japan for years has been based
upon a small money clique in partnership vith the overnment carrying
on the expansion and development of industry. When money was needed
there was government subsidy or the monopolists borrowed from them-
selves. The military clique have now replaced the monopolists in their
CONFIDENTIAL

partnership vith the government and by excercising control over
money, men and materials, can force productive expansion to be
totally conscripted for war. This means the surplus from the rich
as well as the poor in the form of savings and taxes can be stimu-
lated and gathered for use in this emergency. Taxes are still
relatively low (12% in comparison to Germany) but savings are being

The internal financial stability of Japan is now in an
artificial unsound condition. It is tied to absolute success in
Eastern and Southern Asia. : ortgaged to this condition are the taxes
both in blood and sweat which the people for years to come must pay.
The recovery period from 1932-36 which was gearing Japan to a quasi-
vartime economy was successful only because vages were kept down and
industrial rationalization increased. Inflation did not set in due
to this fact. Tov inflation is in a cumulative stage as living costs
are risin over and above va es and the people are forced daily to

From all indications Japan's peak in productive stability

fallen off and thicre is & rising realizatior on the part of the

The conclusion seems to be that though Japan's internal
financial structure may be at present sound, it is purely artificial.
American-Japanese trade is still furnishin Japan vith the gold as
well as certain basic strategic supplies that maintain her present
wartime economy. Any chan e would force Japan to take by arms the
materials she lacks, where she can, and in quantities that vill

Population figures are quite inaccurate since the beginning
of the China "Incident"; vhere they approach accuracy, they are
misleading. But there are certain factors of importance that should
be noted.
The total population of Japan proper in 1925 was approxi-
mately 59,737,000 with 30,013,000 males and 29,274,000 females
(a difference of 739,000 in favor of males); in 1930, the ponulation
was 64,450,000 vith 32,390,000 males and 32,060,000 females (a dif-
ference of 330,000 in favor of males); in 1935, the population was
69,254,000 vith 34,734,000 males and 34,520,000 females (a difference
of 214,000 in favor of males). The official figures for 1940, if and
when they are published, will be extremely interesting.
The phpulation of Japan proper for 1937 was estimated at
about 71,000,000 people. If the conditions in 1937 are re arded as
normal, those in 1938 may be considered to have been affected by the
China "Incident". The actual increase of births over deaths in 1937
which vas 973,000 in 1937 fell sharply to 669,000 in 1938. This
corresponded to a decrease in the birth rate per 1,000 of population
of from 30.6 in 1937 to 20.7 in 1938. (There is no vay of estimating
the increase of population in China as a result of the Japanese soldiers
being involved in the Cnina "Incident"!)
It is true that if grorth of population is one of the
basic factors of economic progress for investment outlet and capital
formation, the 300,000 decrease in birth rate in 193C as against
1937, and the corresponding decreases in subsecuent years, should be
added to the cost of the China "Incident".
In a previous Ecolonic Estimate (that of 1936), the state-
ment vas made that "the menace of population rovth in Japen lies
rather in the direction of economic warfare than in large scale mi-
gration. Increased industrialization in Japan, under which raw
materials are imported and manufactured into finished comnodities,
and then, in turn, exported and the proceeds thereof used to buy raw
materials abroad seems to be the present Japanese plan for meeting the
population question. This plan of course, involves ruthless economic
varfare by Japan for her national existence and vill more and more
denend upon her ability to dominate Asia by force". This statement,
vritten in 1936, accurately predicted the situation vith vhich Japan
and Asia are confronted at the present time (1941). The great obstacle,
from the point of viev of Japan, is that she is finding considerable
difficulty in dominating Asia by force. As the length of time in-
creases during which Japan is making the attempt, to a similar extent
is Japan's normel acquisition of raw materials for her population
becoming more difficult. The breaking of this vicious circle is
complicated by Japan's ambitious progran in southeast Asie and the
East Indies along vith her program in vorld affairs in general.

In Japan proper the population density is about 476 persons
to the square mile (1935). Only Holland, Belgium and England have
more densely populated countries. The density of population in Japan
differs greatly according to the section. The prefecture of which
Tokyo is a part is the most densely populated with 7,692 per square
mile, while Hokkaido has 90 people per square mile.
b.
Expatriation and the Dual Citizenship Problem. Until 1916
Japan did not recognize expatriation of her sons and daughters who
acquired foreign citizenship, excepting those Japanese women who
married foreign subjects. The result was that Japanese who were born
in the United States and thereby became citizens were still carried
on the Japanese census rolls) thus they were in the peculiar situation
of having double nationality or were "dual citizens". Although the
Japanese Law of Nationality was revised in August, 1916, and further
CONFIDENTIAL

CONFIDENTIAL
Japan
Economic
amended in December, 1924, the problem is not yet solved beceuse
American born Japanese boys (unexpatriated) are still technically
liable to the Japanese conscription law.
Brazil has for many years been the outlet for the largest
number of Jepanese emigrants. In 1936 a total of 5,357 Japanese
subjects, representing 48% of the total number of emigrants for that
year, went to Brazil. The next largest outlet hes been the
Philippines which accounted for 2,89l in the seme year.
C. Lebor. Compared with labor in Occidental countries
Japanese labor receives much lower wages with general labor conditions
being worse. Traditionel subserviency of employees to their masters
still exists. The labor movement is gradually growing in power, how-
ever, in line with the creation of giant orgenizations.
While organized laborers in 1936 accounted for only 6.9
percent of the total number of laborers the absolute increase of such
workers in the eleven years from 1926 to 1936 was 68 percent. The
number of labor unions in the various industries heve fluctuated in
the pest few years. In 1937 there were 837 unions as compared with
973 in the previous year. Union membership has been lergest in the
transportation enterprise in recent years, followed by the machine and
tool industry.
The number of factories increased from 31,717 in 1914 to
106,005 in 1937. The number of laborers increesed two and one-half
times, totaling 2,936,512 factory laborers in 1937. Male laborers
accounted for 1,727,032 or 58.8 percent of the total.
According to an investigation made by the Bank of Japan
the average deily wages of factory workers stood at  2.208 for male
and ¥ 0.723 for female workers in December, 1932. Similar figures in
December, 1938, showed ¥ 2.560 for men and Y 0.839 for women.
The maximum workkng hours are fixed at ll. This, however,
applies only to factories employing the protected workers, i.e., male
operatives under 16 years of age end female operatives. There are no
restrictions for those fectories employing adult males.
Women must not be employed four weeks before and after
childbirth. But four weeks after childbirth they may be employed,
when so desired by them in work judged harmless by physicians. Women
with children not over one year old must be allowed to suckle them
twice a workdey, for periods not exceeding half an hour.
Wartime labor policies. Vith the progress of Sino-Japanese
hostilities, the demand for labor in the armament industries is growing
apace, whilst peacetime industries suffer from the official restriction
of domestic consumtion and raw materials. In view of this situation,
the Welfare Ministry decided on June 2, 1938 on two fundamental labor
policies as follows:
(1) Measures to assure an adequate supply of workers
for the armament industries.
