(a) To transfer unemployed workers in the non-urgent
industries to the armaments industries.
(b) Increased activity of national employment exchanges
to assist discharged workers in changing and finding
occupations.
(c) Inducement to women and girls discharged from the
cotton and woolen industries to return to rural dis-
tricts.
(d) Occunational guidance and re-education, by the
Governnent of unemployed other than the above mentioned
workers.
(e) Unemuloyment relief works and work-providing
facilities.
8.
Economic Penetration by Foreign Interests.
Japan is exceptionally free from economic penetration end en-
croachment on the part of alien powers. This is due to strict govern-
mental control directly through ovmershin of many of the large interests
and indirectly by means of subsidies and protective tariffs favoring
Japanese concerns. General liotors of Japan, Ford Hotor Company of
Japan and the Victor Talking llachine Company are believed to be the
only strictly foreign companies of importance and it has been reported
that the latter company has sold a majority interest to Japan. The
electrical manufacturing industry has large foreign capital investments,
though with minority interest.
There is no doubt about three main features. The first, is that
the chief creditor countries are the United States with $420,000,000 at
the end of 1937 followed by Great Britain with about $215,000,000,
Germany and Sweden. The second, is that the great bulk of the invest-
ments consist of Japanese Government or municipal securities or port-
folio investments taking approximately 87%, the remaining 13% consisting
chiefly of interests in public utilities and direct investment by
American manufactures. The third point is that Japan has never defaulted
on the interest or principal of any of her foreign debt.
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 326 === (confidence 0.99)
59
CONFIDENTIAL
Japan
Economic
Since the war indications point to a growing accumulation of
frozen funds of American firms operating in the country.
9.
Economic Penetration into Foreign Countries.
Japanese penetration, economically, of neighboring territory on
the Asiatic mainlend has progressed rapidly. Investment figures are
incomplete as yet, but at the end of 1935 it was estimated that Japanese
investments in China were approximately 2 billion yen and at the end of
1939 investments in Manchoukuo almost doubled this amount. To system-
atically develop the areas on the Asiatic continent under her control,
Japan is setting up various development companies such as she did in
Korea, with the Oriental Development Company, in Manchuokuo, with the
Manchuria Industrial Development Company, in China, with the North China
Development Company and the Central China Development Company. These
companies are really large holding companies concerned with making
investments in the subsidiary companies within their sphere in order
to consolidate and coordinate management and operation. Japanese prac-
tically control the iron production of China and a ccnsiderable portion
of the coal output, though during the "Incident" normal production has
been curtailed. Many textile mills and other factories in China are
owned by Japanese. Through agreement with Russia, Japan has important
oil concessions which she is developing in North Saghalien, timber
concessions in eastern Siberia and fishing along the Siberian shores.
The large number of Japanese in Havaii, the United States and Brazil
have agricultural and other interests in the countries where they
reside. The Japanese in the Philippine Islands have important holdings
in the Davao hemp district of lfindanao.
Japan's control of Manchoukuo is complete and is affected through
placing Japanese officials in key positions of the lanchoukuo Govern-
ment. Through this control, Japan is enabled to monopolize the economic
development of lianchoulkuo. It might be added that iianchoukuo is a
prototype for a conquered China.
Japanese penetretion of world markets, particularly for textiles,
iron manufactures, peper and pottery has increased enormously in .
recent years.
10. Influence of Economic Situation on Foreign Relations.
Japan's economic policy has been gradually widening during the
past decade. With territorial expansion was linked an economic idea -
that of regional self-sufficiency, or the "bloc economy". In 1931,
Hanchuria was called Japan's "economic life-line". In 1932-33 the
watchword was the "Japan-llanchoukuo Economic Bloc". After 1937 the
demand was for a "Japan-China-iianchoukuo bloc". Finally, the slogan
today is for a "Great East Asia" to include the rich territories of
Indo-China, Malaya the East Indies and the Philippines.
Japan's present policy is to extend political control over a
vast region. Raw materials in this area are to be conscripted and
merkets opened and held by force. The wider this territory becomes
CONFIDEITIAL

=== Page 327 === (confidence 0.99)
60
CONFIDENTIAL
Japan
Economic
the larger become the requirements for materials to extend such an
economy. Germany in Europe is a current example of Japan in Asia.
Japan realizes now that only b military occupation can her bloc
economy succeed.
With each success of Germany, Japan widens her sphere and lays
more grandiose schemes. Existing world economic spheres belonging
to competing powers such as the United States and England are forced
to consolidate their positions. The re-division of bloc economies
and their head-on collisions are the order of a new age. Within this
order is a growing shift to a barter-economy and a cornering of the
basic raw materials by the conquering powers.
The countries most strategically affected in this collision
are forced either to appease or fight. The clearest example today is
the fact that the United States and ngland must continue to sell oil
to Japan or run the risk of pushing Japan into an immediate attack
upon the Netherlands East Indies which would raise the likelihood of
war with Australia, New Zealand and the British forces in Malaya and
Singapore. This in turn would make an American-Japanese clash almost
inevitable. Anglo-American activity at the present must be centered
in urope and the ilear East.
All treaties, either economic or political, today have become
as oil to grease the wheels of the present emergency in the various
spheres of regional hegemony. Japan makes or breaks such treaties
with impunity either to extend her progress or consolidate her portion.
With opportunistic designs she rides before the wind and is to be
stopped only by a stronger power or combination of powers.
11. General Conclusions.
Recognizing the danger of oversimplifying the economic situation
as it relates to the Japanese, one can safely say that she expects
to expand her economic interests in southeestern Asia and the south-
western Pacific as much as she can without going to war with the
United States. In other vords, she proclains her desire for peace
with the United States at the same time she totally disregards any
factor standing between her and eventual domination by force of
regions she considers within her "sphere".
One can justifiably make the statenent that the economic situa-
tion in Japan vill probably get no worse than it is at the present
time (liay 194l). Because of the poor rice crops in Japan proper for
the past year and the lack of raw materials, Japan has probably
reached the proverbial nadir of her economic situation unless addition-
al obstacles are created to prevent the improvement of her economic
condition.
To quote the report of the American Consulate General of liarch
17, 194l: "Japanese activities along economic lines in Indo-China,
Thailand and the Netherlands' Dast Indies are reported to be beginning
to bear fruit; further, Germany reputedly is giving Japan 'a quite
substantial material help' as well as extensive technical advice:
therefore, German experts are said to discount the possibility of a
CONFIDENTIAL

=== Page 328 === (confidence 0.99)
3
CONFIDENTIAL
Japan
Economic
Japanese economic collapse under present political conditions, and
even if the status quo ante were to be reestablished in China in a
settlement of the Sino-Japanese conflict 'it is taken for granted
that the Japanese economic predominance in the East Asiatic area
can be considered firmly established and very difficult to be shaken
off' ".
There are, however, increasing signs of economic strain in
Japan. In spite of the fact that war has brought a temporary pros-
perity, manufacture is booming (where raw materials are obtainable),
and there is no great unemployment problem, wages have not increased
in proportion and the people are carrying a heavy burden. Japan is
facing inflation, a lack of foreign credits; the only reason that
there has not been a complete disruption of her domestic financial
organization may be found in internal governmental control, heavy
taxation, and the increasing savings accounts built up by the people.
The essential fact remains that Japan lacks essential raw
materials to support either her manufacturing trade or a major war
effort. To procure them she must have foreign exchange. If the present
unfavorable trade balance is not radically adjusted it appears that
complete exhaustion of all assets capable of being converted into
foreign exchange to balance the debit in international payments will
occur in the near future (some say by the end of 194l.) This will
result in the stoppage in the inflow of a considerable part of the
raw materials which are vital to the organic well-being of Japan, and
her ability to wage war will be seriously curtailed. No other country
even approaches the United States in its importance to Japan both as
a source of raw materials and as a market for exports. From the point
of view of the Japanese, discontinuance of this trade with the United
States would be catastrophic. In other words, the United States is
todey in a position to wreck completely the economic structure of the
Japanese Empire.

Through the intricate maze of events trans-
piring in Japan and abroad during the year 1940, there
emerged two crystalized objectives in this country;
the establishment of a highly organized national
defense system, and the creation of a Greater East
Asia Co-prosperity Sphere. These two projccts, although
synonymous and interdependent, have been characterized
by Japanese leaders as bcing equally important and
equally essential. During the greater part of the year,
therefore, major efforts were dirccted towards the re-
adjustment of the nation's economy along those lines
considercd most practicable for the actual realization
of these objectives.
Inability to terminate the China Incident
and repercussions from the European War intensified the
urgency for such efforts and wcre chiefly responsible
for the birth of the New National Structure Movement,
the hastening of the plan for the Greater East Asia Co-
prosperity Sphere, and the conclusion of the Tripartite
Pact. The first cnvisagos tho abandonmcnt of liberalism,
the institution of complete totalitarianism, the con-

CONFIDENTIAL.
FOOD RATIONING IN JAPAN Marked "For Internal Cireulation only.
Not to be published"
Rationing commenced on June lst, 1940.
By the end of September l94l, the rations in force in sir of
Japan's largest cities were:-
Rice. 120-570 grammes per diem a head according 'to age,
ser, and the nature of work being done. (Rationed March 1941).
Food Oil. From S/loths of a pint a head per & months, up to
about 22 pints per household of 20 persons or more per 3
months.
Charcoal. From 9 bags a head per annum, up to 36 bags per
housshold of 15 persons per annum. This ration is reduced
if gas is available.
Wheat Flour. From 6 ounces a head per month, up to 38 ounces
per household of 20 persons or more per month.
Sugar. about 3/4 1b. a head per month.
Matohes. l0 boxes per month a head approximately.
Ration cards were issued from local Government offices, and
were stamped by the local Communications Assooiation who
verified and marked the cards with the number of persons in
each family. Cards could then be taken to local retailers
for the purchase of the appropriate rations.
Non-rationed foods.
Meat. This is not rationed, but it does not normally figure
to a large extent in Japanese menus. Chickens are available,
but are expensive.
Fish. Up to the present fish has been abundant.
Fresh vegetables. These are in good supply, but some shortage
may have been notioed in the larger towns owing to transport
diffioulties.
Tinned Foods. Supplies have been distributed in all Japanese
towns which might be exposed to bombing. Tinned Fish, and
tinned fruits are normally exported from Japan, and owing to
recent curtailment of export are plentiful.
Foodsturfs Distribution Committee.
This Committee was formed during the last week in November
l94l to distribute rioe and other important foodstuffs
direotly to the consumers in equal rations. It has not yet
been stated to what ertent the wholesale brade will be
excluded from this distribution soheme. A Bill regulating
distribution is expected to be introduced before the Diet
at the beginning of next year.
General Position
The Food situation in Japan is suoh that she should be self-
suffieient with rationing for at least three years. The
past three rice harvests have been bad, but Japan has made up
the whole of this deficienoy by importing, and will have to
return to previous standards. They have been recently
inoreasing comsumption because of better wages and migration
of labour from country to towns. They can in addition draw
on Manchykuo produotions - partioularly soya beans - that were
previously/

=== Page 393 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
previously exported to Europe and elsewhere.
The abundance of fish can only be ourtailed by shortage
of oil for the fishing fleets, but most of the boats are sail
It is interesting to note that the Japanese have an
objection to unpolished rioe similar to the British objection
to wholemeal bread. The food supply is made certain in
addition to rationing, by the stores which can be got at the
present moment from Indo-China and Thailand.
The shortage of fertilisers, due to the fact that Japan
is cut off from phosphate and potash, should not be fel& for
three years. The Japanese rely for fertilisers, to a
considerable extent, on self-supply (home-made) fertilisers,
and only the switch of labour from the land to industry or
the armed forces would affect this. (There are signs of
this ocourring already and it will have to be stopped by
the Japanese).
M. E. W.
(E.R.D. Japan).
December 13th, 1941.
SUPPLEMENTARY NOTE
Rice
In 1940 compulsory mixing of 10% barley or wheat with
all rice was enforced. This caused considerable dissat-
isfaction and was discontinued after a little time. Reports
indicate that reinforcement was lately under consideration.
The enforced mixing of imported rice, differing in kind and
quality from Japanese rice, has also caused resentment and
discontent.
Since the end of 1939 the sale of fully polished rice
has been prohibited, only 70% of polishing being allowed.

E X T E N T A N D L I M I T A T I O N S
OF JAPAN'S ECONOMIC PREPAREDNESS.
BY
GE OR GE E.RCSDEN;LLD.
NEW YORK, DECEMBER
1941

=== Page 398 === (confidence 0.98)
. 2
homefronts of ths wurring nations.Not only have strategists to con-
sider the industrial and economic situation of their own country as
one of the preponderant factors in all their decisions; they also
have to deirect their war efforts according to the economic possi-
bilities and limitations of the enemy.
That is why the economic structure of Japan is of the ut-
most importance for the Allies.If it is true,what many a writer has
been claiming,namely that Japan is devoid of essential raw materials
to such an extent that she can not possibly sustain warfare for lon
ger than two years,then strategists would have to conclude that an
inexpensive victory can be won by defensive strategy and delaying
tactics.If ,however,Japan is to be credited with sufficient econo-
mic backbone - i.e.industrial capacity and raw materials -to carry
on for time to come,the Allies will have to conduct offensive war-
fare against japan.as soon as possible.
Before Japan started her last crusade for "Lebensraum",tha
is before the conques": of Manchukuo,Japan,indeed,was the prototype
of a have-not nation: Her coal and lumber shortages were proverbiar;
she produced only abou' 1o % of her peace-time petroleum needs; she
had no bauxite, the ore thut contains aluminum and is indispensable
for aircraf: production.nor had she much xubber.In fac, she was de-
void of almost all materials of strategic importance.
Japan's situation in regard to the three most important.
groops of/
materiais for warfare -food,clothing and strategic rav materials -
at that time,represented itself is follows :

=== Page 399 === (confidence 0.99)
As far as food is concerned, Japan was - and stil1 15-
completely selfsufficient.If recently rumors have been heard to the
effect that Japan is running short of food ,they are solely due to
the fact that the conquered tmrritories of China consumed so much
homegrown Japanese food that a strain became apparent.If China shoul(
continue to constitute a debet factor in Nippon's food situation,it
can safely be expected that the Chinese in the conquered sections
will have to starve so that the Japanese may have enough to eat.Thus
we must conclude that Japan never was vulnerable as far as food is
concerned.
Much the same situation prevails in clothing.As a first
rate producer of silk and cotton as well as other materials for cloth-
9
ing,Japan is selfsufficient in this groop.
The strategic raw materials,however,are the sore spots of
Japan's economy.Before the conquest of Manchukuo,that is in 1931,she
was short of coal,wood,oil and bauxite,she was hard pressed for rubber
tin,nickel,mica,mercury,tungsten,manganese and antimony,chromium,
copper and,last not least,iron and steel.Not only was Japan lacking
these raw materials,she also was lacking the industrial capacity
to refine her oil,to smelt her tin and to produce her pig iron and
steel.
The conquest of Manchukuo relieved two key shortcomings :
it gave to Japan all the coal and all the lumber she needed,while
sizable rivers provided locations for important power projects.

=== Page 400 === (confidence 0.99)
LECISION FOR WAP AND ADOPTION OF AN ECONOMIC MASTER PLAN.
The conquest of the entire Far East by no means is a newry
conceived idea for the Japanese leaders.They always have considered
themselves as predestined to rule at least that section of the earth
But,before the ascent of Nazism and Facism,Japan was more concerned
with might and economic domination than with territorial acquisition
After the seizure of Karea and the establishment of the puppet-goveru-
ment in Manchukuo,there remained only two major steps towards ful-
fillment of the immediate Nipponese plans : Elimination of Vladivo-
stok as the"pistol pointed at the heart of Japan" and acquisition of
the whole of China as a vast market for Japan's industrial produc -
tion.The first step was tried repeatedly in vain and plans were shelved
for the time being.The second step was planned and prepared for the
beginning of 1936.
But something happened that changed Japanese plans complete-
ly.Staff-talks in Berlin ,ending in January 1936,convinced Japan that
she need not be content with China,and,what is more,the Japanese dele
gates became infected with General Haushofer's geopolitical doctrine.
They became convinced that it would not be enough to secure a market
and to have access to raw materials but that it would be imperative
for Japan to own all the territories where the needed raw materials
originate.The Chinese venture was started already.It was too late to
stop it,much to the disgust of Germany,whose officers ware training
Chinese troops.But,nevertheless,the new plans,worked out by German
economists were adopted by the Japanese mission and were brought howe
only to encounter fierce resistance by conservative statesmen and

=== Page 401 === (confidence 0.99)
一5-
financiers.It was no wonder that such opposition occurred.Callix
for the conquest of at least parts of the Dutch East Indies,of Malay
and Singapore and of the Philippines,the plan ment war with Great
Britain and Holland,in addition to the ultimate necessity of having
to fight the Soviet Union.The necessity of having to fight the United
States seems to have been ruled out.Germany's leaders,who were certain
that American Iolationism,Pacifism and abhorrence of war and internal
racial and national division would keep the U.S.A. out of war,seemed
to have converted the Japanese staff to their opinion.
With the promise in their hands,that Germany would engage
Great Britain so that Japan would find easy pickings,the Japanese
young officers group convinced many and "eliminated" those who could
not be convinced in the i famous February mutiny of 1936.
The dye was cast ! With the adoption of the German plan,
Japan decided for war as early as in the beginning of 1936.And this
decision was to be final,for the entire financial and economical re-
sources of the Empire had to be pooled and gambled ,if the plan was
to be carried through,a plan that called for the attainement of com-
plyte temporary self-sufficiency for as long a time as the war could
be expected to last.Such temporary or lasting self-sufficiency could
be reached in different ways :
1.Accumulation of vast stock piles.
2.Speed-up of production,build-up of industrial capacity and the
establishment of synthetic plants,wherever possible.
3.The occupation of smaller countries with sources of raw materials.
Thus the plan was conceived and we have to examine how and
in how far it has been carried through until the start ef hostilities

=== Page 402 === (confidence 0.98)
.6 -
THEPREPARATIONS.
1. OIL .
Oil is the life-blood of war.Without it,ships can not move,
aircraft is grounded,tanks are immobilized and aupplies do'nt reach
the front.Japan's domestie oil production never reached as much as
1o per cent of her consumption.
Japan's first step was to step up this home production.In
this she failed.All the typical Japanese energy could not achieve the
impossible,namely exploitation of her domestic oil wells beyond theiv
capacity.She reached a maximum production of about 3 million barrels
annually.
Under the circumstances it seemed reasonable to adopt the
German practice of synthetic production of gasoline.Germany had per-
fected the two known processes : synthesis from coal and from oil-
shale.Having satisfactory coal yields from Manchukuo as well as from
conquered Chinese mines,Japan was enabled to consider building synthe-
tic plants.Moreover,farstrethed oil-shale beds were discovered in Man
chukuo,so that Japan had the materials for both the synthetic proces-
ses.German technicians came to Japan ,together with German machinery.
A plan was adopted ,aiming at 16 million barrels annual production,
and when,in 1937,it had become obvious, that the plans for the increase
of the domestic oil production had failed,the plan for synthetic pro.
duction was revised upwards to 27 million barrels.In the beginning of
194o,eleven plants were already in operation with an estimated produc-
tion of 8t million barruls.At least eight more plants have been com-

pleted in thu first half of 1941.Thilo oxpondituros for tho building

through the blockado to Japan has impeded the construction of oil

in Japan under the supervision of German technicians and engèneers
who reached Japan in a steady stream by way of Russia until the out-
break of the Russo-German war.
Successful,as the synthetic production has shown to be,it
could not possibly be sufficiunt.Consequently,Jepan had to either
conquer a country where she could get oil fields,or she had to accu-
mulate a gigantic stock pile of oil.The former would have been less
expensive in the eyes of authoritarian powers : it would have only ccst
human lives,but little money.But - there was no small country that
could have provided oil,short of the main adversaries.That is,why
Japan saw herself forced to build up her stock.And indeed,she did.

1.Gas fuel oil is used for ship firing.The merchant marine was employed to the
utmost since 1937.That accounts for the relatively stable consumption.
2.few automobiles ,few aircraft(civilian) and very few roads.

As to the figures we have computed on the chart of Japanese

of course are not being given out by Japan.The German magazine "Oel

The fuel oil estimate is right,according to expert opinion.

fact that Japan has been accumulating tremendous stocks.-As far as

to virtually unanimous opinion.
As far as the near future is concerned, Japan can .oount on

neo production of about 6 million barrels,aggregating to about 26
million barrels as against an estimated annual consumption of 46 milli
barrels.At that rate,her stock pile would suffice for 5 years !

=== Page 407 === (confidence 0.98)
2. ALU.INUM.
One of the most important materials for modern warfare is
aluminum.For aircraft production it is indispensable,as it combines
hardness and durability with light weight.By far the ore with the higl
est alumina contents is bauxite,which usually contains in excess of
5o %ρf it.
For the production of aluminum,electricity is needed in hufe
amounts.Here,the newly built powerprojets in Manchukuo became useful.
It is well known that Japan has done her utmost to raise her aluminum
production capacity.Though no relieble figures are available,there is
little doubt that she has reached the capacity she believes necessary.
Not as easy,however,was the procurement of the ore.Japan her
self has no bauxite deposits to speak of.Therefore,Japan tried franti-
cally to induce her industry to make use of the low-grade domestic
aluminum rock and shell deposits in Korea.But since these materials
contain only 2o to 25 per cent of aluminum,the exploitation was extrecme-
ly expensive and Japanese industrialists did anything but like the
iden.Presentl ,however,Japan became aware of extensive and high-grade
bauxite deposits on the isle of Palao,n formerly German island that
was mandated to Japan by the League of Nations.Immediately, Japan star-
ted mining and as early as in 1938 she reported an output of 3o ooo
tons. year later she claimed an output of 1oo ooo tons,which is just
enough to cover Japan's consumption.Though the U.S.Bureau of Mines
doubts the announced figures,probably with reason,there is little
doubt that since that time production must have reached 1oo coo tons
if not more.

=== Page 408 === (confidence 0.98)
-2
But being well aware of the possibility that the communi-
cations between Palao and the mainland might be cut in time of war,
Japan took further steps.
She started bauxite mining in Hanoi.
She subsidized domestic mining of low-grade deposits.
She accumulated a sbck pile.
It seems to be impossible to get nny figures as to the do-
mestic production as well as to the Hanoi production.Only figures
relating to exports of forcign countries to Japan are partly avai-
lable.Japan bought bauxite from the Lutch East Indies,Malay and
Greece mainly.In 1939 alone,her imports amounted to 3oo ooo tons,
five times as much as her total consumption in that year.
Owing to the unavailability of figures,it is most difficul:
to compute a reliable indication as to Japan's stock piles.Two facts
are outstandingly clear : As long as Japan can keep open her lines or
communications with Palao,she is completely selfsufficient in alumi-
num.And even if and when these lines should be cut,indications are
that a stock pile of major proportions has been accumulated.If we
consider that the output of Palao alone was sufficient for Japanese
consumption,we see that the entire import,the whole production of
Hanoi and all of the domestic output must have been salted away.
Under such circumstances,we do well to assume that Japan
is not vulnerable in her bauxite supply.Only destruction of aluminum
plants could do the trick in this field.

=== Page 409 === (confidence 0.98)
3. Miscellaneous strategic raw materials.
TUNGSTEN is a metal that is considered irreplacable as a
hardening agent for armorpiercing ammunition and mechinetools.Japan
has no domestic production to spenk of.Nor has she hoarded any amount
of this metal,as far as is knowmThe reason lies in occupied China,
around Kwangsi : the largest deposits of Tungsten known on this earth
The output of these mines was more than half of the world production
even before the war.With these ines in her hands,Japan is unchallenge-
ably selfsufficient.

annually as against a consumption of 7ooo tons in peace time.As can

4. ATESIALL FOR IROL ANL STHEL PROI CCTION.
For the production of pig iron and steel,threo main ma-
torials are nccessary : scrap iron,primary iron and manganeso.
Japan's domestic manganese production - 68ooo tons in

fromMalay,the Philippines,China and Indo-China.,and,last not leasty

total production of the Yen-Block,even if we include the Philippines

scientists in the U.S.A. are reported to have found such a substitute
that even affords better results than manganese.Thus,although we must
conclude that Japan lacks sufficient manganese,there is no basis for
the assumption that this lack may shorten Japan's period of grace.
As far as the steel production is concerned,Japan has made
tremendous progress.From near to nothing in 193o,she reached a steel
production level of 8.1 million tons in 194o.And as Japan does hardly
any buildings construction with steel frames,nor automobile building:
this steel has been used mainly for military purposes,for shipbuilding
and the manufacture of tools of war.

In fact,it is a matter of doubt,if Japan even has as much as one mil-

be imported.Japan's main source for scrap - the basic material she has
built her warmachine with - came from the United States.Japan imported
as much as 2 million tons of scrap in one single year irom the USA.
A comparison between imports and steel production shows that the Japan
se government was right,when,in July 1941,it was claimed that only one
years supply of scrap was available.It can not very well have been
more than 1t million tons.- As soon,therefore,as scrap became difficult
to secure abroad,Japan started lowering the scrap iron quotient first
to 43,then to about 3o per cent.It is easy to see that Japan's scrap
stock will be used up by the middle of 1942.- Nevertheless,the situ-
ation is by no means precarious. Contrary to wide spread belief,scrar
iron is not necessary for steel production.It is only a means of redu-
cing costs,as it is cheaper than pig iron.Converters can as well be loa-
ded with pig iron only - if there is enough of it.

of November 19, 1941)
We sanctioned the matter concerning revision in
Ordinance pertainin to enforcement of the Machine
Tool Manufacturing Industry Law, and hereby cause the
same to be promulgated.
Imperlal Sin Manual
Imperial Seal
November 18, 1941.
Prime Minister, Minister of
Home Affaire and Minister of War,
E1j1 Tojo.

Imperial Ordinance No. 989
Revision shall be made in Ordinance pertaining
to the enforcement of the Machine Tool Manufacturing
Industry Law as follows:
Artiole 4 shall be eliminated.
2 of Article 5
The soale stipulated by the provisions of para-
graph 1 of Artieke 4 of the Machine Tool Manufacturing
Industry

=== Page 441 === (confidence 0.97)
Industry Taw shall be the equipment with 200 machine tools
as faoilities for one factory. When the machine tools
as specified by decree are manufactured, however, the
soale shall be equipment with 50 machine tools as
facilities for the manufacture.
The machine tools to be the facilities as mentioned
in the foregoing paragraph shall be the maohine tools
as mentioned in Article 1.
The expression "Machine Tool Manufacturing Industry
Companies as contained in paragraph 1 of Article 6
shall be revised as "Enterpriser of Manufacturing
Machine Tools" and the expression "10 or more machine
tools" as "5 or more machine tools".
The expression "Capital Fund of the Business Year"
as contained in paragraph 1 of 2 of Article 7 shall
be revised as "Capital Fund of the Business Year of
Juridical Person" and the expression "an amount of paid-
up share capital" as "an amount of paid-up share
capital or an amount of invested fund."
The expression "months of the Business Year con-
cerned" as contained in paragraph 1 of 4 of Article 7
shall be revised as "months of the business year con-
cerned for Juridieal Person and months of the Business
Term of its year for Natural Person and the same Article
shall be revised as "5 of Article 7".
4 of Artiole 7
2.
The amount of capital of the respective year of
natural person as mentioned in paragraph 2 of Artiole 7
of

=== Page 442 === (confidence 0.98)
of Maohine Teol Manufacturing Industry Law shall be
caloulated on the basis of the monthly average for
each month-end in the preceding year.
The fixed capital as mentioned in the preceding
paragraph shall be calculated on the basis of the value
of land, house, maohine, tools and other facilities
used directly for manufacturing machine tools.
In case where the amount of capital of natural
person is calculated, the calculation shall be made on
the basis of the provisions of preceding Article.
Article 8
When persons are about to obtain exemption from
the income tax or the tax of corporation in accordance
with the provisions of Article 7 of the Machine Tool
Manufacturing Industry Law, they shall file applioation
with the competent tax offices at the time of the pre-
sentation of report on the amount of incomes or capital
fund in accordance with the provisions of Article 34 of
the Income Tax Law or Article 18 of the Corporation
Tax Law reepectively.
In the case of the foregoing paragraph, a statement
of accounts distinguishing the incomes, accruing from
enterprises which are exempt from the income tax and
the corporation tax, from other incomes if there are
any such incomes or other incomes shall be added.
2 of Art1cle 8

the

the provisions of Article 15 or Article 16 of the
Business Profit Tax Law.
In the case of the foregoing paragraph, a state-
ment of accounts distinguishing the net profits
acoruing from enterprises which are exempt from the
Business Tax from other net profits if there are any
such net profits or other net profits, shall be added.
